=== sec-01 | Thinking like an attacker: threat models, assets and trust boundaries
--- teach
You have built programs that work when people use them as intended. Security is about what happens when someone does not. This course starts with the question every security engineer asks before writing a line of defence: **what are we protecting, from whom, and where can they reach us?** The answer is written down as a **threat model**, and this lesson teaches you to build one, including a small piece of it you can compute: the attack tree.

### A house, not a program

Picture securing a house. You do not start by buying the strongest lock. You start by asking what is inside that matters (a passport, a laptop, the people), who might want it (a passing burglar, not a spy agency), and how they could get in (the doors, the windows, the dog flap, the spare key under the mat). Then you spend your money where it helps most. A steel door is wasted if the kitchen window is left open.

Software is the same, with one difference: the burglar can try a million windows a second, and never gets tired.

### The goals

Security is always relative to goals. The classic three are called the **CIA triad**:

- **Confidentiality:** only the right people can read the data. A leaked password file breaks it.
- **Integrity:** data and code change only in allowed ways, by allowed people. A forged command to a spacecraft breaks it.
- **Availability:** the system works when it is needed. A flood of junk traffic that takes a ground station offline breaks it.

Two more are usually added. **Authenticity** means you can tell who sent something. **Non-repudiation** means the sender cannot later deny having sent it.

### The pieces of a threat model

A **threat model** is a written, precise answer to four questions:

1. **Assets:** what is worth protecting, and which goal matters for each. For a satellite: the command link (integrity above all), the telemetry (integrity, then availability), the encryption keys (confidentiality).
2. **Adversaries:** who attacks, what they want, and what they can do. This is the **[[adversary model|adversary]]**: their access (on the internet? inside the building? holding a stolen laptop?), their resources (an afternoon, or a government budget) and their knowledge.
3. **Entry points and trust boundaries:** where data or control passes from something you trust less into something you trust more.
4. **Assumptions:** what you take as given, such as "the operating system is not compromised" or "the hardware random number generator works". Every model has them. Writing them down is how you notice when one stops being true.

In everyday words: a threat model says *what can go wrong, who would make it go wrong, and what we are betting on*.

A **threat** is a way an adversary could harm an asset. A **vulnerability** is a flaw that makes a threat possible. An **attack** is someone actually using it. A **risk** is a threat weighed by how likely it is and how bad it would be.

### Trust boundaries

A **trust boundary** is a line in the system where the level of trust changes: on one side, data you control; on the other side, data that anyone could have written. Examples:

- the network socket of a web server (the internet on one side, your process on the other);
- the system call interface (a user program on one side, the kernel on the other);
- the radio link of a spacecraft (anyone with an antenna on one side, the flight computer on the other);
- a file uploaded by a user and then parsed by your program.

The rule that follows is the most useful sentence in this course: **every piece of data that crosses a trust boundary inward must be checked on the trusted side.** Checking on the untrusted side does not count, because the adversary controls that side. A check in the browser's JavaScript is a convenience for honest users; the server must check again.

The set of all entry points across the boundaries is the **[[attack surface|surface]]**. Every feature that accepts input adds to it. Removing a feature you do not need is often the cheapest defence there is.

A **dataflow diagram** makes boundaries visible. Draw each component as a box, each flow of data as an arrow, and draw the trust boundaries as dashed lines. Then every arrow that crosses a dashed line is a place to ask "what if this is hostile?".

Here is a tiny system written as data, with each component placed in a **zone** of trust:

```python
zone = {"browser": "internet", "web": "dmz", "db": "internal"}
flows = [("browser", "web"), ("web", "db"), ("db", "web")]
for src, dst in flows:
    if zone[src] != zone[dst]:
        print(src, "->", dst, "crosses", zone[src], "to", zone[dst])
```

It prints all three flows, because every arrow in this system crosses a boundary. The **DMZ**, short for demilitarised zone, is the network segment between the internet and the internal network, where public-facing servers sit.

### STRIDE: a checklist of what can go wrong

Staring at a diagram and "thinking of attacks" misses things. Microsoft's engineers made a checklist called **STRIDE**. Each letter is a kind of threat, and each is the opposite of one security property:

| letter | threat | the property it breaks | example |
|---|---|---|---|
| S | Spoofing | authenticity | sending commands while pretending to be the ground station |
| T | Tampering | integrity | changing a telemetry packet in transit |
| R | Repudiation | non-repudiation | an operator deletes the log of a command they sent |
| I | Information disclosure | confidentiality | an error message that prints a database password |
| D | Denial of service | availability | flooding the uplink with junk frames |
| E | Elevation of privilege | authorisation | a normal user gaining administrator rights |

You go through each element of the diagram and each letter, and ask "can this happen here?". It is systematic, so it is repeatable, and two engineers get similar answers.

### Attack trees

An **attack tree** breaks one adversary goal into the ways of reaching it. The root is the goal. Each inner node is either:

- an **OR** node: the goal is reached if **any one** child is reached;
- an **AND** node: the goal is reached only if **every** child is reached.

The leaves are single steps the adversary can take, each with a cost: money, time, or skill. Here is a small tree for "read the mission's private logs":

```text
OR  read the logs
├── AND  use an operator's account
│   ├── leaf  steal the password by phishing      cost 3
│   └── leaf  get past the second factor          cost 8
├── leaf  bribe an insider                         cost 20
└── leaf  copy an unencrypted backup tape          cost 6
```

The cheapest way in costs min(3 + 8, 20, 6) = 6: the backup tape. That is the useful kind of answer. The defender learns that encrypting the backups raises the adversary's cheapest cost from 6 to 11, while making the second factor stronger changes nothing at all until the backups are fixed.

In Python, a tree can be written as nested tuples: `("leaf", name, cost)`, or `("or", child, child, …)`, or `("and", child, child, …)`. The first item says what kind of node it is, and for an inner node the children are everything after it, `node[1:]`. Here is a function that walks such a tree and collects the leaf names, left to right:

```python
def leaves(node):
    if node[0] == "leaf":
        return [node[1]]
    names = []
    for child in node[1:]:
        names.extend(leaves(child))
    return names

tree = ("or", ("leaf", "guess pin", 5), ("and", ("leaf", "find card", 2), ("leaf", "watch typing", 4)))
print(leaves(tree))   # ['guess pin', 'find card', 'watch typing']
```

### Why the cheapest-cost rule is correct

Define an **attack** on a node as a set of leaves that, if all done, reaches that node. The claim: the cheapest attack on a node costs

- the leaf's own cost, for a leaf;
- the **minimum** of the children's cheapest costs, for an OR node;
- the **sum** of the children's cheapest costs, for an AND node.

The proof is by **[[structural induction|structural]]**: prove it for leaves, then prove it for a node assuming it holds for all of its children.

- **Leaf.** The only attack is doing that step, so its cost is the leaf's cost.
- **OR node.** An attack on it is exactly an attack on one of its children. So its cheapest attack is the cheapest among all the children's attacks, and by the assumption that is the minimum of the children's cheapest costs.
- **AND node.** An attack must reach every child. If the children's subtrees share no leaves, the attacks on different children are independent choices, so the cheapest is to pick each child's cheapest attack, and the costs add. By the assumption each of those is the child's cheapest cost, so the total is the sum.

Notice the phrase "share no leaves". If the same step appears under two children of an AND node, doing it once serves both, and the sum counts it twice. The rule then gives an upper bound, not the exact minimum. Trees in this course have distinct leaves.

A second quantity comes from the same recursion. The number of different **attack scenarios** (ways of choosing which branch to take at every OR node) is 1 for a leaf, the **sum** over the children for an OR node, and the **product** over the children for an AND node. The argument is the sum and product rules from Discrete Mathematics: an OR node picks one child's scenario, so the counts add; an AND node picks one scenario from every child independently, so they multiply.

### What it costs

Each node is visited once, and the work at a node, apart from its children, is proportional to its number of children. Every node is the child of at most one parent, so the children counted over the whole tree add up to n − 1 for a tree of n nodes. The total is **Θ(n)** time. The recursion goes as deep as the tree is tall, so the extra space is Θ(h) for height h.

Collecting the list of leaves in the cheapest attack costs more if you copy lists at every level: up to Θ(n·h) in the worst case, which is still small for any real tree.

### Risk, and deciding what to fix

No team can fix everything. A common rough measure is

**risk = likelihood × impact**,

each scored from 1 to 5. A threat that is likely and severe (5 × 4 = 20) is fixed before one that is unlikely and minor (2 × 1 = 2). The numbers are judgements, not measurements, so treat them as a way to sort a list, not as facts. The real value of scoring is that it forces the team to argue about each threat out loud.

The defender can respond to a threat in four ways: **mitigate** it (add a control), **eliminate** it (remove the feature), **transfer** it (insurance, or a supplier's contract), or **accept** it (write down why, and who decided).

### Principles that come from this

- **Kerckhoffs's principle:** a system must stay secure even if the adversary knows everything about it except the keys. Security that depends on keeping the design secret, called **security through obscurity**, fails the day the design leaks, and designs always leak.
- **Least privilege:** every part of a system gets only the access it needs. When it is broken into, the adversary gets only that little access. (Lesson 11 comes back to this.)
- **Defence in depth:** assume any single control can fail, and put several independent ones in a row.
- **Fail safe:** when something goes wrong, deny. A parser that meets something it does not understand should reject it, not guess.

### Where it is used

- Microsoft's Security Development Lifecycle requires a threat model with STRIDE for every product, and the same method is used across the industry.
- Space agencies and satellite operators threat-model their ground segments and radio links. The [[space segment|space]] is now a real target: in 2022 an attack on a satellite internet provider's ground network disabled thousands of user terminals across Europe.
- Car makers must threat-model their vehicles under the ISO/SAE 21434 standard, and medical device makers under their regulators' rules.
- Attack trees are used to compare defences: the one that raises the adversary's cheapest cost the most, per unit of money, goes first.

**Watch out:** the most common mistake is a trust boundary drawn in the wrong place. Teams treat "inside our network" as trusted, so an internal service accepts any request without checking who sent it. Then one phished laptop, one compromised supplier, or one misconfigured firewall rule puts the adversary inside, and everything opens at once. Draw a boundary wherever data comes from something you do not control, including your own other services, and check at every one. The modern name for this stance is [[zero trust|zerotrust]].

::: context adversary Who you are defending against
An adversary model names an attacker's goals, access and resources. It matters because the right defence depends on it. A script that tries common passwords is stopped by a lockout rule; a national intelligence agency with a zero-day exploit is not. Cryptographers make the model mathematical: "an attacker who runs in polynomial time and can ask for the encryption of messages of their choice". Engineers keep it in words, but just as precise. A model that names no adversary protects against nobody in particular.
:::

::: context surface Counting the ways in
The attack surface is every place an adversary can send input or reach code: open network ports, web forms, file parsers, USB ports, update mechanisms, debug interfaces left enabled. Size matters because each entry point is code that must be correct against hostile input. That is why security guides say to turn off services you do not use and remove old features. A spacecraft's attack surface includes its command receiver, and also any software upload path and any test port reachable before launch.
:::

::: context structural Induction on the shape of a tree
Ordinary induction proves a claim for 0, then for n + 1 from n. Structural induction does the same over the ways a tree is built: prove the claim for leaves, then prove it for a node assuming it already holds for each child. Every finite tree is built from leaves by finitely many such steps, so the claim holds for every tree. It is exactly the reasoning behind trusting a recursive function: if the base case is right and each call is right whenever its recursive calls are right, the whole function is right.
:::

::: context space Attacks on space systems
In February 2022, an attack on the ground network of the KA-SAT satellite service sent a malicious update to user modems, disabling tens of thousands of them, including remote monitoring for thousands of wind turbines in Germany. The satellite itself was never touched: the adversary went through the ground segment, where the trust boundary was weakest. Space security frameworks such as NASA's and the Aerospace Corporation's SPARTA catalogue now list threats to the ground, the link and the spacecraft separately.
:::

::: context zerotrust No trusted inside
Zero trust is the design rule that no request is trusted because of where it comes from on the network. Every request between services carries proof of who sent it and is checked against what that sender may do. Google moved to this model, which it calls BeyondCorp, after a 2009 intrusion showed that an attacker inside the perimeter could reach far too much. In threat-model terms, it draws a trust boundary around every service instead of one around the building.
:::
--- task
Attack trees in this task are nested tuples, as in the lesson:

- `("leaf", name, cost)`: one step, with a whole-number cost of 0 or more;
- `("or", child, child, …)`: reached if any one child is reached;
- `("and", child, child, …)`: reached only if every child is reached.

Every inner node has at least one child, and leaf names are all different.

Write two functions.

- `cheapest_attack(tree)` returns a tuple `(cost, steps)`: the cost of the cheapest attack on the root, and a list of the names of the leaves it uses, in left-to-right order in the tree. At an OR node, pick the child with the smallest cost; if several tie, pick the **leftmost** of them.
- `count_scenarios(tree)` returns how many different attack scenarios the tree has: 1 for a leaf, the sum over the children for an OR node, and the product over the children for an AND node.

For example, for `("or", ("leaf", "a", 4), ("and", ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 2)))`, `cheapest_attack` gives `(3, ["b", "c"])` and `count_scenarios` gives `2`.
--- starter
def cheapest_attack(tree):
    raise NotImplementedError


def count_scenarios(tree):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def cheapest_attack(tree):
    kind = tree[0]
    if kind == "leaf":
        return tree[2], [tree[1]]
    results = [cheapest_attack(child) for child in tree[1:]]
    if kind == "or":
        best = results[0]
        for result in results[1:]:
            if result[0] < best[0]:
                best = result
        return best[0], list(best[1])
    total = 0
    steps = []
    for cost, names in results:
        total += cost
        steps.extend(names)
    return total, steps


def count_scenarios(tree):
    kind = tree[0]
    if kind == "leaf":
        return 1
    counts = [count_scenarios(child) for child in tree[1:]]
    if kind == "or":
        return sum(counts)
    product = 1
    for c in counts:
        product *= c
    return product
--- hint
Both functions are recursive on the shape of the tree, like `leaves` in the lesson. Look at `tree[0]` to see the kind of node; for an inner node the children are `tree[1:]`.
--- hint
At an OR node keep the result with the smallest cost, replacing it only when a later child is strictly cheaper, so ties keep the leftmost. At an AND node add the costs and join the step lists in order.
--- hint
`count_scenarios` has the same shape: 1 for a leaf, `sum` of the children's counts at an OR node, and their product at an AND node.
--- check case | The example from the task
cheapest_attack(("or", ("leaf", "a", 4), ("and", ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 2))))
=> (3, ["b", "c"])
--- check case | A single leaf
(cheapest_attack(("leaf", "tailgate", 7)), count_scenarios(("leaf", "tailgate", 7)))
=> ((7, ["tailgate"]), 1)
--- check case | The lesson's backup-tape tree
cheapest_attack(("or", ("and", ("leaf", "phish", 3), ("leaf", "second factor", 8)), ("leaf", "bribe", 20), ("leaf", "backup tape", 6)))
=> (6, ["backup tape"])
--- check case | Ties go to the leftmost child
cheapest_attack(("or", ("leaf", "x", 5), ("and", ("leaf", "y", 2), ("leaf", "z", 3)), ("leaf", "w", 5)))
=> (5, ["x"])
?? Replace the best result only when a later one is strictly cheaper.
--- check case | Zero-cost steps
cheapest_attack(("and", ("leaf", "default password", 0), ("or", ("leaf", "open port", 0), ("leaf", "vpn", 9))))
=> (0, ["default password", "open port"])
--- check case | Nested ANDs keep left-to-right order
cheapest_attack(("and", ("and", ("leaf", "p", 1), ("leaf", "q", 2)), ("leaf", "r", 3), ("and", ("leaf", "s", 4))))
=> (10, ["p", "q", "r", "s"])
--- check case | An OR with one child
cheapest_attack(("or", ("leaf", "only", 12)))
=> (12, ["only"])
--- check case | Counting scenarios in the example
count_scenarios(("or", ("leaf", "a", 4), ("and", ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 2))))
=> 2
--- check case | AND multiplies, OR adds
count_scenarios(("and", ("or", ("leaf", "a", 1), ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 1)), ("or", ("leaf", "d", 1), ("leaf", "e", 1))))
=> 6
--- check test | A deep, wide tree: cost and count agree with a direct computation
(lambda t: cheapest_attack(t)[0] == 20 * 1 and count_scenarios(t) == 3 ** 20 and len(cheapest_attack(t)[1]) == 20)(("and",) + tuple(("or", ("leaf", "a%d" % i, 1), ("leaf", "b%d" % i, 2), ("leaf", "c%d" % i, 3)) for i in range(20)))
--- check test | Does not change the tree
(lambda t: (cheapest_attack(t), count_scenarios(t), t)[2] == ("or", ("leaf", "m", 2), ("leaf", "n", 1)))(("or", ("leaf", "m", 2), ("leaf", "n", 1)))

+++ practice | Flows that cross a trust boundary
--- task
A system is described by a dict `zone`, from component name to the name of its trust zone, and a list `flows` of `(src, dst)` pairs, each meaning "data flows from `src` to `dst`".

A component that is not in `zone` is something you do not control, so it belongs to the zone `"untrusted"`.

Write `crossings(zone, flows)` that returns a list of `(src, dst, src_zone, dst_zone)` tuples, one for each flow whose two ends are in different zones, in the order the flows are given. Flows inside one zone are left out.

For example, `crossings({"app": "phone", "api": "cloud"}, [("app", "api"), ("api", "api")])` gives `[("app", "api", "phone", "cloud")]`. With no flows, return `[]`.
--- starter
def crossings(zone, flows):
    return []
--- solution
def crossings(zone, flows):
    out = []
    for src, dst in flows:
        a = zone.get(src, "untrusted")
        b = zone.get(dst, "untrusted")
        if a != b:
            out.append((src, dst, a, b))
    return out
--- hint
Loop over the flows. Look up each end's zone with `dict.get`, giving `"untrusted"` as the default for a component that is not listed.
--- hint
Keep a flow only when the two zones differ, and append the four-item tuple in that order.
--- check case | The example from the task
crossings({"app": "phone", "api": "cloud"}, [("app", "api"), ("api", "api")])
=> [("app", "api", "phone", "cloud")]
--- check case | Unknown components are untrusted
crossings({"gs": "ground"}, [("antenna", "gs"), ("gs", "ops")])
=> [("antenna", "gs", "untrusted", "ground"), ("gs", "ops", "ground", "untrusted")]
--- check case | Two unknown components share the untrusted zone
crossings({}, [("a", "b")])
=> []
--- check case | No flows
crossings({"x": "z1"}, [])
=> []
--- check case | Order is kept, and repeated flows repeat
crossings({"fc": "flight", "radio": "comms"}, [("radio", "fc"), ("fc", "fc"), ("radio", "fc")])
=> [("radio", "fc", "comms", "flight"), ("radio", "fc", "comms", "flight")]

+++ practice | Sorting threats into STRIDE
--- task
Each STRIDE letter is the threat that breaks one property:

| property | letter |
|---|---|
| `"authenticity"` | `"S"` |
| `"integrity"` | `"T"` |
| `"non-repudiation"` | `"R"` |
| `"confidentiality"` | `"I"` |
| `"availability"` | `"D"` |
| `"authorisation"` | `"E"` |

Write `stride_summary(threats)`. `threats` is a list of `(component, property)` pairs, each meaning "this component has a threat against this property". Return a dict from each component to a string of its STRIDE letters, each letter at most once, in the order `S T R I D E`.

A property that is not in the table is a mistake in the model: return `None` for the whole summary if any property is unknown. An empty list gives `{}`.

For example, `stride_summary([("uplink", "integrity"), ("uplink", "authenticity"), ("db", "confidentiality")])` gives `{"uplink": "ST", "db": "I"}`.
--- starter
def stride_summary(threats):
    return {}
--- solution
LETTER = {
    "authenticity": "S",
    "integrity": "T",
    "non-repudiation": "R",
    "confidentiality": "I",
    "availability": "D",
    "authorisation": "E",
}


def stride_summary(threats):
    found = {}
    for component, prop in threats:
        if prop not in LETTER:
            return None
        found.setdefault(component, set()).add(LETTER[prop])
    return {c: "".join(ch for ch in "STRIDE" if ch in letters) for c, letters in found.items()}
--- hint
Keep a dict from each component to a set of letters, so a repeated threat adds nothing new.
--- hint
To put the letters in STRIDE order, walk the string `"STRIDE"` and keep the letters that are in the component's set.
--- check case | The example from the task
stride_summary([("uplink", "integrity"), ("uplink", "authenticity"), ("db", "confidentiality")])
=> {"uplink": "ST", "db": "I"}
--- check case | Letters come out in STRIDE order, once each
stride_summary([("gs", "authorisation"), ("gs", "availability"), ("gs", "authenticity"), ("gs", "availability")])
=> {"gs": "SDE"}
--- check case | An unknown property rejects the whole model
stride_summary([("gs", "integrity"), ("gs", "speed")])
=> None
--- check case | Empty list
stride_summary([])
=> {}
--- check case | All six on one component
stride_summary([("x", p) for p in ["availability", "non-repudiation", "confidentiality", "integrity", "authorisation", "authenticity"]])
=> {"x": "STRIDE"}

+++ practice | Ranking risks
--- task
A threat is a tuple `(name, likelihood, impact)`, where likelihood and impact are whole numbers from 1 to 5. Its risk is `likelihood * impact`.

A mitigation lowers a threat's likelihood. `mitigations` is a dict from a threat name to how much its likelihood drops; the likelihood never goes below 1.

Write `rank_risks(threats, mitigations)` that returns the names of the threats, highest risk first, after applying the mitigations. When two threats have the same risk, the one with the higher impact comes first (a rare disaster outranks a common nuisance). If those tie as well, sort by name, alphabetically.

For example, `rank_risks([("flood", 2, 5), ("spam", 5, 2), ("leak", 4, 4)], {"leak": 2})` gives `["flood", "spam", "leak"]`: the leak drops to 2 × 4 = 8, and flood and spam both have risk 10, with flood's impact higher.
--- starter
def rank_risks(threats, mitigations):
    return [t[0] for t in threats]
--- solution
def rank_risks(threats, mitigations):
    scored = []
    for name, likelihood, impact in threats:
        likelihood = max(1, likelihood - mitigations.get(name, 0))
        scored.append((-(likelihood * impact), -impact, name))
    scored.sort()
    return [name for _, _, name in scored]
--- hint
For each threat, compute the new likelihood with `max(1, likelihood - drop)`, where the drop is 0 for a threat with no mitigation.
--- hint
Sort by a key of three parts: minus the risk, minus the impact, then the name. Negating the numbers makes the highest come first while the names still sort A to Z.
--- check case | The example from the task
rank_risks([("flood", 2, 5), ("spam", 5, 2), ("leak", 4, 4)], {"leak": 2})
=> ["flood", "spam", "leak"]
--- check case | Likelihood never drops below 1
rank_risks([("a", 2, 5), ("b", 1, 4)], {"a": 10})
=> ["a", "b"]
--- check case | Full ties sort by name
rank_risks([("zeta", 3, 3), ("alpha", 3, 3), ("mid", 3, 3)], {})
=> ["alpha", "mid", "zeta"]
--- check case | No threats
rank_risks([], {"x": 1})
=> []
--- check case | A mitigation for an unknown threat changes nothing
rank_risks([("jam", 4, 3), ("spoof", 2, 5)], {"ghost": 3})
=> ["jam", "spoof"]

+++ practice | Can this attacker do it?
--- task
Attack trees use the lesson's tuples, with two changes that model a particular attacker:

- a leaf's cost may be `None`, meaning this attacker cannot do that step at all;
- an inner node may have **no** children. An `("or",)` with no children can never be reached. An `("and",)` with no children is reached for free: there is nothing to do.

Write `feasible(tree, budget)` that returns `True` if the attacker can reach the root with total cost at most `budget`, and `False` otherwise. Leaf names are all different.

For example, `feasible(("or", ("leaf", "zero-day", None), ("leaf", "phish", 4)), 4)` is `True`, and with a budget of 3 it is `False`.
--- starter
def feasible(tree, budget):
    return True
--- solution
def cheapest(node):
    kind = node[0]
    if kind == "leaf":
        return float("inf") if node[2] is None else node[2]
    costs = [cheapest(child) for child in node[1:]]
    if kind == "or":
        return min(costs, default=float("inf"))
    return sum(costs)


def feasible(tree, budget):
    return cheapest(tree) <= budget
--- hint
Compute the cheapest cost as in the lesson, but let "impossible" be `float("inf")`: infinity is larger than every budget, and adding anything to it stays infinity.
--- hint
The empty cases follow from the same rule if you pick the right starting values: the minimum of nothing is infinity (`min(costs, default=float("inf"))`), and the sum of nothing is 0.
--- check case | The example from the task
(feasible(("or", ("leaf", "zero-day", None), ("leaf", "phish", 4)), 4), feasible(("or", ("leaf", "zero-day", None), ("leaf", "phish", 4)), 3))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | One impossible step blocks an AND
feasible(("and", ("leaf", "badge", 1), ("leaf", "retina", None)), 1000)
=> False
--- check case | An empty OR can never be reached
feasible(("or",), 10 ** 9)
=> False
--- check case | An empty AND is free
(feasible(("and",), 0), feasible(("or", ("and",), ("leaf", "x", 5)), 0))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | A budget of exactly the cost is enough
feasible(("and", ("leaf", "a", 2), ("or", ("leaf", "b", 3), ("leaf", "c", None))), 5)
=> True
--- check case | Every leaf impossible
feasible(("or", ("leaf", "a", None), ("and", ("leaf", "b", None))), 10)
=> False

+++ practice | Debug: the asset the scan missed
--- task
A network is a dict `links` from each machine to the list of machines it can reach directly. `reachable_assets(links, entry, assets)` should return a sorted list of the assets (a set of machine names) that an adversary who starts on machine `entry` can reach by following links, **including `entry` itself** if it is an asset.

The breadth-first search below reaches the right machines, but it never reports the entry machine as an asset, even when it is one. For example, `reachable_assets({"web": ["db"]}, "web", {"web", "db"})` should be `["db", "web"]`, but it gives `["db"]`.

Find the bug and fix it. A machine with no entry in `links` has no outgoing links.
--- starter
def reachable_assets(links, entry, assets):
    seen = {entry}
    queue = [entry]
    found = []
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0)
        for nxt in links.get(node, []):
            if nxt not in seen:
                seen.add(nxt)
                queue.append(nxt)
                if nxt in assets:
                    found.append(nxt)
    return sorted(found)
--- solution
def reachable_assets(links, entry, assets):
    seen = {entry}
    queue = [entry]
    found = []
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0)
        if node in assets:
            found.append(node)
        for nxt in links.get(node, []):
            if nxt not in seen:
                seen.add(nxt)
                queue.append(nxt)
    return sorted(found)
--- hint
Which machines ever go through the `if nxt in assets` test? The entry machine is put in the queue at the start, but is it ever tested?
--- hint
Test each machine when it is taken out of the queue, instead of when it is added. Every reachable machine, including the entry, is taken out exactly once.
--- check case | The example from the task
reachable_assets({"web": ["db"]}, "web", {"web", "db"})
=> ["db", "web"]
--- check case | A cycle does not repeat assets
reachable_assets({"a": ["b"], "b": ["c", "a"], "c": ["a"]}, "a", {"a", "c"})
=> ["a", "c"]
--- check case | Unreachable assets are not reported
reachable_assets({"vpn": ["mail"], "hr": ["payroll"]}, "vpn", {"payroll", "mail"})
=> ["mail"]
--- check case | An entry with no links
reachable_assets({}, "kiosk", {"kiosk", "db"})
=> ["kiosk"]
--- check case | No assets at all
reachable_assets({"a": ["b"]}, "a", set())
=> []

+++ practice | The fewest steps to block
--- task
A defender can **block** leaves of an attack tree: a blocked step becomes impossible. The goal is to make the root impossible to reach by blocking as few leaves as possible.

Trees use the lesson's tuples; every inner node has at least one child and leaf names are all different. Costs do not matter here.

Write `min_block(tree)` that returns the smallest number of leaves whose blocking makes the root unreachable.

Think about each kind of node:

- to stop a leaf, block it: 1;
- to stop an OR node, you must stop **every** child;
- to stop an AND node, it is enough to stop **one** child, so pick the cheapest one to stop.

For example, in `("or", ("leaf", "a", 1), ("and", ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 1)))`, blocking `a` and `b` is enough, so the answer is 2.

Then write `argue()` that returns the letter of the right reason why the OR rule is correct:

- `"A"`: because blocking one child of an OR node leaves the adversary with no way through it;
- `"B"`: because the adversary picks any child that is still possible, so the root is stopped only when every child is stopped, and the children's leaves are distinct so their blocking costs add;
- `"C"`: because the smallest child is always the cheapest to block.
--- starter
def min_block(tree):
    return 1


def argue():
    return ""
--- solution
def min_block(tree):
    kind = tree[0]
    if kind == "leaf":
        return 1
    needs = [min_block(child) for child in tree[1:]]
    if kind == "or":
        return sum(needs)
    return min(needs)


def argue():
    return "B"
--- hint
This is the attack tree recursion with the roles swapped. What was a minimum for the attacker becomes a sum for the defender, and the other way round.
--- hint
An OR node is stopped only when all of its children are stopped: add their answers. An AND node is stopped when any one child is: take the smallest answer.
--- check case | The example from the task
min_block(("or", ("leaf", "a", 1), ("and", ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 1))))
=> 2
--- check case | A single leaf
min_block(("leaf", "x", 3))
=> 1
--- check case | A wide OR must all be blocked
min_block(("or", ("leaf", "a", 1), ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 1), ("leaf", "d", 1)))
=> 4
--- check case | An AND picks the easiest child to stop
min_block(("and", ("or", ("leaf", "a", 1), ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 1)), ("or", ("leaf", "d", 1), ("leaf", "e", 1))))
=> 2
--- check case | Deeper nesting
min_block(("or", ("and", ("leaf", "a", 1), ("or", ("leaf", "b", 1), ("leaf", "c", 1))), ("and", ("or", ("leaf", "d", 1), ("leaf", "e", 1), ("leaf", "f", 1)), ("or", ("leaf", "g", 1), ("leaf", "h", 1)))))
=> 3
--- check case | The reason for the OR rule
argue()
=> "B"

=== sec-02 | Memory-safety bugs: buffer overflows, use-after-free and format strings
--- teach
Last lesson you learned to ask where hostile data crosses into your program. This lesson looks at the oldest and still one of the most damaging things that hostile data can do once it is inside: break the rules of memory. These are **memory-safety bugs**, and in languages like C and C++ they turn a wrong-sized input into control of the machine.

You met the raw material in Systems Programming: a `char` array with a terminating zero, the stack with its frames and saved return addresses, the heap with `malloc` and `free`. Here you will see what an attacker does with each mistake. The bugs are shown in C++, because that is where they live. You will build and grade a **model** of the machine in Python, because Python cannot corrupt its own memory, which is exactly why it is a safe place to study the corruption.

### The buffer overflow

A **buffer overflow** (also **buffer overrun**) is writing past the end of an array. In C the classic cause is `strcpy`, which copies a string with no idea how big the destination is:

```cpp fragment
void greet(const char* name) {
    char buffer[8];
    strcpy(buffer, name);       // copies until name's NUL, however long it is
    printf("Hi %s\n", buffer);
}
```

If `name` is `"Ald"`, all is well. If `name` is 40 bytes long, `strcpy` writes 40 bytes into an 8-byte array. The extra 32 bytes go wherever they land in memory.

Where they land is the point. Recall the stack frame from Systems Programming: the local array sits in the frame, and higher up the same frame is the **saved return address**, the place the function jumps back to when it finishes. A `char` array that overflows writes towards higher addresses, straight over the saved return address.

So an attacker who controls `name` can choose the bytes that land on the return address. When `greet` returns, the CPU jumps to an address the attacker picked. This is called **[[smashing the stack|smash]]**, and in the 1990s and 2000s it was behind a huge share of internet worms.

### A model of the frame

You cannot make Python overflow a real buffer, so model the frame as a `bytearray`, a mutable block of bytes. Lay it out as the machine does: the buffer at the bottom, then the saved return address just above it. In ORBIT's 32-bit world a pointer is 4 bytes, so store the return address as a 4-byte little-endian integer. Reading it back:

```python
mem = bytearray(16)
mem[8:12] = (0xCAFE).to_bytes(4, "little")   # a saved return address at offset 8
print(int.from_bytes(mem[8:12], "little"))   # 51966, which is 0xCAFE
```

`to_bytes(4, "little")` writes the number as four bytes, least significant first, and `int.from_bytes(..., "little")` reads them back. A copy that runs past offset 8 changes those bytes, and so changes the address the model "returns" to.

Here is what a single byte written past a 3-byte buffer does to a value stored just above it:

```python
region = bytearray([0, 0, 0, 9, 9])   # buffer is region[0:3]; region[3:] is "next"
region[3] = 65                          # one byte too far: writes 'A' (65) over the 9
print(list(region))                     # [0, 0, 0, 65, 9]
```

The write succeeded, nothing crashed, and a value the function never meant to touch is now 65. That silence is what makes overflows dangerous: a quick test looks fine.

### The off-by-one from the terminator

C strings carry a hidden extra byte: the terminating `\0`. A copy of an n-character string writes **n + 1** bytes. So a string of exactly 8 characters does not fit in an 8-byte buffer: the 8 characters fill it, and the terminating zero lands one byte past the end. That single zero byte is enough to corrupt the lowest byte of whatever sits above the buffer. Off-by-one overflows of exactly one byte have been turned into full exploits.

### Use-after-free

The second bug is about time, not space. A **use-after-free** is using memory through a pointer after that memory has been freed.

```cpp fragment
int* p = new int(42);
delete p;            // the memory is handed back to the allocator
int* q = new int(7); // the allocator may hand the same memory back out
*p = 99;             // writes through the old pointer into q's object
```

After `delete p`, the pointer `p` still holds the old address, but the object is gone: `p` is a **dangling pointer** (Systems Programming's word). The allocator is free to give that exact memory to the next `new`. Now `p` and `q` may point at the same bytes, and a write through `p` silently changes `q`'s object. If one of them is a "you are logged in" flag or a pointer to a function, the attacker who controls the timing controls the program.

You can model an allocator's reuse in Python with a pool of slots. Freeing a slot marks it available but does not erase it, and the next allocation reuses it. A handle kept after the free still reads the slot, now holding someone else's data. You will build this in the practice.

### The double free

A cousin of the use-after-free is the **double free**: calling `free` twice on the same pointer. It corrupts the allocator's own bookkeeping (the free list you built in Systems Programming), and that corruption has been turned into attacker-controlled writes. The rule that avoids both is simple to say and easy to forget: after `free`, set the pointer to null, and never free a null-checked pointer twice.

### Format strings

The third bug looks harmless. C's `printf` decides what to read from its arguments by scanning the **format string** for `%` specifiers. Passing user input *as* the format string lets the user write the specifiers:

```cpp fragment
printf(user_input);              // wrong: user_input is the format string
printf("%s", user_input);        // right: user_input is data
```

If `user_input` is `"%x %x %x"`, `printf` dutifully reads three values from where the arguments would be, on the stack, and prints them, even though no arguments were passed. That leaks memory contents (an information-disclosure bug, the I in STRIDE). Worse, the specifier `%n` *writes* the number of characters printed so far to an address taken from the arguments, giving an attacker a way to write chosen values to chosen places.

You will model the leak in Python: a formatter that, when the format asks for more values than were supplied, keeps reading from a list standing in for the rest of the stack.

### Why these are specific to unsafe languages

Python, Java, Go and Rust are **[[memory-safe|memory-safe]]**: an index past the end of a list raises an error, memory is freed only when nothing points to it, and there is no way to treat user data as a format string by accident. C and C++ trade that safety for control and speed, and hand the programmer the duty of never making these mistakes. The whole rest of this course's memory chapter, the next lesson especially, is about the defences, the [[stack canary|canary-preview]] first among them, that were added because humans cannot keep that duty perfectly.

### Where it is used, and where it bites

- **Morris worm (1988)** and **Code Red (2001)** spread through buffer overflows. **Heartbleed (2014)** was an over-**read** in OpenSSL that leaked private keys. Roughly two thirds of the serious vulnerabilities that Microsoft and Google report each year are memory-safety bugs.
- Space and aviation software is mostly C and C++, so these bugs are exactly what flight-software coding rules (lesson 12) are written to prevent: fixed-size buffers, no dynamic memory after start-up, every copy bounded.
- The response across the industry is partly to move to memory-safe languages: the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency now urges new code be written in them.

**Watch out:** the reason these bugs survive is that a program with one usually *works* on ordinary input. The overflow only overwrites memory that ordinary input is too short to reach; the use-after-free only misbehaves when the freed memory happens to be reused; the format string only leaks when someone puts a `%` in their name. Passing the tests proves nothing about safety. Only checking sizes and lifetimes against **hostile** input does, which is why the checks in this lesson feed your code inputs an attacker would choose.

::: context smash Smashing the stack
"Smashing the stack" is overwriting a stack frame's saved return address by overflowing a local array, so that the function returns to code the attacker chose. The name comes from the 1996 article "Smashing the Stack for Fun and Profit" by Aleph One in the hacker magazine Phrack, which explained the technique in careful detail and turned it from folklore into a standard tool. Every defence in the next lesson, the stack canary above all, exists to make this one move harder. In a 32-bit model like ORBIT's the return address is 4 bytes, so four attacker-chosen bytes landing on it are enough.
:::

::: context memory-safe What "memory-safe" means
A language is memory-safe when its rules make out-of-bounds access, use-after-free and reading uninitialised memory impossible in ordinary code: the runtime checks indices, tracks what is still referenced, and frees only what is dead. Python does all three, which is why a model of an overflow written in Python cannot itself overflow anything. Rust is notable for giving the same guarantees with no runtime garbage collector, by checking ownership and lifetimes when it compiles. Memory safety does not make a program secure on its own, it removes one large family of bugs, and it is the family behind most remote takeovers.
:::

::: context canary-preview A taste of the next defence
The next lesson's main defence against stack smashing is the stack canary: a secret random value the compiler places between a local array and the saved return address, checked just before the function returns. An overflow big enough to reach the return address must first cross the canary and change it, so the check fails and the program stops instead of returning to attacker code. The name is from the caged canaries miners carried: the bird reacts to danger first. You will build and grade a canary model next.
:::
--- task
Model the bottom of a stack frame as a `bytearray`: a buffer starting at index 0, and a 4-byte saved return address stored little-endian just above it. Write three functions.

- `unsafe_copy(mem, dst, src)`: copy the bytes of `src` (a `bytes` object) into `mem` starting at index `dst`, one byte at a time, then write a terminating `0` byte at the position just after them. Do **no** bounds checking, exactly as C's `strcpy` does. Return `None`. (Writing past the end of `mem` will raise `IndexError` on its own; that models a crash, and the checks stay inside `mem`.)
- `safe_copy(mem, dst, cap, src)`: copy `src` into `mem` at `dst`, but never write at or beyond index `dst + cap`. Copy at most `cap - 1` bytes so there is always room for the terminating `0`, which you write at the end of what you copied. If `cap` is 0, copy nothing and write nothing. Return the number of data bytes actually copied (not counting the terminator).
- `saved_return(mem, off)`: return the 4-byte little-endian integer stored at `mem[off:off+4]`.

For example, with a buffer of capacity 8 and the saved return address at offset 8, copying a 12-byte `src` with `unsafe_copy` overwrites the return address, while `safe_copy` with the same `src` copies only 7 bytes and leaves it untouched.
--- starter
def unsafe_copy(mem, dst, src):
    raise NotImplementedError


def safe_copy(mem, dst, cap, src):
    raise NotImplementedError


def saved_return(mem, off):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def unsafe_copy(mem, dst, src):
    i = 0
    while i < len(src):
        mem[dst + i] = src[i]
        i += 1
    mem[dst + len(src)] = 0
    return None


def safe_copy(mem, dst, cap, src):
    if cap <= 0:
        return 0
    n = len(src)
    if n > cap - 1:
        n = cap - 1
    k = 0
    while k < n:
        mem[dst + k] = src[k]
        k += 1
    mem[dst + n] = 0
    return n


def saved_return(mem, off):
    return int.from_bytes(mem[off:off + 4], "little")
--- hint
`unsafe_copy` is a plain loop over `src` writing `mem[dst + i] = src[i]`, then one more write of `0` at index `dst + len(src)`. There is deliberately no length check.
--- hint
`safe_copy` first limits the number of bytes to `cap - 1` (so `n = min(len(src), cap - 1)`), copies that many, writes the `0` terminator at `dst + n`, and returns `n`. Handle `cap == 0` by copying nothing.
--- hint
`saved_return` is one line: `int.from_bytes(mem[off:off+4], "little")`, the reverse of writing four little-endian bytes.
--- check case | A short copy leaves the saved return address intact
(lambda m: (unsafe_copy(m, 0, b"OK"), saved_return(m, 8))[1])(bytearray(b"\x00" * 8 + b"\x01\x02\x04\x08" + b"\x00" * 12))
=> 134480385
--- check case | Overflowing 12 bytes overwrites the whole return address with attacker bytes
(lambda m: (unsafe_copy(m, 0, b"A" * 12), saved_return(m, 8))[1])(bytearray(b"\x00" * 8 + b"\x01\x02\x04\x08" + b"\x00" * 12))
=> 1094795585
--- check case | Exactly 8 characters: the terminator alone corrupts the low byte
(lambda m: (unsafe_copy(m, 0, b"AAAAAAAA"), saved_return(m, 8))[1])(bytearray(b"\x00" * 8 + b"\x01\x02\x04\x08" + b"\x00" * 12))
=> 134480384
?? An 8-character string writes 8 bytes, then a 9th byte (the 0) at index 8, the first byte of the return address.
--- check case | safe_copy truncates a too-long source and protects the return address
(lambda m: (safe_copy(m, 0, 8, b"A" * 12), saved_return(m, 8), bytes(m[0:8]))) (bytearray(b"\x00" * 8 + b"\x01\x02\x04\x08" + b"\x00" * 12))
=> (7, 134480385, b'AAAAAAA\x00')
--- check case | safe_copy of a source that fits copies all of it and terminates
(lambda m: (safe_copy(m, 0, 8, b"ALDRIN"), bytes(m[0:8])))(bytearray(16))
=> (6, b'ALDRIN\x00\x00')
--- check case | safe_copy with capacity 1 has room only for the terminator
(lambda m: (safe_copy(m, 0, 1, b"hello"), bytes(m[0:4])))(bytearray(8))
=> (0, b'\x00\x00\x00\x00')
--- check case | safe_copy with capacity 0 writes nothing
(lambda m: (safe_copy(m, 2, 0, b"hi"), bytes(m)))(bytearray(4))
=> (0, b'\x00\x00\x00\x00')
--- check case | saved_return reads little-endian
saved_return(bytearray(b"\x00\x00\xff\x00\x00\x01\x00\x00"), 3)
=> 65536
--- check test | safe_copy never writes at or beyond dst + cap, for every source length
all((lambda m: (safe_copy(m, 4, 8, b"Z" * n), m[12] == 0 and all(b == 0 for b in m[12:])))(bytearray(20)) for n in range(0, 20))
?? With cap 8 at dst 4, nothing at index 12 or above may ever change, however long the source.
--- check test | safe_copy return value is the min of the source length and cap minus one
all(safe_copy(bytearray(40), 0, cap, b"x" * n) == min(n, cap - 1) for cap in range(1, 12) for n in range(0, 15))

+++ practice | Build the frame
--- task
Write `build_frame(cap, ret)` that returns a `bytearray` modelling a stack frame: a buffer of `cap` zero bytes at the bottom, then the saved return address `ret` stored as 4 little-endian bytes just above it. The total length is `cap + 4`.

Also write `read_ret(frame, cap)` that returns the saved return address, the 4-byte little-endian integer starting at index `cap`.

For example, `build_frame(4, 0xABCD)` is `bytearray(b'\x00\x00\x00\x00\xcd\xab\x00\x00')`, and `read_ret(build_frame(4, 0xABCD), 4)` is `0xABCD`, which is `43981`.
--- starter
def build_frame(cap, ret):
    return bytearray(cap)


def read_ret(frame, cap):
    return 0
--- solution
def build_frame(cap, ret):
    return bytearray(cap) + bytearray(ret.to_bytes(4, "little"))


def read_ret(frame, cap):
    return int.from_bytes(frame[cap:cap + 4], "little")
--- hint
`bytearray(cap)` is `cap` zero bytes. Append the four bytes of `ret` with `ret.to_bytes(4, "little")`.
--- hint
`read_ret` is the reverse: `int.from_bytes(frame[cap:cap+4], "little")`.
--- check case | The example from the task
build_frame(4, 0xABCD)
=> bytearray(b'\x00\x00\x00\x00\xcd\xab\x00\x00')
--- check case | Reading the return address back
read_ret(build_frame(4, 0xABCD), 4)
=> 43981
--- check case | A zero-capacity buffer is just the return address
(build_frame(0, 1), read_ret(build_frame(0, 1), 0))
=> (bytearray(b'\x01\x00\x00\x00'), 1)
--- check case | Total length is cap + 4
[len(build_frame(c, 0)) for c in [0, 3, 8, 16]]
=> [4, 7, 12, 20]
--- check case | Round-trips a large address
read_ret(build_frame(8, 0xDEADBEEF), 8)
=> 3735928559

+++ practice | Model a use-after-free
--- task
A tiny allocator keeps a `pool`: a dict `{"slots": [...], "free": [...]}`, where `slots` is a list of stored values and `free` is a list of indexes of slots that have been freed, most recently freed last.

Write three functions:

- `allocate(pool, value)`: if any slot is free, reuse the **most recently freed** one (the last index in `free`, removed from it); otherwise append a new slot. Store `value` in that slot and return its index (the "pointer").
- `deallocate(pool, ptr)`: mark slot `ptr` free by appending it to `free`. Do **not** erase the stored value. (That missing erase is the bug that makes the leak possible.)
- `deref(pool, ptr)`: return the value stored in slot `ptr`, whether or not it is free. (An unchecked read, exactly the vulnerability.)

The point: after you free a slot and allocate again, the old pointer reads the new occupant's value. Start each pool as `{"slots": [], "free": []}`.

For example: allocate a slot holding `"session-A"`, free it, allocate one holding `"session-B"`; dereferencing the first pointer now returns `"session-B"`, because the slot was reused.
--- starter
def allocate(pool, value):
    return 0


def deallocate(pool, ptr):
    pass


def deref(pool, ptr):
    return None
--- solution
def allocate(pool, value):
    if pool["free"]:
        i = pool["free"].pop()
        pool["slots"][i] = value
        return i
    pool["slots"].append(value)
    return len(pool["slots"]) - 1


def deallocate(pool, ptr):
    pool["free"].append(ptr)


def deref(pool, ptr):
    return pool["slots"][ptr]
--- hint
`allocate` checks `pool["free"]`. If it is non-empty, `pop()` (which removes the last item) gives the most recently freed index to reuse; otherwise append and return the new last index.
--- hint
`deallocate` only records the index in `pool["free"]`; it must not clear `pool["slots"][ptr]`. `deref` reads `pool["slots"][ptr]` with no check.
--- check case | A dangling pointer reads the new occupant
(lambda pool: (lambda a: (deallocate(pool, a), allocate(pool, "session-B"), deref(pool, a))[2])(allocate(pool, "session-A")))({"slots": [], "free": []})
=> "session-B"
--- check case | Fresh allocations get new slots
(lambda pool: [allocate(pool, "x"), allocate(pool, "y"), allocate(pool, "z")])({"slots": [], "free": []})
=> [0, 1, 2]
--- check case | Reuse is most-recently-freed first
(lambda pool: (allocate(pool, "a"), allocate(pool, "b"), allocate(pool, "c"), deallocate(pool, 0), deallocate(pool, 2), [allocate(pool, "d"), allocate(pool, "e")])[-1])({"slots": [], "free": []})
=> [2, 0]
--- check case | deref works before any free
(lambda pool: (allocate(pool, 42), deref(pool, 0))[1])({"slots": [], "free": []})
=> 42
--- check test | The freed value survives until reused
(lambda pool: (lambda p: (deallocate(pool, p), deref(pool, p) == "secret")[1])(allocate(pool, "secret")))({"slots": [], "free": []})

+++ practice | Would it overflow?
--- task
Before making a copy, you often want to know whether it is safe. A C-style copy of a source string writes one byte per character **plus** a terminating zero, so it needs `len(src) + 1` bytes.

Write `would_overflow(cap, src)` that returns `True` if copying `src` (a `bytes` object) into a buffer of capacity `cap` with a C-style copy would write past the buffer, and `False` otherwise. Copying needs `len(src) + 1` bytes; it overflows when that exceeds `cap`.

Then write `count_dangerous(cap, inputs)` that returns how many of the byte strings in the list `inputs` would overflow a buffer of capacity `cap`. These inputs are data crossing a trust boundary, so each must be checked.

For example, `would_overflow(8, b"AAAAAAAA")` is `True` (8 characters need 9 bytes), `would_overflow(8, b"AAAAAAA")` is `False` (7 need 8), and `count_dangerous(4, [b"ok", b"toolong", b"abc"])` is `1`.
--- starter
def would_overflow(cap, src):
    return False


def count_dangerous(cap, inputs):
    return 0
--- solution
def would_overflow(cap, src):
    return len(src) + 1 > cap


def count_dangerous(cap, inputs):
    return sum(1 for src in inputs if would_overflow(cap, src))
--- hint
The copy needs `len(src) + 1` bytes. It overflows exactly when that is greater than `cap`.
--- hint
`count_dangerous` counts the inputs for which `would_overflow(cap, src)` is `True`.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(would_overflow(8, b"AAAAAAAA"), would_overflow(8, b"AAAAAAA"), count_dangerous(4, [b"ok", b"toolong", b"abc"]))
=> (True, False, 1)
--- check case | The empty string still needs its terminator
(would_overflow(0, b""), would_overflow(1, b""))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | A capacity of exactly len + 1 fits
would_overflow(5, b"abcd")
=> False
--- check case | Nothing dangerous
count_dangerous(100, [b"a", b"bb", b"ccc"])
=> 0
--- check case | Everything dangerous, including ties at the boundary
count_dangerous(3, [b"abc", b"abcd", b"xy"])
=> 2

+++ practice | Debug: the bounds check that lets one byte through
--- task
`checked_append(mem, length, cap, byte)` should append one `byte` to a buffer that currently holds `length` bytes and has capacity `cap`, but only if there is room. On success it writes the byte at index `length` and returns `length + 1`, the new length. If the buffer is already full, it must change nothing and return `length`.

The version below has an off-by-one bug: it lets a write happen when the buffer is already full, writing one byte past the end. For a buffer with `cap == 4` that already holds 4 bytes, calling it should return `4` and change nothing, but it writes at index 4 and returns 5.

Find and fix the bug. It is one character.
--- starter
def checked_append(mem, length, cap, byte):
    if length <= cap:
        mem[length] = byte
        return length + 1
    return length
--- solution
def checked_append(mem, length, cap, byte):
    if length < cap:
        mem[length] = byte
        return length + 1
    return length
--- hint
When `length == cap` the buffer is already full: indexes `0` to `cap - 1` are all used. Should the write happen then?
--- hint
The valid indexes are `0` to `cap - 1`, so a write is allowed only when `length < cap`, not `length <= cap`.
--- check case | A full buffer is left alone
(lambda m: (checked_append(m, 4, 4, 65), bytes(m)))(bytearray(6))
=> (4, b'\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00')
--- check case | Appending into a buffer with room
(lambda m: (checked_append(m, 2, 4, 65), bytes(m[0:4])))(bytearray(6))
=> (3, b'\x00\x00A\x00')
--- check case | Filling the last slot is allowed
(lambda m: (checked_append(m, 3, 4, 90), bytes(m[0:4])))(bytearray(6))
=> (4, b'\x00\x00\x00Z')
--- check case | Capacity zero never writes
(lambda m: (checked_append(m, 0, 0, 1), bytes(m)))(bytearray(2))
=> (0, b'\x00\x00')
--- check test | Filling then overfilling stops exactly at cap
(lambda m: __import__("functools").reduce(lambda L, _: checked_append(m, L, 4, 7), range(6), 0) == 4 and bytes(m) == b'\x07\x07\x07\x07')(bytearray(4))

+++ practice | Model a format-string leak
--- task
A stripped-down `printf`. The format string `fmt` is made of ordinary characters and the specifier `%d`, which prints the next integer argument. The intended arguments are in the list `args`. But if the format contains more `%d` specifiers than there are arguments, a real `printf` keeps reading from the stack; model that "rest of the stack" as the list `stack`.

Write `vuln_printf(fmt, args, stack)` that returns a tuple `(text, leaked)`:

- `text` is the result of replacing each `%d` in order with the string of the next value, taking values first from `args`, then, once `args` runs out, from `stack`. Ordinary characters are copied as they are. A `%%` is a literal `%` and consumes no value. You may assume the format is valid and never asks for more values than `args` and `stack` together provide.
- `leaked` is how many values were taken from `stack` (that is, how many the attacker was not supposed to see).

For example, `vuln_printf("id=%d", [7], [999])` is `("id=7", 0)`, but `vuln_printf("%d %d", [7], [999])` is `("7 999", 1)`: the second `%d` had no argument, so it leaked a stack value.
--- starter
def vuln_printf(fmt, args, stack):
    return (fmt, 0)
--- solution
def vuln_printf(fmt, args, stack):
    supply = list(args) + list(stack)
    used = 0
    leaked = 0
    out = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(fmt):
        if fmt[i] == "%" and i + 1 < len(fmt):
            spec = fmt[i + 1]
            if spec == "%":
                out.append("%")
            elif spec == "d":
                out.append(str(supply[used]))
                if used >= len(args):
                    leaked += 1
                used += 1
            i += 2
        else:
            out.append(fmt[i])
            i += 1
    return ("".join(out), leaked)
--- hint
Join `args` and `stack` into one supply list. Walk the format; when you see `%d`, take the next value from the supply and remember how many you have taken.
--- hint
A value is a leak when its position in the supply is at or beyond `len(args)`. Handle `%%` as a literal percent that takes no value.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(vuln_printf("id=%d", [7], [999]), vuln_printf("%d %d", [7], [999]))
=> (("id=7", 0), ("7 999", 1))
--- check case | Enough arguments: nothing leaks
vuln_printf("%d-%d-%d", [1, 2, 3], [111, 222])
=> ("1-2-3", 0)
--- check case | Every specifier past the arguments leaks
vuln_printf("%d %d %d %d", [], [10, 20, 30, 40])
=> ("10 20 30 40", 4)
--- check case | A literal percent consumes no value
vuln_printf("100%% done: %d", [5], [777])
=> ("100% done: 5", 0)
--- check case | No specifiers at all
vuln_printf("all clear", [1], [2, 3])
=> ("all clear", 0)
--- check test | Leaked count equals specifiers beyond the argument count
vuln_printf("%d " * 6, [1, 2], list(range(100, 110)))[1] == 4

=== sec-03 | Mitigations: stack canaries, ASLR, NX and safe APIs
--- teach
Last lesson you smashed a modelled stack frame and overwrote its saved return address. Real systems have not stood still: over twenty years, compilers and operating systems added defences that make that attack far harder. This lesson builds models of the three most important ones, and names the safe programming interfaces that stop the bug at the source. None of them is perfect alone; together they are **defence in depth** (lesson 1) against memory corruption.

Keep the honest framing in mind. The bug is still there. A mitigation does not fix a buffer overflow; it tries to make an overflow **crash** instead of **execute**. Turning a takeover into a crash is a huge win (a crash is a denial of service, not a compromise), but the real fix is not to overflow at all.

### The stack canary

The **stack canary** (also **stack cookie**) is a secret random value the compiler places between a function's local buffers and its saved return address. Before the function returns, it checks the canary. An overflow large enough to reach the return address must first write over the canary, changing it, so the check fails and the program aborts instead of returning to attacker-chosen code.

The layout, from the last lesson's frame with one thing added:

```text
low addresses                                   high addresses
[ buffer .......... ][ canary (4 bytes) ][ saved return address ]
   overflow writes this way  ->->->->->->->->->->
```

Model the frame as a `bytearray` again, with the canary sitting between the buffer and the return address. The check is an equality test:

```python
frame = bytearray(4) + (0x1234).to_bytes(4, "little") + bytearray(4)
stored = int.from_bytes(frame[4:8], "little")
print(stored == 0x1234)     # True: nothing has overflowed yet
frame[5] = 0xFF             # an overflow reaches into the canary
print(int.from_bytes(frame[4:8], "little") == 0x1234)   # False: detected
```

When the check fails, GCC-compiled programs print `*** stack smashing detected ***` and abort. That message is the canary doing its job.

Three details make a real canary strong:

- It is **random**, chosen fresh each time the program starts, so the attacker cannot know it in advance. Kerckhoffs's principle (lesson 1) again: the secret is the value, not the design.
- It often contains a **[[zero byte|nullcanary]]**. String functions stop copying at the first zero, so an overflow built from a C string cannot write a zero in the middle to forge a canary that has one.
- It is checked **just before the return**, so any overflow that happened during the function is caught before the corrupted return address is used.

The canary's cost is small: a few instructions per protected function, and it does not protect against every overwrite, only ones that cross it on the way to the return address. An overflow that corrupts another local variable, or that writes to an arbitrary address, sails past.

### ASLR: hide the targets

Even if an attacker overwrites the return address, they need a useful address to write. **Address space layout randomization (ASLR)** makes that hard by placing the stack, heap, libraries and program at **random** addresses each time the program runs. The attacker no longer knows where their injected code or a useful library function sits.

Model the randomness with a seeded generator so it is reproducible:

```python
import random
rng = random.Random(20240501)
base = 0x400000 + rng.randrange(2 ** 8) * 0x1000
print(hex(base))    # a base that is different for each seed
```

The strength of ASLR is measured in **[[bits of entropy|aslr-entropy]]**: if the base can fall in any of 2^k places, the attacker guessing blind is right with probability 1/2^k. With 8 bits that is 1 in 256, weak enough to brute-force; 64-bit systems give far more. This is the discrete-probability reasoning from Discrete Mathematics: a uniform choice over N places gives each a probability of 1/N.

ASLR's weakness is an **information leak**. If any bug tells the attacker one real address (a format-string leak from last lesson, say), they can compute the others, because the pieces move together. Defence in depth: ASLR and no info leaks, not ASLR alone.

### NX: data is not code

The classic stack-smash injected the attacker's own machine code into the buffer and returned into it. The **NX bit** (No-eXecute, also called **W^X**, "write xor execute", and **DEP**, Data Execution Prevention, on Windows) stops that: memory is either writable or executable, never both. The stack and heap are writable, so they are not executable; code is executable, so it is not writable. Jumping to injected code on the stack now faults.

Model memory as regions with permissions:

```python
regions = [(0x1000, 0x2000, {"r", "x"}), (0x7000, 0x8000, {"r", "w"})]  # code; stack
def may_execute(addr):
    return any(start <= addr < end and "x" in perms for start, end, perms in regions)
print(may_execute(0x1500), may_execute(0x7500))   # True False
```

Attackers answered NX with **[[return-oriented programming (ROP)|rop]]**: instead of injecting code, they chain together tiny snippets that already exist in the program's executable memory, each ending in a return. It works, but it is far more effort, and it is what the other two defences and newer ones (control-flow integrity) target. Making the attack harder still counts.

### Bounds checking and safe APIs: fix the cause

The mitigations above catch or blunt an overflow after it starts. The real cure is not to overflow. Two habits do it:

- **Bounds checking:** never copy without knowing the destination's size. You built the bounds-checked copy last lesson: `safe_copy(mem, dst, cap, src)` that writes at most `cap` bytes and always leaves room for the terminator.
- **Safe APIs:** use library functions that take the destination size. `snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, ...)` instead of `sprintf`; `strlcpy`/`strncpy` (with care) instead of `strcpy`; `std::string` and `std::vector` in C++ instead of raw arrays. Each of these knows how big the destination is, so it truncates instead of overflowing.

```cpp fragment
char buf[8];
snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "flight %d", n);   // never writes past buf; truncates if needed
```

A safe API that **truncates** turns a memory-corruption bug into, at worst, a wrong-but-bounded string. You must still check the return value to know truncation happened, but nothing is corrupted.

### Putting the numbers together

Suppose an overflow can reach the return address, the canary has 32 bits of entropy, and ASLR has 24 bits. To both survive the canary check (by writing the right canary) and land on the right address, a blind attacker guesses correctly with probability 1/2^32 × 1/2^24 = 1/2^56. Each wrong guess crashes the program. That is why the defences are stacked: each multiplies the attacker's difficulty, and multiplying small numbers gets very small fast.

### Where it is used

- Stack canaries are on by default in GCC and Clang (`-fstack-protector-strong`); ASLR and NX are standard in Linux, Windows, macOS, Android and iOS. Turning any of them off is now a notable, deliberate act.
- Flight and medical software often runs with no dynamic memory and every buffer fixed and bounds-checked (lesson 12), so overflows are prevented rather than merely caught.
- The long-run direction is to remove the bug class: memory-safe languages (Rust in particular) need none of these mitigations for their safe code, because the overflow cannot happen.

**Watch out:** treating a mitigation as a fix. "We have stack canaries, so overflows are handled" is wrong twice over. A canary only guards the return address, not other overwritten data, and only turns a takeover into a crash, which is itself a denial-of-service bug. Do not let a green light on the mitigations excuse an unbounded copy. Fix the copy.

::: context nullcanary The canary with a hole in it
Many stack canaries deliberately include a zero byte, often as the lowest byte, giving what is called a "null-byte canary" or "terminator canary". The reason is precise: the string functions that cause most overflows, `strcpy` and friends, stop at the first zero byte, so they cannot be used to write a zero into the middle of the region they overflow. An attacker who must overflow with a C string therefore cannot reproduce a canary that contains a zero, so even if they somehow learned its value they could not write it back. The cost is a little entropy, one byte's worth, traded for stopping a whole technique.
:::

::: context aslr-entropy Why bits of entropy is the right measure
If a randomized address is chosen uniformly from N equally likely places, an attacker guessing once is right with probability 1/N, and expects to need about N/2 tries. Writing N as 2^k, the number k is the "bits of entropy": each extra bit doubles N and so halves the attacker's chance per guess. Because each wrong guess against a modern service usually crashes the target and raises an alarm, even 20-odd bits can be enough to make blind brute force impractical. This is the same reasoning as password strength: what matters is the size of the space the secret is drawn from, measured in bits.
:::

::: context rop Return-oriented programming, in one paragraph
When NX stopped injected code from running, attackers found they did not need to inject any. A large program already contains many short instruction sequences that end in a return, called gadgets. By overflowing the stack with a list of addresses of gadgets, the attacker makes the CPU run them one after another, each return jumping to the next, stitching together a program out of pieces that were already there and already executable. ROP defeats NX but not ASLR (the gadget addresses move) and not a canary (the overflow still crosses it), which is exactly why the defences are layered.
:::
--- task
Model a guarded stack frame: a buffer of `cap` bytes, then a 4-byte canary (little-endian), then a 4-byte saved return address. Write three functions.

- `make_guarded_frame(cap, canary, ret)`: return a `bytearray` of length `cap + 8`: `cap` zero bytes, then `canary` as 4 little-endian bytes, then `ret` as 4 little-endian bytes.
- `canary_intact(mem, cap, canary)`: return `True` if the 4 bytes at `mem[cap:cap+4]`, read little-endian, still equal `canary`, and `False` otherwise.
- `protected_copy(mem, cap, canary, src)`: copy the bytes of `src` into the buffer starting at index 0, one byte at a time, then write a terminating `0` byte (an unsafe, C-style copy). Then check the canary. Return the string `"ok"` if the canary is still intact, or `"*** stack smashing detected ***"` if it changed. (Assume `src` is short enough that the copy stays inside `mem`.)

For example, with `cap = 8` and a canary of `0x01020304`, copying a 6-byte source returns `"ok"` and the canary is intact; copying a 12-byte source returns `"*** stack smashing detected ***"`.
--- starter
def make_guarded_frame(cap, canary, ret):
    raise NotImplementedError


def canary_intact(mem, cap, canary):
    raise NotImplementedError


def protected_copy(mem, cap, canary, src):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def make_guarded_frame(cap, canary, ret):
    return bytearray(cap) + bytearray(canary.to_bytes(4, "little")) + bytearray(ret.to_bytes(4, "little"))


def canary_intact(mem, cap, canary):
    return int.from_bytes(mem[cap:cap + 4], "little") == canary


def protected_copy(mem, cap, canary, src):
    i = 0
    while i < len(src):
        mem[i] = src[i]
        i += 1
    mem[len(src)] = 0
    if canary_intact(mem, cap, canary):
        return "ok"
    return "*** stack smashing detected ***"
--- hint
`make_guarded_frame` concatenates three pieces: `bytearray(cap)`, then the canary's four little-endian bytes, then the return address's four. `canary_intact` reads `mem[cap:cap+4]` with `int.from_bytes(..., "little")` and compares.
--- hint
`protected_copy` does the unsafe copy from last lesson (a loop plus a terminating `0`), then calls `canary_intact`. Return `"ok"` or the detection message based on the result.
--- hint
An overflow that writes into `mem[cap:cap+4]` changes the canary, so `canary_intact` returns `False` and you return the detection message.
--- check case | A copy that fits leaves the canary intact
(lambda m: protected_copy(m, 8, 0x01020304, b"ALDRIN"))(make_guarded_frame(8, 0x01020304, 0x08040201))
=> "ok"
--- check case | An overflow reaching the canary is detected
(lambda m: protected_copy(m, 8, 0x01020304, b"A" * 12))(make_guarded_frame(8, 0x01020304, 0x08040201))
=> "*** stack smashing detected ***"
--- check case | Exactly cap characters: the terminator alone trips the canary
(lambda m: protected_copy(m, 8, 0x01020304, b"A" * 8))(make_guarded_frame(8, 0x01020304, 0x08040201))
=> "*** stack smashing detected ***"
?? cap characters fill the buffer and the terminating 0 lands at index cap, the first canary byte.
--- check case | One short of cap is fine
(lambda m: protected_copy(m, 8, 0x01020304, b"A" * 7))(make_guarded_frame(8, 0x01020304, 0x08040201))
=> "ok"
--- check case | The frame is laid out buffer, canary, return address
(lambda m: (bytes(m[0:4]), bytes(m[4:8]), bytes(m[8:12])))(make_guarded_frame(4, 0x11223344, 0xDEADBEEF))
=> (b'\x00\x00\x00\x00', b'\x44\x33\x22\x11', b'\xef\xbe\xad\xde')
--- check case | canary_intact detects a single changed byte
(lambda m: (canary_intact(m, 4, 0x11223344), m.__setitem__(6, 0xFF), canary_intact(m, 4, 0x11223344))[::2])(make_guarded_frame(4, 0x11223344, 0))
=> (True, False)
--- check test | Detection happens exactly when the source needs cap or more bytes
all((lambda m: (protected_copy(m, 8, 0x01020304, b"Z" * n) == "*** stack smashing detected ***") == (n >= 8))(make_guarded_frame(8, 0x01020304, 0x08040201)) for n in range(0, 12))
--- check case | Even when detected, the return address may already be corrupt: the canary is what saved us
(lambda m: (protected_copy(m, 8, 0x01020304, b"B" * 12), int.from_bytes(m[8:12], "little"))[1])(make_guarded_frame(8, 0x01020304, 0x08040201))
=> 1111638594

+++ practice | Reproducible ASLR
--- task
Model ASLR with a seeded generator so results are reproducible. A base address can fall in any of `2 ** entropy_bits` slots, each `0x1000` bytes apart, above a fixed floor of `0x400000`.

Write `aslr_base(seed, entropy_bits)` that returns the randomized base address. Build the generator as `random.Random(seed)` and choose the slot with its `randrange(2 ** entropy_bits)` method, then return `0x400000 + slot * 0x1000`.

Also write `guess_probability(entropy_bits)` that returns the probability a single blind guess hits the base, as a `fractions.Fraction`: one over the number of slots.

For example, `aslr_base(1, 8)` is a fixed address for that seed, and `guess_probability(8)` is `Fraction(1, 256)`. With `entropy_bits = 0` there is one slot, so the base is always `0x400000` and the probability is `Fraction(1, 1)`.
--- starter
def aslr_base(seed, entropy_bits):
    return 0x400000


def guess_probability(entropy_bits):
    return 0
--- solution
import random
from fractions import Fraction


def aslr_base(seed, entropy_bits):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    slot = rng.randrange(2 ** entropy_bits)
    return 0x400000 + slot * 0x1000


def guess_probability(entropy_bits):
    return Fraction(1, 2 ** entropy_bits)
--- hint
Make a fresh `random.Random(seed)` each call, so the same seed always gives the same slot. Use `.randrange(2 ** entropy_bits)`.
--- hint
`guess_probability` is `Fraction(1, 2 ** entropy_bits)`; with 0 bits that is `Fraction(1, 1)`.
--- check case | Zero entropy pins the base and makes guessing certain
(aslr_base(999, 0), guess_probability(0))
=> (4194304, Fraction(1, 1))
--- check case | The same seed is reproducible
aslr_base(1, 8) == aslr_base(1, 8)
=> True
--- check case | The base is a valid slot above the floor
(lambda b: b >= 0x400000 and (b - 0x400000) % 0x1000 == 0 and (b - 0x400000) // 0x1000 < 256)(aslr_base(42, 8))
=> True
--- check case | Probabilities shrink as entropy grows
[guess_probability(k) for k in [1, 4, 16]]
=> [Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 16), Fraction(1, 65536)]
--- check test | Over many seeds the slot never leaves its range
all(0x400000 <= aslr_base(s, 10) < 0x400000 + 1024 * 0x1000 for s in range(200))

+++ practice | The null-byte canary
--- task
An attacker overflows a buffer using a C string, so the bytes they write onto the canary region cannot contain a zero (string copies stop at the first zero). Model whether they can forge the canary.

Write `can_forge(canary, attacker_bytes)`:

- `canary` is a 4-byte value (a little-endian integer) sitting in the region;
- `attacker_bytes` is the `bytes` object the attacker manages to write over the canary region.

Return `True` if `attacker_bytes` is exactly 4 bytes, contains no zero byte, and equals the canary's little-endian bytes; otherwise `False`. In particular, if the canary itself contains a zero byte, no zero-free `attacker_bytes` can ever match it, so the answer is `False`.

For example, `can_forge(0x01020304, b'\x04\x03\x02\x01')` is `True`, but `can_forge(0xAA00BB00, b'\x00\xbb\x00\xaa')` is `False`, because the attacker cannot write those zero bytes.
--- starter
def can_forge(canary, attacker_bytes):
    return True
--- solution
def can_forge(canary, attacker_bytes):
    if len(attacker_bytes) != 4:
        return False
    if 0 in attacker_bytes:
        return False
    return attacker_bytes == canary.to_bytes(4, "little")
--- hint
The attacker's bytes must be 4 long and contain no zero. In Python, `0 in some_bytes` tells you whether a zero byte is present.
--- hint
Only then does it matter whether they match: compare with `canary.to_bytes(4, "little")`. A canary that contains a zero can never be matched by zero-free bytes.
--- check case | A zero-free canary can be forged if the attacker knows it
can_forge(0x01020304, b'\x04\x03\x02\x01')
=> True
--- check case | A null-byte canary cannot be forged with a C string
can_forge(0xAA00BB00, b'\x00\xbb\x00\xaa')
=> False
--- check case | Wrong length never forges
(can_forge(0x01020304, b'\x04\x03\x02'), can_forge(0x01020304, b'\x04\x03\x02\x01\x00'))
=> (False, False)
--- check case | Right length, no zeros, but wrong value
can_forge(0x01020304, b'\xff\xff\xff\xff')
=> False
--- check case | Any canary with a zero byte resists every zero-free guess
all(not can_forge(0x11002233, bytes(b)) for b in [[0x33, 0x22, 1, 0x11], [1, 1, 1, 1], [0x33, 0x22, 0x00, 0x11]])
=> True

+++ practice | NX: data is not code
--- task
Model W^X memory permissions. `regions` is a list of `(start, end, perms)` tuples, where a region covers addresses `start` up to but not including `end`, and `perms` is a set drawn from `"r"`, `"w"`, `"x"`.

Write two functions:

- `may_execute(regions, addr)`: return `True` if `addr` lies in some region whose permissions include `"x"`, else `False`.
- `injected_code_runs(regions, addr)`: an attacker has written machine code to `addr` (so `addr` must be in a writable region) and tries to jump to it. Return `True` only if `addr` is in a region that is **both** writable and executable. Under W^X no region is both, so this is `False`; that is the whole point of NX.

For example, with a code region `(0x1000, 0x2000, {"r", "x"})` and a stack region `(0x7000, 0x8000, {"r", "w"})`, `may_execute` is `True` at `0x1500` and `False` at `0x7500`, and `injected_code_runs` at `0x7500` is `False`.
--- starter
def may_execute(regions, addr):
    return True


def injected_code_runs(regions, addr):
    return True
--- solution
