def may_execute(regions, addr):
    return any(start <= addr < end and "x" in perms for start, end, perms in regions)


def injected_code_runs(regions, addr):
    return any(start <= addr < end and "w" in perms and "x" in perms for start, end, perms in regions)
--- hint
For `may_execute`, scan the regions for one that contains `addr` (`start <= addr < end`) and has `"x"` in its permission set.
--- hint
`injected_code_runs` is the same scan but needs both `"w"` and `"x"` in the same region. Under W^X that never happens.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(lambda regions: (may_execute(regions, 0x1500), may_execute(regions, 0x7500), injected_code_runs(regions, 0x7500)))([(0x1000, 0x2000, {"r", "x"}), (0x7000, 0x8000, {"r", "w"})])
=> (True, False, False)
--- check case | A region boundary is exclusive at the top
(may_execute([(0x1000, 0x2000, {"x"})], 0x2000), may_execute([(0x1000, 0x2000, {"x"})], 0x1FFF))
=> (False, True)
--- check case | An address in no region cannot execute
may_execute([(0x1000, 0x2000, {"x"})], 0x5000)
=> False
--- check case | A broken (writable and executable) region lets injected code run
injected_code_runs([(0x3000, 0x4000, {"r", "w", "x"})], 0x3500)
=> True
--- check case | Overlapping regions: any executable one is enough
may_execute([(0x1000, 0x2000, {"r", "w"}), (0x1800, 0x2800, {"r", "x"})], 0x1900)
=> True

+++ practice | Debug: the canary check that misses the last byte
--- task
`is_safe_to_return(mem, cap, canary)` should return `True` only if the 4-byte canary at `mem[cap:cap+4]` still equals `canary` (a little-endian integer). It compares the canary byte by byte, but it has an off-by-one bug: it checks only the first 3 of the 4 bytes, so an overflow that changes only the **last** canary byte slips through undetected.

Find and fix the bug. The comparison should cover all 4 bytes.
--- starter
def is_safe_to_return(mem, cap, canary):
    expected = canary.to_bytes(4, "little")
    for i in range(3):
        if mem[cap + i] != expected[i]:
            return False
    return True
--- solution
def is_safe_to_return(mem, cap, canary):
    expected = canary.to_bytes(4, "little")
    for i in range(4):
        if mem[cap + i] != expected[i]:
            return False
    return True
--- hint
The canary is 4 bytes, but the loop runs `i` over `range(3)`, which is `0, 1, 2`. Which byte is never compared?
--- hint
Change `range(3)` to `range(4)` so all four bytes, including `mem[cap + 3]`, are checked.
--- check case | An intact canary is safe
(lambda m: is_safe_to_return(m, 4, 0x11223344))(bytearray(4) + bytearray((0x11223344).to_bytes(4, "little")))
=> True
--- check case | A change to the last byte must be caught
(lambda m: (m.__setitem__(7, 0xFF), is_safe_to_return(m, 4, 0x11223344))[1])(bytearray(4) + bytearray((0x11223344).to_bytes(4, "little")))
=> False
--- check case | A change to an earlier byte is caught
(lambda m: (m.__setitem__(4, 0xFF), is_safe_to_return(m, 4, 0x11223344))[1])(bytearray(4) + bytearray((0x11223344).to_bytes(4, "little")))
=> False
--- check case | A canary whose last byte differs from the rest
(lambda m: is_safe_to_return(m, 0, 0xAABBCCDD))(bytearray((0xAABBCCDD).to_bytes(4, "little")))
=> True
--- check test | Any single-byte change anywhere in the canary is detected
all((lambda m: (m.__setitem__(2 + i, m[2 + i] ^ 0xFF), not is_safe_to_return(m, 2, 0x01020304))[1])(bytearray(2) + bytearray((0x01020304).to_bytes(4, "little")) + bytearray(2)) for i in range(4))

+++ practice | Layered defences multiply
--- task
An attacker must defeat several independent, blind defences at once. Each defence is described by its bits of entropy: the attacker's chance of beating it in one try is `1 / 2 ** bits`. The defences are independent, so the chance of beating all of them in one try is the product.

Write `combined_odds(bits_list)` that returns, as a `fractions.Fraction`, the probability of beating every defence in `bits_list` in a single blind attempt. An empty list means no defences, so the probability is `Fraction(1, 1)` (certain).

Also write `total_bits(bits_list)` that returns the total bits of entropy, which is just the sum, because multiplying the probabilities adds the exponents.

For example, `combined_odds([32, 24])` is `Fraction(1, 2 ** 56)` and `total_bits([32, 24])` is `56`. `combined_odds([])` is `Fraction(1, 1)`.
--- starter
def combined_odds(bits_list):
    return 0


def total_bits(bits_list):
    return 0
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def combined_odds(bits_list):
    result = Fraction(1, 1)
    for bits in bits_list:
        result *= Fraction(1, 2 ** bits)
    return result


def total_bits(bits_list):
    return sum(bits_list)
--- hint
Start from `Fraction(1, 1)` and multiply by `Fraction(1, 2 ** bits)` for each defence. An empty list leaves the starting value, `Fraction(1, 1)`.
--- hint
`total_bits` is `sum(bits_list)`; note that `combined_odds(bits_list)` always equals `Fraction(1, 2 ** total_bits(bits_list))`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(combined_odds([32, 24]), total_bits([32, 24]))
=> (Fraction(1, 72057594037927936), 56)
--- check case | No defences means certain success
(combined_odds([]), total_bits([]))
=> (Fraction(1, 1), 0)
--- check case | A single defence
combined_odds([8])
=> Fraction(1, 256)
--- check case | Order does not matter
combined_odds([10, 6, 4]) == combined_odds([4, 6, 10])
=> True
--- check test | Combined odds always match one over two to the total bits
all(combined_odds(bl) == Fraction(1, 2 ** total_bits(bl)) for bl in [[1], [2, 3], [8, 8, 8], [0, 5], []])

=== sec-04 | Injection: SQL injection, command injection, and parameters
--- teach
The last three lessons were about a program corrupting its own memory. This one is about a program being tricked into running the wrong **commands**, without any memory bug at all. It is called **injection**, and it has topped the industry's list of web vulnerabilities for two decades. The idea reaches straight back to lesson 1: data crossing a trust boundary, treated as if it were trusted.

### The one sentence that explains every injection

An injection happens whenever a program builds a **command** out of a fixed part it wrote and a variable part that came from an untrusted user, by **pasting the strings together**. The interpreter on the other end, SQL engine, shell, HTML parser, cannot tell which characters the programmer meant as structure and which came from the user. So a user who writes characters that look like structure changes the command.

Every injection has the same cure, and it is not "filter bad characters". It is: **keep the code and the data apart**, and hand the data to the interpreter through a channel that can never be read as code.

### SQL injection

You know SQL from the SQL courses. Here is a lookup that finds a user by name, built the wrong way, by string concatenation:

```python fragment
# WRONG: the name is pasted straight into the query text
query = "SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = '" + name + "'"
con.execute(query)
```

For an ordinary name like `ada`, the query is `SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = 'ada'`. Fine. Now the user gives the name

```text
' OR '1'='1
```

The pasted-together query becomes:

```sql
SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = '' OR '1'='1'
```

The user's quote closed the string early, and `OR '1'='1'` is always true, so the query returns **every** user. The same trick with `'; DROP TABLE users; --` can destroy data; the `--` starts a SQL comment that hides the leftover `'`. This is **SQL injection**, and it has leaked millions of passwords, credit-card numbers and health records.

### The fix: parameters

The fix is a **parameterised query**, also called a **[[prepared statement|prepared]]**. You write the query with a placeholder, `?` in SQLite, where the value goes, and pass the value separately:

```python fragment
# RIGHT: the ? is a placeholder; the value is passed apart from the query text
con.execute("SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = ?", (name,))
```

The database receives the query **structure** and the **value** through two different channels. It compiles the query with a hole in it, then drops the value into the hole as data. There is no string for the value to break out of, because the value is never part of the query text. Whatever `name` contains, quotes, semicolons, `OR '1'='1`, it is only ever compared as a literal name. No name in the table equals the string `' OR '1'='1`, so the malicious input simply matches nothing.

This is not "escaping done for you", though it is often described that way. It is structurally different: the value never enters the SQL grammar at all.

### Why not just escape the quotes?

You could try to make the concatenation safe by **escaping**: in SQL a single quote inside a string is written as two single quotes, so you could replace every `'` in the user's input with `''`. It even works for the simple case. But hand-rolled escaping is a losing game:

- You must get every context right: string literals, identifiers, `LIKE` patterns (where `%` and `_` are special), numbers, dates. Each has different rules.
- One forgotten spot, or one different database with different rules, and the hole is back.
- A backslash, a different quote style, a multi-byte character trick, any of these has defeated real escaping code.

The right rule is: **let the interpreter do it, through parameters, every time.** Write your own escaping only when there is truly no parameter channel, and then treat it as dangerous code that needs review. You will implement the SQL-string escaping in the practice, precisely so you can see how many cases it has to cover, and why parameters are better.

### Command injection

The same bug with a different interpreter. A program runs an external tool by building a **command line** and handing it to a **shell**:

```python fragment
# WRONG: filename is pasted into a string handed to a shell
os.system("cat logs/" + filename)
```

For `filename = "flight.log"` the shell runs `cat logs/flight.log`. But the shell treats `;`, `|`, `&`, `$()` and backticks as **structure**. So `filename = "x; rm -rf data"` makes the shell see two commands:

```text
cat logs/x ; rm -rf data
```

The `;` ends the first command and starts a second one the attacker chose. This is **command injection**, and it is how many devices with a web interface, routers, cameras, get taken over.

The cure is the same shape: keep code and data apart. Do not build a shell string at all. Pass the program and its arguments as a **list**, straight to the operating system, with no shell in between:

```python fragment
# RIGHT: a list of arguments; no shell parses it, so ; is just part of a filename
subprocess.run(["cat", "logs/" + filename], shell=False)
```

Now `filename` is one argument, whole. The `;` in `"x; rm -rf data"` is just characters in a filename that does not exist, not a command separator, because no shell ever looked at it. The list is the parameter channel for the operating system: the command (`"cat"`) and the data (the filename) travel in separate slots that cannot be confused.

You will model both sides in Python: a tiny "shell" that splits a command string on `;` the way a real shell does, to show the injection, and the safe argument-list form that no shell ever parses.

### The general principle

Injection is one bug wearing many hats. Others you will meet: **cross-site scripting** (next lesson) is HTML/JavaScript injection into a web page; **LDAP injection**, **XPath injection**, **log injection** and **template injection** are the same mistake against other interpreters. The defence is always one of:

1. **Use a parameter channel** (prepared statements, argument lists, a templating engine that escapes automatically). This is first choice, always.
2. If there is genuinely no parameter channel, **escape for the exact target grammar** using a well-tested library, never by hand.
3. **Validate** the input against a strict [[allowlist|allowlist]] (a flight id is `[A-Z]{3}-\d{3}` and nothing else) as an extra layer, never as the only one.

Data does not become safe by having been stored, either: a value written to your database with parameters and later read back and concatenated into a new query is a **[[second-order injection|second-order]]** waiting to fire. Allowlisting deserves a word: checking that input is one of a known-good set is far stronger than blocklisting "bad" characters, because you cannot enumerate everything bad, but you can often enumerate everything good.

### Where it is used

- SQL injection appears in the OWASP Top Ten every time it is published, and every serious database library offers parameters; using them is the norm, and not using them is the finding an auditor writes up.
- Command injection is the classic way embedded and IoT devices fall: a web form field pasted into a shell command inside the firmware.
- ORBIT's own tools take user input everywhere, a mission name typed into a form, a filename in an upload, a device id from a serial port, and each is a place to ask lesson 1's question: does this cross a trust boundary, and is it kept as data on the other side?

**Watch out:** the trap is thinking your input filter is enough. Teams strip the word `OR`, or block the character `'`, and feel safe. Attackers answer with `oR`, with `%27` (a URL-encoded quote the framework decodes later), with comment tricks, with numeric contexts that need no quote at all (`id = ` + `1 OR 1=1`). Blocklists lose. The only reliable fix is the parameter channel, which makes the input harmless no matter what it contains, because it is never treated as code in the first place.

::: context prepared How a prepared statement works inside
When you call `execute` with a `?` placeholder, the database first **prepares** the statement: it parses the SQL text, plans how to run it, and keeps that plan with a hole where the value goes. Then it **binds** your value into the hole and runs the plan. Because parsing and planning happen before your value is anywhere near the statement, the value cannot change the plan's shape; it can only fill the hole, as data of a fixed type. A bonus: the same prepared plan can be run many times with different bound values, which is faster than re-parsing each time, so parameters are both safer and often quicker.
:::

::: context second-order The injection that waits
A subtle variant is "second-order" or stored injection. The attacker's malicious string is safely stored first, perhaps their chosen username, with parameters, so nothing happens yet. Later, some other code reads that value back out of the database and builds a new query by concatenating it, trusting it because "it came from our own database". The payload fires then. The lesson: data does not become trusted by having been stored. Anything that originated outside the trust boundary stays untrusted forever, and every query built from it needs parameters, even the ones that read your own tables.
:::

::: context allowlist Allowlists beat blocklists
A blocklist tries to name everything dangerous and reject it; an allowlist names everything permitted and rejects the rest. Allowlists win because the set of good inputs is usually small and knowable, while the set of bad inputs is effectively infinite and grows as attackers invent encodings. For a field with a known shape, a date, an identifier, a country code, validate it against a strict pattern and refuse anything else. Combined with a parameter channel, this is defence in depth: parameters make the input harmless to the interpreter, and the allowlist makes sure it was the shape you expected in the first place.
:::
--- task
Write both the vulnerable and the safe versions of each kind of lookup, so the difference is concrete.

- `build_query(name)`: return the SQL string built the **wrong** way, by concatenation: `SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = '<name>'`, with `name` pasted directly between the single quotes. (This is the vulnerable version, shown so a check can prove it is exploitable.)
- `safe_lookup(con, name)`: using the SQLite connection `con`, run a **parameterised** query `SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = ?` with `name` bound as the parameter, and return the list of matching ids (each row's first column), in the order returned.
- `shell_commands(command)`: model a shell. Split `command` on the separator `;` into separate commands, strip the surrounding spaces from each, and drop any empty pieces. Return the list of command strings. (This shows how a built string becomes several commands.)
- `safe_argv(filename)`: return the safe argument list for showing a log file without a shell: `["cat", "logs/" + filename]`. Because it is a list, the whole filename is one argument.

For example, `build_query("ada")` is `"SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = 'ada'"`; `shell_commands("cat logs/x; rm -rf data")` is `["cat logs/x", "rm -rf data"]`; and `safe_argv("x; rm -rf data")` is `["cat", "logs/x; rm -rf data"]` (one filename argument, no second command).
--- starter
def build_query(name):
    raise NotImplementedError


def safe_lookup(con, name):
    raise NotImplementedError


def shell_commands(command):
    raise NotImplementedError


def safe_argv(filename):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def build_query(name):
    return "SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = '" + name + "'"


def safe_lookup(con, name):
    cur = con.execute("SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = ?", (name,))
    return [row[0] for row in cur.fetchall()]


def shell_commands(command):
    parts = command.split(";")
    return [p.strip() for p in parts if p.strip() != ""]


def safe_argv(filename):
    return ["cat", "logs/" + filename]
--- hint
`build_query` is plain string concatenation: `"...name = '" + name + "'"`. `safe_lookup` uses a `?` placeholder and passes `(name,)` as the second argument to `execute`, then collects `row[0]` for each row.
--- hint
`shell_commands` does `command.split(";")`, then strips each piece and keeps only the non-empty ones. `safe_argv` returns the two-element list `["cat", "logs/" + filename]`.
--- check case | The vulnerable query for an ordinary name
build_query("ada")
=> "SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = 'ada'"
--- check case | The classic payload turns the query into one that is always true
build_query("' OR '1'='1")
=> "SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = '' OR '1'='1'"
--- check test | The concatenated query really does leak every row when run
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO users VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "ada"), (2, "lin"), (3, "sam")]), len(con.execute(build_query("' OR '1'='1")).fetchall()) == 3)[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
?? Build the query for the payload, run it, and count the rows: all of them come back.
--- check test | The parameterised lookup is immune to the same payload
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO users VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "ada"), (2, "lin"), (3, "sam")]), safe_lookup(con, "' OR '1'='1") == [])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check test | The parameterised lookup finds the right user
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO users VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "ada"), (2, "lin"), (3, "sam")]), safe_lookup(con, "lin") == [2])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check test | Parameters handle a name with an apostrophe with no trouble
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO users VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "O'Brien"), (2, "lin")]), safe_lookup(con, "O'Brien") == [1])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check case | A single command stays one command
shell_commands("cat logs/flight.log")
=> ["cat logs/flight.log"]
--- check case | An injected separator becomes a second command
shell_commands("cat logs/x; rm -rf data")
=> ["cat logs/x", "rm -rf data"]
--- check case | Empty pieces and extra spaces are dropped
shell_commands("  a ;; b ; ")
=> ["a", "b"]
--- check case | The safe argument list keeps the whole filename as one argument
safe_argv("x; rm -rf data")
=> ["cat", "logs/x; rm -rf data"]
--- check case | No shell means the separator never splits anything
(len(safe_argv("a; b; c")), shell_commands(safe_argv("a; b; c")[1]))
=> (2, ["logs/a", "b", "c"])
?? safe_argv returns two items no matter what; only if you handed its filename back to a shell would ; matter.

+++ practice | Two parameters
--- task
Write `find_recent(con, mission, min_id)` that returns the ids of rows in a table `events(id INTEGER, mission TEXT)` whose `mission` equals the given `mission` **and** whose `id` is at least `min_id`, ordered by `id` ascending. Use a parameterised query with two placeholders; never build the query by concatenation.

Return a list of ids. If nothing matches, return `[]`.

For example, with rows `(1, "apollo")`, `(5, "apollo")`, `(3, "gemini")`, `find_recent(con, "apollo", 2)` is `[5]`.
--- starter
def find_recent(con, mission, min_id):
    return []
--- solution
def find_recent(con, mission, min_id):
    cur = con.execute("SELECT id FROM events WHERE mission = ? AND id >= ? ORDER BY id", (mission, min_id))
    return [row[0] for row in cur.fetchall()]
--- hint
Use two `?` placeholders, one for the mission and one for the minimum id, and pass `(mission, min_id)` as the parameter tuple.
--- hint
Add `ORDER BY id` so the ids come back ascending, and collect `row[0]` from each row.
--- check test | The example from the task
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE events(id INTEGER, mission TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO events VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "apollo"), (5, "apollo"), (3, "gemini")]), find_recent(con, "apollo", 2) == [5])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check test | Ordered ascending, both conditions applied
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE events(id INTEGER, mission TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO events VALUES(?,?)", [(9, "apollo"), (2, "apollo"), (5, "apollo"), (7, "gemini")]), find_recent(con, "apollo", 3) == [5, 9])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check test | No match gives an empty list
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE events(id INTEGER, mission TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO events VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "apollo")]), find_recent(con, "gemini", 0) == [])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check test | A malicious mission name matches nothing, injects nothing
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE events(id INTEGER, mission TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO events VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "apollo"), (2, "gemini")]), find_recent(con, "' OR '1'='1", 0) == [])[-1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))

+++ practice | Escaping a SQL string literal by hand
--- task
Sometimes there is no parameter channel and you must build a string literal yourself. In SQL, a text literal is wrapped in single quotes, and a single quote **inside** it is written as two single quotes.

Write `sql_literal(s)` that returns `s` as a safe SQL single-quoted string literal: wrap the whole thing in single quotes, and double every single quote inside `s`.

For example, `sql_literal("ada")` is `"'ada'"`, and `sql_literal("O'Brien")` is `"'O''Brien'"`. The empty string gives `"''"`, and `sql_literal("' OR '1'='1")` is `"''' OR ''1''=''1'"`, which is a harmless literal, not a broken query.

(This is a teaching exercise. In real code, use parameters; only escape by hand when you truly cannot, and then use a tested library.)
--- starter
def sql_literal(s):
    return s
--- solution
def sql_literal(s):
    return "'" + s.replace("'", "''") + "'"
--- hint
Replace every single quote in `s` with two single quotes: `s.replace("'", "''")`.
--- hint
Then wrap the whole result in single quotes by adding one at the start and one at the end.
--- check case | An ordinary word
sql_literal("ada")
=> "'ada'"
--- check case | An apostrophe is doubled
sql_literal("O'Brien")
=> "'O''Brien'"
--- check case | The empty string
sql_literal("")
=> "''"
--- check case | The injection payload becomes a harmless literal
sql_literal("' OR '1'='1")
=> "''' OR ''1''=''1'"
--- check case | Several quotes in a row
sql_literal("a''b")
=> "'a''''b'"

+++ practice | Build the safe argument vector
--- task
A tool is run with several user-supplied arguments. Building one shell string invites command injection; building an argument list does not.

Write `build_argv(program, args)` that returns the argument vector: a list whose first element is `program` and whose remaining elements are the items of the list `args`, unchanged and in order. Nothing is quoted or joined; each argument stays a separate list element, so no shell ever splits it.

Then write `is_shell_safe(argv)` that returns `True` if running `argv` as an argument vector (no shell) cannot inject a second command, which is always the case for a well-formed vector: return `True` when `argv` is a non-empty list of strings, and `False` otherwise.

For example, `build_argv("grep", ["-n", "boom; rm x", "file.txt"])` is `["grep", "-n", "boom; rm x", "file.txt"]`, and `is_shell_safe` of it is `True`.
--- starter
def build_argv(program, args):
    return [program]


def is_shell_safe(argv):
    return True
--- solution
def build_argv(program, args):
    return [program] + list(args)


def is_shell_safe(argv):
    return isinstance(argv, list) and len(argv) >= 1 and all(isinstance(a, str) for a in argv)
--- hint
`build_argv` puts `program` first and then every element of `args`, in order: `[program] + list(args)`.
--- hint
`is_shell_safe` checks that `argv` is a list, has at least one element, and every element is a string.
--- check case | The example from the task
build_argv("grep", ["-n", "boom; rm x", "file.txt"])
=> ["grep", "-n", "boom; rm x", "file.txt"]
--- check case | A dangerous-looking argument stays one element
(lambda v: (len(v), v[2]))(build_argv("grep", ["-n", "a; b | c", "f"]))
=> (4, "a; b | c")
--- check case | No extra arguments
build_argv("ls", [])
=> ["ls"]
--- check case | A well-formed vector is shell-safe
is_shell_safe(build_argv("cat", ["logs/x; rm -rf data"]))
=> True
--- check case | Malformed vectors are rejected
(is_shell_safe([]), is_shell_safe("cat logs/x"), is_shell_safe(["cat", 3]))
=> (False, False, False)

+++ practice | Debug: the escaper that stops after the first quote
--- task
`escape_all(s)` should double **every** single quote in `s`, so the result can go safely inside a SQL string literal. The version below uses `replace` with a count of 1, so it doubles only the **first** quote and leaves the rest to break out of the literal.

Find and fix the bug. For example, `escape_all("a'b'c")` should be `"a''b''c"`, but the buggy version gives `"a''b'c"`.
--- starter
def escape_all(s):
    return s.replace("'", "''", 1)
--- solution
def escape_all(s):
    return s.replace("'", "''")
--- hint
The third argument to `replace` is the maximum number of replacements. What does `1` mean here?
--- hint
Remove the `, 1` so `replace` doubles every quote, not just the first.
--- check case | The example from the task
escape_all("a'b'c")
=> "a''b''c"
--- check case | A string with no quotes is unchanged
escape_all("ada")
=> "ada"
--- check case | Many quotes are all doubled
escape_all("''''")
=> "''''''''"
--- check case | The empty string
escape_all("")
=> ""

+++ practice | Detect a risky command build
--- task
Before a program hands a built string to a shell, a reviewer wants to flag the risky ones. A command string is **risky** if it contains any shell metacharacter that could start or change a command. Treat these as metacharacters: `;`, `|`, `&`, `` ` `` (backtick), `$`, `>`, `<`, and the newline character.

Write `is_risky(command)` that returns `True` if `command` contains any of those characters, and `False` otherwise.

Then write `count_risky(commands)` that returns how many strings in the list `commands` are risky.

For example, `is_risky("cat logs/flight.log")` is `False`, `is_risky("cat logs/x; rm -rf data")` is `True`, and `count_risky(["ls -l", "a | b", "c > d"])` is `2`.
--- starter
def is_risky(command):
    return False


def count_risky(commands):
    return 0
--- solution
META = set(";|&`$><\n")


def is_risky(command):
    return any(ch in META for ch in command)


def count_risky(commands):
    return sum(1 for c in commands if is_risky(c))
--- hint
Put the metacharacters in a set, then check whether any character of `command` is in that set with `any(...)`.
--- hint
`count_risky` counts the commands for which `is_risky` is `True`. Remember the newline `"\n"` is one of the metacharacters.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(is_risky("cat logs/flight.log"), is_risky("cat logs/x; rm -rf data"), count_risky(["ls -l", "a | b", "c > d"]))
=> (False, True, 2)
--- check case | A command substitution is risky
(is_risky("echo $(whoami)"), is_risky("echo `id`"))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | A newline can smuggle a second command
is_risky("cat file\nrm -rf data")
=> True
--- check case | Plain arguments are not risky
count_risky(["grep -n boom file", "python main.py", "ls"])
=> 0
--- check case | Redirection and background both count
(is_risky("dump > /etc/passwd"), is_risky("worker &"))
=> (True, True)

=== sec-05 | Web security: XSS, the same-origin policy, CSRF and escaping
--- teach
Last lesson's injection had the database and the shell as its victims. On the web, the interpreter being tricked is often the **browser**, and the command being injected is HTML and JavaScript. This lesson covers the browser's own defences and the escaping that a server must do. You have the background from Computer Networks (HTTP, requests and responses) and from the SQL injection lesson; the shape of the bug is the same, keep code and data apart, in a new setting.

### Cross-site scripting

A web page is HTML text. When a server builds that text by pasting in something a user typed, and a user typed HTML, the browser runs it. This is **cross-site scripting (XSS)**: injecting HTML or JavaScript into a page so it runs in another user's browser.

Here is a comment shown on a page, built the wrong way:

```python fragment
# WRONG: the comment is pasted straight into the page's HTML
html = "<li>" + comment + "</li>"
```

For a comment like `Nice launch!` the HTML is `<li>Nice launch!</li>`. Fine. Now someone posts the comment

```text
<script>steal(document.cookie)</script>
```

The page becomes `<li><script>steal(document.cookie)</script></li>`, and **every visitor's browser runs the script**, in the context of your site. It can read their session cookie, act as them, rewrite the page, or send their data away. Because the script runs with the victim's session, XSS turns one posted comment into an attack on everyone who views it.

Why is this so dangerous? A visitor's browser trusts code that comes from your site's page. An injected script *is* code on your site's page, as far as the browser can tell. That trust is what the same-origin policy, below, is built to protect, and what XSS defeats from the inside.

### The fix: escape on output

The value the user typed is **data**; it must never be read as HTML **structure**. So before putting any untrusted text into HTML, replace the characters that mean "structure" with their harmless **[[HTML entities|entities]]**:

| character | entity | why |
|---|---|---|
| `&` | `&amp;` | starts an entity, so must itself be escaped first |
| `<` | `&lt;` | starts a tag |
| `>` | `&gt;` | ends a tag |
| `"` | `&quot;` | ends a quoted attribute value |
| `'` | `&#x27;` | ends a single-quoted attribute value |

After escaping, `<script>` becomes the text `&lt;script&gt;`, which the browser **shows** as the characters `<script>` and does not run. Here is the ampersand and one bracket, escaped on a small example:

```python
text = "Tom & Jerry <3"
step1 = text.replace("&", "&amp;")     # ampersand first, always
step2 = step1.replace("<", "&lt;")
print(step2)                            # Tom &amp; Jerry &lt;3
```

**The order matters.** Escape `&` **first**. If you escaped `<` first, turning it into `&lt;`, then escaped `&`, you would turn that `&` into `&amp;`, giving `&amp;lt;`, which shows on the page as the literal text `&lt;` instead of `<`. Ampersand first, then the rest.

The real rule is the same as last lesson: prefer a channel that escapes automatically. Every serious templating engine (Jinja, React's JSX, and others) escapes by default, and you turn escaping **off** only for a value you have deliberately made safe. Manual escaping is for when you build HTML by hand, and even then a tested library is better than your own `replace` chain, because different **contexts** need different escaping.

### Context matters

Escaping for HTML text is not the same as escaping for other places in a page:

- **HTML text** (between tags): escape `&`, `<`, `>`.
- **An attribute value**: also escape the quote that surrounds it, or the attacker closes the attribute and adds their own (`onmouseover=...`).
- **Inside a `<script>` block or a URL or CSS**: different rules again; putting untrusted data there is best avoided entirely.

This is why "just escaping" is not enough on its own: you must escape for the exact place the data lands. Libraries know these contexts; hand-rolled code forgets them.

### The same-origin policy

The browser's foundational defence is the **same-origin policy (SOP)**. An **origin** is the triple **(scheme, host, port)**: `https://orbit.example:443`. The policy says that a page from one origin cannot read data from a different origin: a script on `evil.example` cannot read the responses your bank's page received from `bank.example`, cannot read its cookies, cannot see inside its frame.

Two URLs share an origin only when all three parts match. A missing port means the scheme's default (80 for `http`, 443 for `https`).

```python
from urllib.parse import urlsplit
a = urlsplit("https://orbit.example/dashboard")
b = urlsplit("https://orbit.example:443/login")
print(a.scheme, a.hostname)          # https orbit.example
print(a.hostname == b.hostname)      # True
```

Different scheme (`http` vs `https`), different host (`a.example` vs `b.example`), or different port are all **different** origins, and the policy keeps them apart. The SOP is why one open browser tab cannot silently ransack another. Servers can relax it deliberately and carefully with **CORS** (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) headers, which is a way to opt specific other origins in, not a way to turn the policy off.

### Cross-site request forgery

The SOP stops another origin from **reading** your site's responses. It does **not** stop another origin from **sending** a request to your site, with the user's cookies attached. That gap is **cross-site request forgery (CSRF)**.

Cookies are sent by the browser on every request to a site, automatically, whoever caused the request. So a page on `evil.example` can contain

```html fragment
<img src="https://bank.example/transfer?to=eve&amount=1000">
```

and when the victim, already logged in to the bank, loads the evil page, their browser fetches that URL **with their bank cookies attached**. The bank sees a valid, logged-in request and does the transfer. The attacker never saw the response (the SOP blocks that); they did not need to.

Two defences, used together:

- **A CSRF token.** The server puts a secret, unpredictable token into its own forms, and requires it back on every state-changing request. A page from another origin cannot read the token (the SOP stops it reading your page), so it cannot include the right one. The server compares the submitted token with the expected one using a **[[constant-time comparison|consttime]]** so that timing does not leak it.
- **[[`SameSite` cookies|samesite]].** A cookie marked `SameSite=Strict` or `SameSite=Lax` is **not** sent on requests that come from another site, which removes the automatic-cookie behaviour CSRF relies on. This is now the browser default for `Lax`.

The deeper rule behind CSRF defence: a request that changes state must carry proof that it came from **your** page, not merely proof of **who** the user is. Cookies prove who; the token proves where from.

### Escaping versus validation, again

As with SQL: escaping (or a parameter/auto-escaping channel) makes the data safe for the interpreter, and validation (an allowlist for what the field should contain) is a second layer. A comment field can hold almost anything, so escaping carries the weight there; a "country code" field should be validated to two letters and escaping is a backstop. Use both.

### Where it is used

- XSS and CSRF are perennial entries in the OWASP Top Ten. Real incidents include the Samy worm (2005), which used XSS to add over a million MySpace friends in a day, and countless cookie-stealing attacks since.
- The same-origin policy is the reason the web can host untrusted content at all: it is enforced by every browser, and much of web security is careful, explicit exceptions to it (CORS, `postMessage`, cookies' `SameSite` and `Secure` flags).
- ORBIT's dashboards show user-entered mission names, operator comments and uploaded filenames; each is untrusted text going into HTML, so each is escaped on output, and each state-changing action carries a token.

**Watch out:** escaping on **input** instead of on **output**. Teams sometimes escape when they store the data, then store it escaped, then sometimes escape again on display (double-escaping, so `&amp;` shows on the page), and sometimes use the stored value in a non-HTML context where the HTML escaping is wrong. Store the data **raw** (kept safe from SQL injection by parameters), and escape it **when and where you output it**, for the exact context it lands in. Output escaping is the rule.

::: context entities Why HTML has entities at all
An HTML entity is a code standing for a character that would otherwise be taken as markup: `&lt;` for `<`, `&amp;` for `&`, and named ones like `&copy;` for the copyright sign. They exist because the characters that structure HTML, chiefly `<` and `&`, also sometimes need to appear as ordinary text. Entities are the escape mechanism built into the language. Escaping user data for HTML is exactly the act of turning any structural character into its entity, so the browser renders it as text rather than acting on it. Every character can also be written as a numeric entity, `&#60;` or `&#x3c;` for `<`, which is why blocklisting the word "script" never works: `&#x73;cript` and many other spellings survive.
:::

::: context consttime Comparing secrets in constant time
An ordinary string comparison returns `False` at the first differing character, so it takes slightly longer the more leading characters match. An attacker who can measure the time can therefore guess a secret one character at a time, turning an astronomically large search into a short one. A constant-time comparison always looks at the whole input and takes the same time whether the strings match or differ at the first or last position. Python's `hmac.compare_digest` does exactly this, and it is what you should use to check tokens, password hashes and signatures. You will use it in this lesson's practice and again in the password and protocol lessons.
:::

::: context samesite The cookie flags that matter for security
A cookie can carry flags that change when the browser sends it. `Secure` means "only over HTTPS", so it is never sent in the clear. `HttpOnly` means "JavaScript cannot read this cookie", which limits what an XSS script can steal. `SameSite` controls cross-site sending: `Strict` never sends the cookie on a cross-site request, `Lax` (now the default) sends it only on top-level navigations like clicking a link, and `None` sends it always but then requires `Secure`. Together these flags harden a session cookie against both XSS theft and CSRF, which is why setting them correctly is a standard item on any web security checklist.
:::
--- task
Model the server's output-escaping and origin checks in Python.

- `escape_html(s)`: return `s` with the five HTML-special characters replaced by their entities, in the correct order (`&` first): `&`→`&amp;`, `<`→`&lt;`, `>`→`&gt;`, `"`→`&quot;`, `'`→`&#x27;`.
- `render_comment(comment)`: return the safe HTML list item for a comment: `"<li>"`, then the escaped comment, then `"</li>"`.
- `same_origin(url1, url2)`: return `True` if the two URLs have the same origin, that is the same scheme, host and port, and `False` otherwise. A URL with no explicit port uses the scheme's default: 80 for `http`, 443 for `https`. Use `urllib.parse.urlsplit`; its `.hostname` is already lower-cased and `.port` is `None` when absent.

For example, `escape_html("<script>")` is `"&lt;script&gt;"`; `render_comment("Nice & tidy")` is `"<li>Nice &amp; tidy</li>"`; and `same_origin("https://a.example/x", "https://a.example:443/y")` is `True`.
--- starter
def escape_html(s):
    raise NotImplementedError


def render_comment(comment):
    raise NotImplementedError


def same_origin(url1, url2):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
from urllib.parse import urlsplit

_DEFAULT_PORT = {"http": 80, "https": 443}


def escape_html(s):
    s = s.replace("&", "&amp;")
    s = s.replace("<", "&lt;")
    s = s.replace(">", "&gt;")
    s = s.replace('"', "&quot;")
    s = s.replace("'", "&#x27;")
    return s


def render_comment(comment):
    return "<li>" + escape_html(comment) + "</li>"


def _origin(url):
    parts = urlsplit(url)
    port = parts.port if parts.port is not None else _DEFAULT_PORT.get(parts.scheme)
    return (parts.scheme, parts.hostname, port)


def same_origin(url1, url2):
    return _origin(url1) == _origin(url2)
--- hint
`escape_html` is a chain of `replace` calls. Do `&` first, then `<`, `>`, `"` and `'`. Doing `&` first is what stops the other entities' ampersands from being re-escaped.
--- hint
`render_comment` wraps `escape_html(comment)` in `<li>` and `</li>`.
--- hint
For `same_origin`, split each URL with `urlsplit`, take `(scheme, hostname, port)`, and fill in the default port (80 or 443) when `.port` is `None`. Two URLs share an origin when those triples are equal.
--- check case | A script tag is neutralised
escape_html("<script>alert(1)</script>")
=> "&lt;script&gt;alert(1)&lt;/script&gt;"
--- check case | Ampersand is escaped first, not double-escaped
escape_html("Tom & Jerry")
=> "Tom &amp; Jerry"
--- check case | An ampersand next to a bracket comes out right
escape_html("a<b & c>d")
=> "a&lt;b &amp; c&gt;d"
?? If you escaped < before &, the & in &lt; would become &amp;lt;. Do & first.
--- check case | Both kinds of quote
escape_html("say \"hi\" it's me")
=> "say &quot;hi&quot; it&#x27;s me"
--- check case | Text with nothing special is unchanged
escape_html("Nice launch")
=> "Nice launch"
--- check case | A rendered comment is safe
render_comment("<img src=x onerror=steal()>")
=> "<li>&lt;img src=x onerror=steal()&gt;</li>"
--- check case | Same origin with an explicit default port
same_origin("https://a.example/x", "https://a.example:443/y")
=> True
--- check case | Different scheme, host or port are different origins
(same_origin("http://a.example/", "https://a.example/"), same_origin("https://a.example/", "https://b.example/"), same_origin("https://a.example:8443/", "https://a.example/"))
=> (False, False, False)
--- check case | Host comparison ignores case; path and query do not matter
same_origin("https://ORBIT.Example/dashboard?x=1", "https://orbit.example/login")
=> True
--- check test | Escaping never leaves a raw structural character behind
all(c not in escape_html(s) for s in ["<a href='x'>&\"", "100% < 200% & rising", "</li>"] for c in "<>")

+++ practice | Escaping an attribute value
--- task
When user text goes inside a double-quoted HTML attribute, the dangerous character is the double quote, which would end the attribute and let the attacker add their own (like `onmouseover=`). The `<`, `>` and `&` still matter too.

Write `attr_value(value)` that returns the value ready to sit inside double quotes: escape `&`, `<`, `>` and `"` (in that order), then wrap the whole thing in double-quote characters.

For example, `attr_value("normal")` is `'"normal"'`, and `attr_value('x" onmouseover="evil()')` is `'"x&quot; onmouseover=&quot;evil()"'`: the injected quotes are neutralised, so the attribute cannot be closed early.
--- starter
def attr_value(value):
    return value
--- solution
def attr_value(value):
    value = value.replace("&", "&amp;")
    value = value.replace("<", "&lt;")
    value = value.replace(">", "&gt;")
    value = value.replace('"', "&quot;")
    return '"' + value + '"'
--- hint
Escape in the order `&`, `<`, `>`, `"`. The double quote is the key one here, because it is what ends the attribute.
--- hint
After escaping, wrap the result in literal double-quote characters, one at each end.
--- check case | An ordinary value
attr_value("normal")
=> "\"normal\""
--- check case | An attribute-breakout attempt is neutralised
attr_value("x\" onmouseover=\"evil()")
=> "\"x&quot; onmouseover=&quot;evil()\""
--- check case | Ampersand first
attr_value("a & b")
=> "\"a &amp; b\""
--- check case | Angle brackets are escaped too
attr_value("<>")
=> "\"&lt;&gt;\""
--- check case | The empty value is just a pair of quotes
attr_value("")
=> "\"\""

+++ practice | A safe comment list
--- task
Write `render_list(comments)` that returns one HTML string: a `<ul>`, then one `<li>...</li>` for each comment in `comments` with the comment escaped for HTML, then a closing `</ul>`. There is no whitespace between the tags.

Escape each comment by replacing `&`, `<` and `>` with their entities (`&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;`), in that order.

For example, `render_list(["hi", "<b>"])` is `"<ul><li>hi</li><li>&lt;b&gt;</li></ul>"`. An empty list gives `"<ul></ul>"`.
--- starter
