    return ""
--- solution
def esc(s):
    return s.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;")


def render_list(comments):
    return "<ul>" + "".join("<li>" + esc(c) + "</li>" for c in comments) + "</ul>"
--- hint
Escape each comment (`&`, then `<`, then `>`), wrap it in `<li>...</li>`, and join all of them with no separator.
--- hint
Put the joined items between `"<ul>"` and `"</ul>"`. An empty list joins to the empty string, giving `"<ul></ul>"`.
--- check case | The example from the task
render_list(["hi", "<b>"])
=> "<ul><li>hi</li><li>&lt;b&gt;</li></ul>"
--- check case | An empty list
render_list([])
=> "<ul></ul>"
--- check case | A script payload is escaped
render_list(["<script>x</script>"])
=> "<ul><li>&lt;script&gt;x&lt;/script&gt;</li></ul>"
--- check case | Ampersands are handled first
render_list(["A & B", "C"])
=> "<ul><li>A &amp; B</li><li>C</li></ul>"
--- check test | The rendered list never contains a raw < from user text
"<script" not in render_list(["<script>evil</script>", "ok"])

+++ practice | Origin matching for CORS
--- task
A server keeps an allowlist of origins it will share data with. An **origin** is written `scheme://host:port`, with the port left off when it is the scheme's default (80 for `http`, 443 for `https`).

Write `origin_of(url)` that returns the origin of a URL as the string `"scheme://host:port"`, always including the port (fill in the default when absent), with the host lower-cased. Use `urllib.parse.urlsplit`.

Then write `is_allowed(url, allowlist)` that returns `True` if the URL's origin, as `origin_of` produces it, is in the set or list `allowlist`.

For example, `origin_of("https://A.Example/path")` is `"https://a.example:443"`, and `is_allowed("https://a.example/x", ["https://a.example:443"])` is `True`.
--- starter
def origin_of(url):
    return url


def is_allowed(url, allowlist):
    return False
--- solution
from urllib.parse import urlsplit

DEFAULT = {"http": 80, "https": 443}


def origin_of(url):
    p = urlsplit(url)
    port = p.port if p.port is not None else DEFAULT.get(p.scheme)
    return p.scheme + "://" + p.hostname + ":" + str(port)


def is_allowed(url, allowlist):
    return origin_of(url) in allowlist
--- hint
Split the URL, take the scheme, host and port, filling in 80 or 443 when the port is missing, and build the string `scheme://host:port`.
--- hint
`is_allowed` just checks whether `origin_of(url)` is a member of `allowlist`.
--- check case | Default port is filled in and host lower-cased
origin_of("https://A.Example/path")
=> "https://a.example:443"
--- check case | An explicit non-default port is kept
origin_of("http://a.example:8080/x")
=> "http://a.example:8080"
--- check case | An allowed origin
is_allowed("https://a.example/x", ["https://a.example:443"])
=> True
--- check case | A different port is not allowed
is_allowed("https://a.example:8443/x", ["https://a.example:443"])
=> False
--- check case | http and https are different origins
is_allowed("http://a.example/", ["https://a.example:443"])
=> False

+++ practice | Debug: the escaper with the wrong order
--- task
`escape(s)` should replace `&` with `&amp;`, `<` with `&lt;` and `>` with `&gt;`, so that HTML-special characters become entities exactly once. The version below escapes `<` and `>` **before** `&`, so the `&` it introduces (in `&lt;` and `&gt;`) gets escaped again, producing `&amp;lt;` and `&amp;gt;`, which show up as visible text on the page.

Find and fix the bug. For example, `escape("<b>")` should be `"&lt;b&gt;"`, but the buggy version gives `"&amp;lt;b&amp;gt;"`.
--- starter
def escape(s):
    s = s.replace("<", "&lt;")
    s = s.replace(">", "&gt;")
    s = s.replace("&", "&amp;")
    return s
--- solution
def escape(s):
    s = s.replace("&", "&amp;")
    s = s.replace("<", "&lt;")
    s = s.replace(">", "&gt;")
    return s
--- hint
Each entity the escaper writes starts with an `&`. If you escape `&` last, you escape those new ampersands too.
--- hint
Move the `&` replacement to the front, before `<` and `>`.
--- check case | The example from the task
escape("<b>")
=> "&lt;b&gt;"
--- check case | A lone ampersand is escaped once
escape("Tom & Jerry")
=> "Tom &amp; Jerry"
--- check case | Mixed characters
escape("a<b>&c")
=> "a&lt;b&gt;&amp;c"
--- check case | No special characters
escape("plain text")
=> "plain text"
--- check test | No entity is ever double-escaped
"&amp;lt;" not in escape("<<>>&&") and "&amp;gt;" not in escape("<<>>&&")

+++ practice | CSRF: is this request allowed?
--- task
A state-changing request must prove it came from your own page, not just that the user is logged in. Model the server's check.

Write `request_allowed(method, cookie_token, form_token)`:

- If `method` is `"GET"` or `"HEAD"` (a read-only, "safe" method), allow it: return `True`. These do not change state, so they need no token.
- Otherwise (a `"POST"`, `"PUT"`, `"DELETE"`, and so on), allow it only if `form_token` is a non-empty string and equals `cookie_token`, compared in **constant time** with `hmac.compare_digest`. Return `True` or `False`.

A missing token is `None`; a `None` or empty token on a state-changing request must be rejected.

For example, `request_allowed("GET", None, None)` is `True`; `request_allowed("POST", "abc123", "abc123")` is `True`; and `request_allowed("POST", "abc123", "wrong")` is `False`.
--- starter
def request_allowed(method, cookie_token, form_token):
    return True
--- solution
import hmac


def request_allowed(method, cookie_token, form_token):
    if method in ("GET", "HEAD"):
        return True
    if not cookie_token or not form_token:
        return False
    return hmac.compare_digest(str(cookie_token), str(form_token))
--- hint
Safe methods (`GET`, `HEAD`) return `True` at once. For the rest, first reject if either token is missing or empty.
--- hint
Compare the two tokens with `hmac.compare_digest`, which takes the same time whether they match early or late, so timing cannot leak the token.
--- check case | Safe methods need no token
(request_allowed("GET", None, None), request_allowed("HEAD", "x", None))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | Matching tokens allow a POST
request_allowed("POST", "abc123", "abc123")
=> True
--- check case | A wrong token is rejected
request_allowed("POST", "abc123", "wrong")
=> False
--- check case | Missing tokens on a state-changing request are rejected
(request_allowed("POST", "abc", None), request_allowed("DELETE", None, "abc"), request_allowed("PUT", "", ""))
=> (False, False, False)
--- check case | A forged cross-site POST has no token and is blocked
request_allowed("POST", "server-secret-token", None)
=> False

=== sec-06 | Hashing: cryptographic hash functions and integrity
--- teach
The last five lessons defended against attacks that corrupt memory or inject commands. This lesson starts the cryptography half of the course with the workhorse it is all built on: the **cryptographic hash function**. You met hashing for hash tables in DSA, where the goal was speed and an even spread. Cryptographic hashing has a different, harder goal: to be a fingerprint that no one can forge. Everything after this, passwords, signatures, protocols, uses it.

### What a hash function is

A **hash function** takes an input of any size and returns a fixed-size output, called the **hash**, **digest** or **fingerprint**. **SHA-256** takes any bytes and returns 256 bits, 32 bytes, usually written as 64 hexadecimal characters. In Python:

```python
import hashlib
print(hashlib.sha256(b"abc").hexdigest())
# ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad
```

Note the `b`: hash functions work on **bytes**, not text. Text must be encoded first, almost always as UTF-8: `"café".encode("utf-8")`. The same input always gives the same digest; that is what makes a digest usable as a fingerprint.

### The properties that make it cryptographic

Any function from big inputs to small outputs is a hash. A **cryptographic** hash must also resist a determined attacker. Three properties, in plain terms:

1. **Preimage resistance (one-wayness).** Given a digest `h`, it is infeasible to find any input `m` with `hash(m) = h`. You cannot run the hash backwards. This is why a digest can be published without revealing its input.
2. **Second-preimage resistance.** Given an input `m1`, it is infeasible to find a *different* input `m2` with `hash(m2) = hash(m1)`. You cannot take a known document and craft a different one with the same fingerprint.
3. **Collision resistance.** It is infeasible to find *any* two different inputs `m1 ≠ m2` with `hash(m1) = hash(m2)`. Note this is stronger: the attacker gets to choose **both** inputs.

"Infeasible" means: no method meaningfully faster than brute force, and brute force takes so long that no computer that could exist would finish. For SHA-256, brute force for a preimage is about 2^256 tries, and for a collision about 2^128 (the [[birthday bound|birthday]], below).

A fourth, informal but vital property:

4. **The avalanche effect.** Changing the input even by a single bit changes about **half** of the output bits, unpredictably. There is no smooth relationship between input and output; a tiny change scrambles everything. This is what makes the digest a sensitive detector of any change at all.

```python
import hashlib
a = hashlib.sha256(b"orbit").hexdigest()
b = hashlib.sha256(b"Orbit").hexdigest()   # one bit different in one letter
print(a[:12])   # 4fa1a13ac468
print(b[:12])   # 29ac7e56b3b4  -- completely different
```

### Collisions must exist; finding them must not

By the [[pigeonhole principle|pigeonhole]] from Discrete Mathematics, collisions **have** to exist: there are infinitely many possible inputs and only 2^256 possible digests, so many inputs share each digest. Collision resistance does not claim there are none. It claims no one can *find* one. A hash function is "broken" when someone finds a way to produce collisions much faster than brute force. This happened to **MD5** (collisions are now trivial) and to **SHA-1** (a real collision was published in 2017), which is why both are banned for security use and SHA-256 (and SHA-3) are the standards today.

### The birthday bound: why collision resistance is "only" half the bits

Finding a preimage of an n-bit hash takes about 2^n tries. Finding a collision takes only about 2^(n/2), far fewer. The reason is the **birthday paradox**: in a room of just 23 people, two probably share a birthday, because there are 23×22/2 ≈ 253 *pairs*, and each pair is a chance to match. Likewise, after hashing about 2^(n/2) random inputs, you have about 2^n pairs, enough that two probably collide. So SHA-256's 256 bits give 128-bit collision resistance, and a hash needs twice as many bits against collision attacks as against preimage attacks. This is why modern hashes are 256 bits, not 128.

### Integrity: the main everyday use

The plainest use of a hash is an **integrity check**: proving that data has not changed. You publish the digest of a file through a trusted channel; anyone can download the file from anywhere, hash it themselves, and compare. If the digests match, the file is intact (assuming the published digest is authentic); if a single byte changed, the avalanche effect guarantees the digests differ.

```python
import hashlib
def digest(data):
    return hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest()
downloaded = b"firmware v2.1 ..."
print(digest(downloaded) == "expected value here")
```

When you compare a computed digest against an expected one that a user or attacker could influence the *timing* of, compare in **constant time** with `hmac.compare_digest` (lesson 5's note), so the comparison does not leak how many leading characters matched. For a plain "did my download corrupt" check this matters less; for anything security-sensitive, always use it.

One caution: a bare hash proves integrity **only if the published digest itself is authentic**. An attacker who can change the file can often change a digest published next to it. To bind a digest to a secret (so only someone with the key could have produced it) you need a **[[keyed hash, an HMAC|hmac-preview]]**, which is the next lessons' tool. A plain hash detects accidental corruption and detects tampering only when the reference digest is out of the attacker's reach.

### Merkle trees: integrity for big or streaming data

To verify one item out of millions without hashing all of them, hashes are arranged in a **Merkle tree** (a **hash tree**). The leaves are the hashes of the data blocks. Each internal node is the hash of its two children joined together. The single hash at the top, the **Merkle root**, fingerprints the entire dataset: change any block and the root changes.

Its power is the **proof of inclusion**: to convince someone that a particular block is in a dataset whose root they trust, you give them the block and the handful of sibling hashes along the path from the leaf to the root, about log₂ n of them. They recompute the root from those and check it matches. Git names every commit and file by its hash this way; Bitcoin puts the Merkle root of its transactions in each block header; certificate transparency logs and backup systems use them to verify huge datasets cheaply.

You will build a Merkle root in the practice. The rule when a level has an odd number of nodes: duplicate the last node so it can be paired (this is the common convention Bitcoin uses).

### Where it is used

- **Downloads and packages:** Linux distributions, container images and app stores publish SHA-256 digests so you can verify what you fetched.
- **Version control:** Git identifies every object by its hash, which is what makes a repository tamper-evident.
- **Deduplication and caching:** storing data under its hash means identical data is stored once and found instantly.
- **Passwords, signatures, blockchains, TLS:** all build on a hash you can trust, which is the rest of this course.

**Watch out:** three classic errors. First, using a broken hash (MD5 or SHA-1) for security because it is familiar or faster; use SHA-256 or SHA-3. Second, hashing a **password** with a plain fast hash: a fast hash is exactly wrong for passwords, because it lets an attacker try billions of guesses a second, which the next lesson fixes with slow, salted hashes. Third, trusting a hash for integrity when the reference digest is not itself protected; a plain hash by an untrusted channel proves nothing, and you need a keyed hash or a signature.

::: context birthday The birthday bound in one line
The birthday paradox is the surprising fact that you need only about the square root of the number of possibilities to get a likely collision, because it is pairs that matter and there are roughly k²/2 pairs among k items. For hashes this means an n-bit digest gives only n/2 bits of collision resistance: about 2^(n/2) hashes make a collision probable. It is why SHA-256 targets 128-bit collision security and why a 128-bit hash would be dangerously weak against collision attacks even though 2^128 preimage tries is out of reach. Whenever a design's security rests on no two things ever hashing alike, it is the birthday bound, not the full output size, that sets the safety margin.
:::

::: context pigeonhole Why collisions are guaranteed
The pigeonhole principle says that if you put more items into boxes than there are boxes, some box holds at least two items. A hash maps infinitely many possible inputs into a finite set of digests (2^256 of them for SHA-256), so infinitely many inputs must share each digest: collisions certainly exist. This is not a weakness. Collision resistance is not the claim that there are no collisions, which would be impossible, but the claim that no one can find one faster than the birthday bound allows. The distinction between "exists" and "can be found" is the heart of a great deal of cryptography.
:::

::: context hmac-preview A hash with a key
A plain hash is public: anyone can compute it, so anyone who changes the data can also recompute a matching digest. An HMAC (hash-based message authentication code) mixes a secret key into the hashing so that only someone holding the key can produce the correct code, and anyone can be given the key to verify it. It turns "this data is unchanged" into "this data is unchanged and was vouched for by someone who knows the key", which is integrity plus authenticity. Python offers it as `hmac.new(key, msg, hashlib.sha256)`. You will use HMACs for password verification and for protocol messages in the coming lessons; here, just know that a keyed hash is what you reach for when the reference digest is within an attacker's reach.
:::
--- task
Use `hashlib` and `hmac` from the standard library.

- `sha256_hex(data)`: return the SHA-256 digest of the `bytes` object `data`, as a 64-character lower-case hex string.
- `verify(data, expected_hex)`: return `True` if the SHA-256 hex digest of `data` equals `expected_hex`, compared in constant time with `hmac.compare_digest`, and `False` otherwise.
- `avalanche_bits(hex_a, hex_b)`: given two hex digests of the same length, return the number of **bits** that differ between them. Convert each hex string to an integer, exclusive-or them, and count the 1 bits. (This measures the avalanche effect: for a good hash, two digests of nearly identical inputs differ in about half their bits.)

For example, `sha256_hex(b"abc")` starts `"ba7816bf..."`; `verify(b"abc", sha256_hex(b"abc"))` is `True`; and `avalanche_bits("f0", "0f")` is `8` (every bit differs).
--- starter
def sha256_hex(data):
    raise NotImplementedError


def verify(data, expected_hex):
    raise NotImplementedError


def avalanche_bits(hex_a, hex_b):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def sha256_hex(data):
    return hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest()


def verify(data, expected_hex):
    return hmac.compare_digest(sha256_hex(data), expected_hex)


def avalanche_bits(hex_a, hex_b):
    x = int(hex_a, 16) ^ int(hex_b, 16)
    return bin(x).count("1")
--- hint
`sha256_hex` is `hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest()`. `verify` computes that and compares with `hmac.compare_digest(computed, expected_hex)`.
--- hint
For `avalanche_bits`, turn each hex string into an integer with `int(hex_string, 16)`, exclusive-or them with `^`, then count the set bits with `bin(x).count("1")`.
--- check case | The empty input has a known digest
sha256_hex(b"")
=> "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855"
--- check case | The digest of abc
sha256_hex(b"abc")
=> "ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad"
--- check case | A known test vector
sha256_hex(b"The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog")
=> "d7a8fbb307d7809469ca9abcb0082e4f8d5651e46d3cdb762d02d0bf37c9e592"
--- check case | Verify accepts the right digest and rejects a wrong one
(verify(b"hello", "2cf24dba5fb0a30e26e83b2ac5b9e29e1b161e5c1fa7425e73043362938b9824"), verify(b"hello", "0" * 64))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | A single changed byte changes the digest completely (avalanche)
avalanche_bits(sha256_hex(b"orbit"), sha256_hex(b"Orbit"))
=> 133
--- check case | Counting differing bits directly
(avalanche_bits("f0", "0f"), avalanche_bits("ff", "ff"), avalanche_bits("00", "ff"))
=> (8, 0, 8)
--- check case | Verify is exact: adding a full stop breaks it
verify(b"The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.", "d7a8fbb307d7809469ca9abcb0082e4f8d5651e46d3cdb762d02d0bf37c9e592")
=> False
--- check test | Text must be encoded to bytes the same way both times
verify("mission-alpha".encode("utf-8"), sha256_hex(b"mission-alpha"))
--- check test | The digest is always 64 lower-case hex characters
all(len(sha256_hex(bytes([n]))) == 64 and all(c in "0123456789abcdef" for c in sha256_hex(bytes([n]))) for n in range(0, 256, 17))
--- check test | Avalanche between two different single characters is close to half of 256 bits
100 <= avalanche_bits(sha256_hex(b"a"), sha256_hex(b"b")) <= 156

+++ practice | Hash text
--- task
Write `hash_text(text)` that returns the SHA-256 hex digest of a **string**, encoding it as UTF-8 first. The digest is 64 lower-case hex characters.

For example, `hash_text("abc")` is `"ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad"`, and `hash_text("")` is the empty-input digest `"e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855"`.
--- starter
def hash_text(text):
    return text
--- solution
import hashlib


def hash_text(text):
    return hashlib.sha256(text.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
--- hint
A hash works on bytes, so call `text.encode("utf-8")` first.
--- hint
Then `hashlib.sha256(...).hexdigest()` gives the 64-character hex string.
--- check case | The digest of abc
hash_text("abc")
=> "ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad"
--- check case | The empty string
hash_text("")
=> "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855"
--- check case | A word
hash_text("orbit")
=> "4fa1a13ac468ac495f3390e859d76d5e8ef49806815b45a21de7711bcc624194"
--- check test | The output is always 64 hex characters
all(len(hash_text(s)) == 64 for s in ["", "a", "a longer piece of text", "café"])
--- check test | Encoding is UTF-8: matches hashing the UTF-8 bytes
hash_text("café") == __import__("hashlib").sha256("café".encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

+++ practice | Hashing a file in chunks
--- task
A large file is read in pieces, not all at once. A hash object supports this: create it, `update` it with each chunk in order, then read the digest. The result is the same as hashing the whole content at once.

Write `hash_chunks(chunks)` that takes a list of `bytes` chunks and returns the SHA-256 hex digest of their concatenation, by creating one `hashlib.sha256()` object and calling `.update(chunk)` for each chunk in order.

For example, `hash_chunks([b"foo", b"bar", b"baz"])` equals the digest of `b"foobarbaz"`, which is `"97df3588b5a3f24babc3851b372f0ba71a9dcdded43b14b9d06961bfc1707d9d"`. An empty list gives the empty-input digest.
--- starter
def hash_chunks(chunks):
    return ""
--- solution
import hashlib


def hash_chunks(chunks):
    h = hashlib.sha256()
    for chunk in chunks:
        h.update(chunk)
    return h.hexdigest()
--- hint
Make one hash object with `hashlib.sha256()`, then loop over the chunks calling `h.update(chunk)`.
--- hint
After the loop, return `h.hexdigest()`. Feeding the chunks in order gives the same digest as hashing the joined bytes.
--- check case | Chunks match the whole
hash_chunks([b"foo", b"bar", b"baz"])
=> "97df3588b5a3f24babc3851b372f0ba71a9dcdded43b14b9d06961bfc1707d9d"
--- check case | An empty list is the empty-input digest
hash_chunks([])
=> "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855"
--- check case | One chunk equals a plain hash
hash_chunks([b"abc"])
=> "ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad"
--- check test | Any chunking of the same data gives the same digest
(lambda whole: all(hash_chunks(parts) == __import__("hashlib").sha256(whole).hexdigest() for parts in [[whole], [whole[:2], whole[2:]], [whole[i:i+1] for i in range(len(whole))]]))(b"integrity")
--- check test | Empty chunks in the middle change nothing
hash_chunks([b"", b"ab", b"", b"c", b""]) == __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"abc").hexdigest()

+++ practice | Find the tampered files
--- task
A **manifest** records the expected SHA-256 hex digest of each file. You are given `manifest`, a dict from file name to expected hex digest, and `contents`, a dict from file name to the file's current `bytes`.

Write `tampered(manifest, contents)` that returns a sorted list of the names of files whose current content does **not** match the manifest. A name that is in the manifest but missing from `contents` counts as tampered (the file is gone). Ignore any name in `contents` that is not in the manifest.

For example, if the manifest lists `boot.bin` and `map.dat` and `boot.bin`'s content no longer matches, `tampered` returns `["boot.bin"]`.
--- starter
def tampered(manifest, contents):
    return []
--- solution
import hashlib


def tampered(manifest, contents):
    bad = []
    for name, expected in manifest.items():
        data = contents.get(name)
        if data is None or hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest() != expected:
            bad.append(name)
    return sorted(bad)
--- hint
Loop over the manifest's names. For each, look up the content; if it is missing, or its SHA-256 hex digest does not equal the expected one, it is tampered.
--- hint
Collect the tampered names and return them `sorted`. Do not look at names that are only in `contents`.
--- check case | One file changed
tampered({"boot.bin": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"good boot").hexdigest(), "map.dat": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"map").hexdigest()}, {"boot.bin": b"EVIL boot", "map.dat": b"map"})
=> ["boot.bin"]
--- check case | Nothing changed
tampered({"a": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"a").hexdigest()}, {"a": b"a"})
=> []
--- check case | A missing file counts as tampered
tampered({"a": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"a").hexdigest(), "b": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"b").hexdigest()}, {"a": b"a"})
=> ["b"]
--- check case | Extra files in contents are ignored
tampered({"a": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"a").hexdigest()}, {"a": b"a", "z": b"whatever"})
=> []
--- check case | Several tampered, returned sorted
tampered({"c": "0" * 64, "a": "0" * 64, "b": __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"b").hexdigest()}, {"a": b"x", "b": b"b", "c": b"y"})
=> ["a", "c"]

+++ practice | Debug: comparing a digest to the wrong thing
--- task
`is_valid(data, expected_hex)` should return `True` when the SHA-256 hex digest of `data` equals the expected hex string. The version below compares the raw digest **bytes** (from `.digest()`) to the expected **hex string**, so it is never equal and always returns `False`.

Find and fix the bug: compare like with like. Use `.hexdigest()` so both sides are hex strings.
--- starter
import hashlib


def is_valid(data, expected_hex):
    return hashlib.sha256(data).digest() == expected_hex
--- solution
import hashlib


def is_valid(data, expected_hex):
    return hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest() == expected_hex
--- hint
`.digest()` returns raw bytes; `expected_hex` is a hex string. Those are never equal. Which method returns a hex string?
--- hint
Use `.hexdigest()` so both sides are 64-character hex strings.
--- check case | A matching digest
is_valid(b"abc", "ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad")
=> True
--- check case | A non-matching digest
is_valid(b"abc", "0" * 64)
=> False
--- check case | The empty input
is_valid(b"", "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855")
=> True
--- check test | Agrees with hexdigest for many inputs
all(is_valid(bytes([n]), __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes([n])).hexdigest()) for n in range(0, 256, 9))

+++ practice | A Merkle root
--- task
Build a Merkle root over a list of `bytes` leaves, using SHA-256 on raw digest bytes.

Write `merkle_root(leaves)` that returns the root as a hex string:

1. If `leaves` is empty, return the SHA-256 hex digest of the empty input.
2. Otherwise start with the level `[sha256(leaf).digest() for leaf in leaves]` (raw 32-byte digests).
3. While the level has more than one node: if it has an odd number of nodes, duplicate the last one so the count is even; then form the next level by hashing each pair's two digests **joined together**, `sha256(left + right).digest()`.
4. When one node remains, return it as a hex string with `.hex()`.

For example, `merkle_root([b"a", b"b"])` is `sha256(sha256(b"a").digest() + sha256(b"b").digest()).hexdigest()`, which is `"e5a01fee14e0ed5c48714f22180f25ad8365b53f9779f79dc4a3d7e93963f94a"`. A single leaf's root is just that leaf's own digest.
--- starter
def merkle_root(leaves):
    return ""
--- solution
import hashlib


def merkle_root(leaves):
    if not leaves:
        return hashlib.sha256(b"").hexdigest()
    level = [hashlib.sha256(leaf).digest() for leaf in leaves]
    while len(level) > 1:
        if len(level) % 2 == 1:
            level.append(level[-1])
        level = [hashlib.sha256(level[i] + level[i + 1]).digest() for i in range(0, len(level), 2)]
    return level[0].hex()
--- hint
Work with raw digest bytes (`.digest()`) all the way up, and only convert to hex with `.hex()` at the very end. Joining two digests is just `left + right` on bytes.
--- hint
Each round: if the number of nodes is odd, append a copy of the last node, then pair them up with `range(0, len(level), 2)` and hash `level[i] + level[i+1]`.
--- check case | A single leaf's root is its own digest
merkle_root([b"a"])
=> "ca978112ca1bbdcafac231b39a23dc4da786eff8147c4e72b9807785afee48bb"
--- check case | Two leaves
merkle_root([b"a", b"b"])
=> "e5a01fee14e0ed5c48714f22180f25ad8365b53f9779f79dc4a3d7e93963f94a"
--- check case | Three leaves: the last is duplicated to make a pair
merkle_root([b"a", b"b", b"c"])
=> "d31a37ef6ac14a2db1470c4316beb5592e6afd4465022339adafda76a18ffabe"
--- check case | Four leaves
merkle_root([b"tx1", b"tx2", b"tx3", b"tx4"])
=> "ea59a369466be42d1a4783f09ae0721a5a157d6dba9c4b053d407b5a4b9af145"
--- check case | Empty input
merkle_root([])
=> "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855"
--- check test | Changing any leaf changes the root
merkle_root([b"tx1", b"tx2", b"tx3", b"tx4"]) != merkle_root([b"tx1", b"tx2", b"tx3", b"tx5"])

=== sec-07 | Passwords: salts, slow hashes, and verifying without storing
--- teach
Last lesson you used SHA-256 as a fingerprint: fast, fixed-size, and impossible to run backwards. This lesson is about the one job where a fast hash is exactly the wrong tool: storing passwords. By the end you will build the scheme real systems use, a **salted, deliberately slow** hash called PBKDF2, from nothing but HMAC.

### The threat: the password table leaks

Plan for the day the server is breached. It happens to large companies every year, and the attacker walks away with a copy of the user table. Two things make that dangerous:

- the attacker can now try guesses **offline**, on their own machines, as fast as their hardware allows, with no login page to slow them down or lock them out;
- people reuse passwords, so a password cracked here opens their email and bank elsewhere.

So the goal is precise: **even an attacker who holds the whole table should learn each password only at a very high cost.** "Do not get breached" is not a plan; this is the layer that still works after the others fail.

### Step 1: store a verifier, not the password

The server does not need the password itself. It only needs to *check a guess*. So it stores a **verifier**: a value from which a guess can be checked but the password cannot be read. A hash is the obvious verifier. Store `hash(password)`; at login, hash what the user typed and compare.

```python
import hashlib
stored = hashlib.sha256(b"hunter2").hexdigest()
typed = "hunter2"
print(hashlib.sha256(typed.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest() == stored)   # True
```

Preimage resistance from last lesson means no one can turn `stored` back into `hunter2` by running the hash backwards. That sounds like the end of the story. It is not, because the attacker does not need to run it backwards. They can run it **forwards** on guesses.

### Why a plain hash fails

Three separate problems, each fatal on its own:

1. **Equal passwords give equal hashes.** Everyone whose password is `123456` has the same stored value. One look at the table shows which accounts share a password, and cracking one cracks them all.
2. **Precomputation.** The attacker can hash a list of a billion common passwords **once**, sort the results, and then look up every hash in every leaked table ever, instantly. [[Rainbow tables|rainbow]] are a compressed form of exactly this.
3. **Speed.** SHA-256 was designed to be fast. A single [[graphics card|gpu]] computes around 10¹⁰ SHA-256 hashes a second; MD5 is faster still.

Speed is the killer, so count it. The **guess space** of a password drawn uniformly from an alphabet of size A with length L has N = A^L members. Its **entropy** is log₂ N bits. An attacker trying them in any order needs N/2 guesses on average. For 8 lower-case letters, N = 26⁸ ≈ 2.1 × 10¹¹, which is about 37.6 bits. At 10¹⁰ guesses a second the average crack takes **10 seconds**. And real passwords are nowhere near uniform: people pick words, names and dates, which a dictionary finds far sooner.

This is also why "MD5 is not enough" is the wrong way to put it. MD5's broken collision resistance is not what hurts here. Any general-purpose hash, including SHA-256, is too fast for passwords.

### Step 2: a salt for every user

A **salt** is a random value, different for every user, stored in the clear next to the hash. The verifier becomes `hash(salt + password)`.

```python
import secrets
salt = secrets.token_bytes(16)     # 16 random bytes = 128 bits
print(len(salt), salt.hex())       # 16, then 32 random hex characters
```

Why it works, one problem at a time:

- **Equal passwords no longer match.** Two users with `123456` have different salts, so different stored values.
- **Precomputation dies.** A precomputed table is built for one salt. With 128-bit salts from `secrets`, no two users anywhere are expected to share a salt (by the birthday bound you would need about 2⁶⁴ users before a repeat is likely), so every table would have to be built again for every single user. That is no better than guessing directly.
- **The attacker's work multiplies.** Without salts, one hash of a guess is checked against every row at once. With salts, the guess must be hashed again for each user. Cracking a million users costs a million times as much as cracking one.

The salt is **not a secret**. It must be random and unique, nothing more. It is stored beside the hash because the server needs it to recompute the hash at login.

What a salt does *not* fix is problem 3. An attacker who targets one user still makes 10¹⁰ guesses a second against that user's salt.

### Step 3: make every guess expensive

The fix for speed is to make the hash slow **on purpose**, by a factor you choose. This is called **key stretching**. The standard construction is **PBKDF2** (Password-Based Key Derivation Function 2, RFC 8018). It takes a password P, a salt S, an **iteration count** c and an output length, and uses a keyed hash, the **PRF** (pseudorandom function), which here is HMAC-SHA256 keyed with the password.

The output is made of 32-byte blocks T₁, T₂, … joined and cut to length. Block number i is:

- U₁ = HMAC(P, S ‖ INT(i)), where ‖ means "joined to" and INT(i) is i as 4 bytes, big-endian, counting from 1;
- U₂ = HMAC(P, U₁), U₃ = HMAC(P, U₂), and so on up to U_c: each one is the HMAC of the one before;
- Tᵢ = U₁ ⊕ U₂ ⊕ … ⊕ U_c, where ⊕ is exclusive-or, byte by byte.

You need three small pieces of Python for this. An HMAC, from the `hmac` module, returns 32 bytes with SHA-256:

```python
import hmac, hashlib
tag = hmac.new(b"launch-key", b"telemetry frame 7", hashlib.sha256).digest()
print(len(tag))   # 32
```

Exclusive-or of two equal-length byte strings, byte by byte, with `zip`:

```python
left = bytes([0x0C, 0xFF, 0x10])
right = bytes([0x0A, 0x0F, 0x10])
mixed = bytes(p ^ q for p, q in zip(left, right))
print(mixed.hex())   # 06f000
```

And a whole number as a fixed number of big-endian bytes:

```python
print((258).to_bytes(4, "big").hex())   # 00000102
```

### Why PBKDF2 slows the attacker and not the user

The argument has three steps.

1. **Testing a guess needs the full output.** The stored value is the whole of T₁ (and later blocks). A guess is right only if its output matches, so the attacker must compute it.
2. **The output needs every Uⱼ, in order.** Each Uⱼ is an HMAC of the one before, so U_c cannot be computed without first computing U_{c−1}, and so on back to U₁. HMAC behaves like a random function to anyone who does not already know the answer, so no one knows a shortcut. Because T is the exclusive-or of **all** the Uⱼ, not just the last, skipping any step would change the result.
3. **So each guess costs c HMACs, for everyone.** The honest server pays c HMACs **once per login**. The attacker pays c HMACs **per guess**, for billions of guesses.

Put numbers on it. With c = 600,000 (the 2023 OWASP guidance for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256), one guess is about 1.2 million SHA-256 compressions, since each HMAC does two. A login costs the server a fraction of a second. The graphics card from before drops from 10¹⁰ to about 8,000 guesses a second, and the 8-letter password goes from 10 seconds to about **5 months**. The salt stops shortcuts across users; the iterations tax every single guess.

**Cost:** computing a d-byte key takes c × ⌈d / 32⌉ HMAC calls, which is Θ(c) for a fixed output length, and O(1) extra memory. That linear cost in c is the whole point: c is a dial that trades login time for attacker time.

### Memory-hard functions

PBKDF2 has one weakness: it needs almost no memory, so a graphics card or custom chip runs thousands of guesses side by side cheaply. **[[Memory-hard functions|memoryhard]]** such as **scrypt** and **Argon2id** force each guess to fill and reread a large block of memory, which is expensive to copy thousands of times. Argon2id is today's first choice for new systems, with bcrypt and scrypt still widely used and PBKDF2 used where a standard demands it. Python ships `hashlib.scrypt` and `hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac`; real applications usually call a library such as `argon2-cffi`. You build PBKDF2 yourself here because it shows the whole idea in a few lines.

### Storing it, and checking a login

The stored record holds everything needed to recompute the hash, in one string:

```text
pbkdf2_sha256$600000$9f3c...salt in hex...$1b7a...key in hex...
```

That is the algorithm, the iteration count, the salt and the derived key, separated by `$`. This is the [[format Django uses|django]]. Keeping the algorithm and count in the record lets you raise the count later without breaking old accounts.

To **verify** a login:

1. split the record into its four fields;
2. recompute PBKDF2 of the typed password with the stored salt and iteration count;
3. compare the result to the stored key with `hmac.compare_digest`.

The password itself is never stored, and never needs to be. That is "verifying without storing".

Use `hmac.compare_digest`, not `==`. A plain comparison stops at the first differing byte, so how long it takes leaks [[how many leading bytes matched|timing]]. Over many tries that lets an attacker build up a secret one byte at a time. `compare_digest` takes the same time whatever the inputs.

You can only rehash with a higher count when you have the plain password, which is at login. So servers **upgrade on login**: after a successful check, if the record uses an old algorithm or too few iterations, they compute a new record and save it.

### Where it is used

- **Django**, the Python web framework, stores exactly `pbkdf2_sha256$iterations$salt$hash` by default.
- **Linux** keeps salted, slow hashes in `/etc/shadow` (`$6$` is SHA-512-crypt, `$y$` is yescrypt).
- **[[WPA2 Wi-Fi|wpa2]]** turns your Wi-Fi password into its key with PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA1, 4,096 iterations, using the network name as the salt.
- **Disk encryption** (LUKS, FileVault, BitLocker) derives the disk key from your passphrase with a slow function.
- **ORBIT's ground station** would store operator logins this way, so a stolen database does not hand an attacker the command console.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- **One salt for everyone** (a "site-wide salt"). Equal passwords match again and a single table cracks the whole site. The salt must be random and different for every user.
- **Too few iterations**, or a count chosen years ago and never raised. Hardware gets faster; raise the count and upgrade on login.
- **Comparing with `==`.** Use `hmac.compare_digest`.
- **Encrypting passwords instead of hashing them.** Encryption can be reversed by whoever gets the key, and keys leak with the database. A verifier must be one-way.
- **Using `random` for salts.** The `random` module is predictable; use `secrets`.

::: context rainbow Rainbow tables
A rainbow table is a clever way to store precomputed hashes of a huge list of passwords in far less space than a plain list, by storing only the start and end of long chains of hash-then-reduce steps and recomputing the middle when needed. Tables for unsalted MD5 and SHA-1 covering every short password are freely downloadable. The only thing that matters for defence is that every such table is built for one fixed hash function with no salt. A random per-user salt means a table would have to be rebuilt for each user, which makes precomputation pointless.
:::

::: context gpu Why graphics cards crack passwords
A graphics card has thousands of small cores built to do the same arithmetic on many pieces of data at once, which is exactly what trying many password guesses is. A single modern card computes tens of billions of MD5 hashes a second and around ten billion SHA-256 hashes a second; rented cloud machines hold eight of them. Password-cracking tools such as hashcat are built for this. Any defence has to assume the attacker has this hardware, which is why the cost per guess, not the hash's one-wayness, decides how long a password survives.
:::

::: context memoryhard Memory as a cost
The cost of an attack is really money: chips, power and time. A PBKDF2 guess needs only a few hundred bytes of memory, so a custom chip can pack thousands of guessing units side by side. A memory-hard function such as scrypt or Argon2id makes each guess fill, say, 64 MB of memory and read it back in an unpredictable order. Memory is expensive in silicon, so running a thousand guesses in parallel now needs 64 GB, and the attacker's advantage from custom hardware shrinks sharply. Argon2 won the Password Hashing Competition in 2015 for this reason.
:::

::: context django The format real frameworks use
Django, a popular Python web framework, stores a password as `algorithm$iterations$salt$hash`, and its default is PBKDF2 with SHA-256. Each Django release raises the default iteration count. When a user logs in and their record uses a lower count, Django recomputes the hash with the new count and saves it, so the whole user base upgrades over time without anyone resetting a password. Passlib and many other libraries use similar self-describing strings, which is why the algorithm name is always stored alongside the hash.
:::

::: context timing Timing leaks
Comparing two strings with `==` usually checks them one character at a time and stops at the first difference. So a guess whose first byte is right takes a few nanoseconds longer to reject than one whose first byte is wrong. An attacker who can measure that difference, even through network noise by averaging many requests, can find the first byte, then the second, and so on, needing about 256 tries per byte instead of 256 to the power of the length. `hmac.compare_digest` always examines every byte, so its running time says nothing about where the inputs differ.
:::

::: context wpa2 Wi-Fi passwords and PBKDF2
When you join a WPA2 home network, your device and the router both turn the Wi-Fi password into a 256-bit key with PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA1, 4,096 iterations, using the network's name (the SSID) as the salt. Using the name as the salt means a precomputed table has to be built per network name, which is why tables exist only for very common names such as "linksys". Attackers who capture a handshake can still guess offline, so a long, uncommon Wi-Fi password matters. WPA3 replaced this with a protocol that does not allow offline guessing at all.
:::
--- task
Build password storage from `hmac` and `hashlib.sha256`. Do not use `hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac`: the point is to build it.

- `pbkdf2_sha256(password, salt, iterations, dklen)`: `password` and `salt` are `bytes`; `iterations` and `dklen` are whole numbers, at least 1. Return the first `dklen` bytes of PBKDF2 with HMAC-SHA256, exactly as the lesson defines it: blocks numbered from 1, the block number as 4 big-endian bytes after the salt, and each block the exclusive-or of all `iterations` values U₁ to U_c. If `dklen` is more than 32, you need more than one block.
- `hash_password(password, salt, iterations)`: `password` is a `str`, encoded as UTF-8. Return the record `"pbkdf2_sha256$<iterations>$<salt as lower-case hex>$<32-byte key as lower-case hex>"`.
- `verify_password(password, stored)`: `stored` is a record made by `hash_password`. Recompute the key from its salt and iteration count and return `True` if it matches, compared with `hmac.compare_digest`, and `False` otherwise.

For example, `pbkdf2_sha256(b"password", b"salt", 1, 20).hex()` is `"120fb6cffcf8b32c43e7225256c4f837a86548c9"`, and `verify_password("pw", hash_password("pw", b"ab", 3))` is `True`.
--- starter
def pbkdf2_sha256(password, salt, iterations, dklen):
    raise NotImplementedError


def hash_password(password, salt, iterations):
    raise NotImplementedError


def verify_password(password, stored):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2_sha256(password, salt, iterations, dklen):
    out = b""
    block = 1
    while len(out) < dklen:
        u = hmac.new(password, salt + block.to_bytes(4, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t = u
        for _ in range(iterations - 1):
            u = hmac.new(password, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
            t = bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(t, u))
        out += t
        block += 1
    return out[:dklen]


def hash_password(password, salt, iterations):
    key = pbkdf2_sha256(password.encode("utf-8"), salt, iterations, 32)
    return "pbkdf2_sha256$%d$%s$%s" % (iterations, salt.hex(), key.hex())


def verify_password(password, stored):
    _, iterations, salt_hex, key_hex = stored.split("$")
    key = pbkdf2_sha256(password.encode("utf-8"), bytes.fromhex(salt_hex), int(iterations), 32)
    return hmac.compare_digest(key.hex(), key_hex)
--- hint
Build one block at a time while the output is shorter than `dklen`. For block number `i` (starting at 1), U₁ is `hmac.new(password, salt + i.to_bytes(4, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()`.
--- hint
Keep a running `t` that starts as U₁. Then repeat `iterations - 1` times: compute the next U as the HMAC of the previous U, and exclusive-or it into `t` byte by byte. Append `t` to the output, move to the next block, and at the end cut the output to `dklen`.
--- hint
`hash_password` formats `iterations`, `salt.hex()` and the 32-byte key's `.hex()` with `$` between them. `verify_password` splits on `$`, turns the salt back into bytes with `bytes.fromhex`, recomputes, and compares with `hmac.compare_digest`.
--- check case | One iteration, 20 bytes (a published test vector)
pbkdf2_sha256(b"password", b"salt", 1, 20).hex()
=> "120fb6cffcf8b32c43e7225256c4f837a86548c9"
--- check case | 4,096 iterations (a published test vector)

=> "c5e478d59288c841aa530db6845c4c8d962893a001ce4e11a4963873aa98134a"
--- check case | 64 bytes needs two blocks (RFC 7914 test vector)

?? The second block uses block number 2 as INT(i), then the output is cut to 64 bytes.
--- check case | 40 bytes: the second block is cut short, and the password may be empty

=> "62384466264daadc4144018c6bd864648272b34da8980d31521ffcce92ae003bbf34cc19ec05e240"
--- check case | A single byte, with an empty salt

=> "72"
--- check test | Each extra iteration changes the key

?? Every iteration's U must be folded into the running exclusive-or.

=> "pbkdf2_sha256$1000$000102030405060708090a0b0c0d0e0f$c914cc4f06cc6e8f46d157e3a1b5aa7abceebb17bb0444cd4c4ac16ca2ae9864"

=> "pbkdf2_sha256$10$4e61436c4e61436c4e61436c4e61436c$91797c44c536907298e3f49e023bebee37566f7ea9112f8e70cd568478ea4765"
--- check test | The same password with two salts gives two different records
hash_password("123456", b"salt-one", 5).split("$")[3] != hash_password("123456", b"salt-two", 5).split("$")[3]
--- check case | verify_password accepts the right password and rejects others
(verify_password("pw", hash_password("pw", b"ab", 3)), verify_password("pW", hash_password("pw", b"ab", 3)), verify_password("", hash_password("pw", b"ab", 3)))
=> (True, False, False)
--- check test | verify_password uses the record's own salt and iteration count
verify_password("password", "pbkdf2_sha256$4096$73616c74$c5e478d59288c841aa530db6845c4c8d962893a001ce4e11a4963873aa98134a")
--- check test | The empty password round-trips
verify_password("", hash_password("", b"\x00" * 16, 1))
--- check source | Compares with hmac.compare_digest
compare_digest
--- check source absent | Builds PBKDF2 instead of calling the library's
