+++ practice | Which accounts share a password?
--- task
A site stored passwords as plain, **unsalted** SHA-256 hex digests, and its table leaked. The table is a dict from user name to hex digest.

Write `shared_passwords(table)` that returns every group of two or more users whose digests are equal, so they must share a password. Each group is a sorted list of user names, and the groups are sorted by their first name. Treat digests case-insensitively: `"AB12..."` and `"ab12..."` are the same digest. Users whose digest is unique appear in no group. An empty table gives `[]`.

For example, if `ada` and `sam` both have the digest of `"123456"` and `lin` has another, the answer is `[["ada", "sam"]]`.
--- starter
def shared_passwords(table):
    return []
--- solution
def shared_passwords(table):
    groups = {}
    for user, digest in table.items():
        groups.setdefault(digest.lower(), []).append(user)
    shared = [sorted(users) for users in groups.values() if len(users) >= 2]
    return sorted(shared)
--- hint
Group the users by digest with a dict from digest to a list of users. Lower-case each digest before you use it as a key.
--- hint
Keep only the lists with at least two users, sort each one, then sort the list of groups (lists compare by their first item first).
--- check test | The example from the task
(lambda h: shared_passwords({"sam": h("123456"), "lin": h("orbit!"), "ada": h("123456")}) == [["ada", "sam"]])(lambda s: __import__("hashlib").sha256(s.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check test | Two groups, sorted by first name
(lambda h: shared_passwords({"zoe": h("x"), "bo": h("y"), "al": h("y"), "cy": h("x"), "dee": h("z")}) == [["al", "bo"], ["cy", "zoe"]])(lambda s: __import__("hashlib").sha256(s.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check test | Upper-case and lower-case digests are the same digest
(lambda h: shared_passwords({"a": h("pw").upper(), "b": h("pw")}) == [["a", "b"]])(lambda s: __import__("hashlib").sha256(s.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check case | Everyone unique
shared_passwords({"a": "01" * 32, "b": "02" * 32})
=> []
--- check case | An empty table
shared_passwords({})
=> []
--- check case | Everyone the same
shared_passwords({"c": "ff" * 32, "a": "ff" * 32, "b": "FF" * 32})
=> [["a", "b", "c"]]

+++ practice | A dictionary attack on salted hashes
--- task
See what a salt costs an attacker. A leaked table is a list of `(user, salt_hex, digest_hex)` tuples, where `digest_hex` is the SHA-256 hex digest of the salt bytes followed by the password's UTF-8 bytes: `sha256(salt + password)`.

Write `crack(records, words)` that tries each word of the list `words`, in order, against each user, and returns a pair `(found, work)`:

- `found` is a dict from every user to the first word that matches, or `None` if no word does;
- `work` is the number of SHA-256 hashes computed. For each user, stop at the first match; a user with no match costs one hash per word.

Because every user has their own salt, a guess has to be hashed again for every user. For example, with two users and three words where the first user's password is the second word and the second user's is not in the list, `work` is 2 + 3 = 5.
--- starter
def crack(records, words):
    return {}, 0
--- solution
import hashlib


def crack(records, words):
    found = {}
    work = 0
    for user, salt_hex, digest_hex in records:
        salt = bytes.fromhex(salt_hex)
        found[user] = None
        for word in words:
            work += 1
            if hashlib.sha256(salt + word.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest() == digest_hex:
                found[user] = word
                break
    return found, work
--- hint
Loop over the users, and inside that over the words. Turn the salt back into bytes with `bytes.fromhex`, then hash `salt + word.encode("utf-8")`.
--- hint
Count one unit of work for every hash you compute, record the word and `break` at the first match, and start each user at `None`.
--- check test | The example from the task
(lambda h: crack([("ada", "0a0b", h("0a0b", "orbit")), ("lin", "ffee", h("ffee", "zebra7"))], ["123456", "orbit", "letmein"]) == ({"ada": "orbit", "lin": None}, 5))(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check test | The same password under two salts must be cracked twice
(lambda h: crack([("a", "01", h("01", "pw")), ("b", "02", h("02", "pw"))], ["x", "y", "pw"]) == ({"a": "pw", "b": "pw"}, 6))(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check test | A match on the first word costs one hash
(lambda h: crack([("a", "aa", h("aa", "123456"))], ["123456", "password"]) == ({"a": "123456"}, 1))(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check case | An empty word list finds nothing and costs nothing
crack([("a", "00", "00" * 32)], [])
=> ({"a": None}, 0)
--- check case | No records
crack([], ["a", "b"])
=> ({}, 0)
--- check test | Non-ASCII passwords are hashed as UTF-8
(lambda h: crack([("a", "c0ffee", h("c0ffee", "élan"))], ["elan", "élan"]) == ({"a": "élan"}, 2))(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest())

+++ practice | How long would it take?
--- task
Put numbers on password strength. A password is drawn uniformly at random: each of its `length` characters is one of `alphabet` possible characters. An attacker computes `rate` hashes per second, and checking one guess costs `iterations` hashes (1 for a plain hash, more for PBKDF2).

Write three functions:

- `guess_space(alphabet, length)`: the exact number of possible passwords, as an integer.
- `entropy_bits(alphabet, length)`: log₂ of the guess space, as a float. Use `math.log2`.
- `expected_days(alphabet, length, rate, iterations)`: the **average** time to crack, in days, as a float. On average the attacker tries half the guess space. There are 86,400 seconds in a day.

For example, `guess_space(10, 4)` is `10000`, `entropy_bits(2, 8)` is `8.0`, and `expected_days(10, 4, 1, 1)` is 5,000 seconds, about `0.0579` days. A length of 0 has a guess space of 1 and 0.0 bits.
--- starter
def guess_space(alphabet, length):
    return 0


def entropy_bits(alphabet, length):
    return 0.0


def expected_days(alphabet, length, rate, iterations):
    return 0.0
--- solution
import math


def guess_space(alphabet, length):
    return alphabet ** length


def entropy_bits(alphabet, length):
    return length * math.log2(alphabet)


def expected_days(alphabet, length, rate, iterations):
    guesses = guess_space(alphabet, length) / 2
    seconds = guesses * iterations / rate
    return seconds / 86400
--- hint
The guess space is `alphabet ** length`. Its log base 2 is `length * math.log2(alphabet)`, which avoids building a huge number first.
--- hint
The average number of guesses is half the space; multiply by `iterations` to get hashes, divide by `rate` for seconds, then by 86,400 for days.
--- check case | Small guess spaces, exactly
(guess_space(10, 4), guess_space(26, 8), guess_space(95, 0))
=> (10000, 208827064576, 1)
--- check test | Entropy of some common shapes
abs(entropy_bits(2, 8) - 8.0) < 1e-9 and abs(entropy_bits(26, 8) - 37.6035) < 1e-3 and abs(entropy_bits(95, 12) - 78.8383) < 1e-3
--- check test | Length 0 has no entropy
entropy_bits(62, 0) == 0.0
--- check test | Four digits at one guess a second
abs(expected_days(10, 4, 1, 1) - 5000 / 86400) < 1e-9
--- check test | Eight lower-case letters against a fast plain hash: minutes, not days
abs(expected_days(26, 8, 10**10, 1) - 0.000120849) < 1e-8
--- check test | The same password behind 600,000 PBKDF2 iterations takes months
abs(expected_days(26, 8, 10**10, 600000) - 72.5094) < 1e-3
--- check test | Exact for a guess space too big for a float to hold every digit
guess_space(95, 20) == 95 ** 20 and guess_space(95, 20) % 95 == 0

+++ practice | Check a stored record, whatever it holds
--- task
A login server reads password records from a database that an attacker might have been able to edit. A broken or hostile record must make the login fail, never crash the server, and never make it spend minutes hashing.

The starter gives you `pbkdf2(password_bytes, salt, iterations)`, which returns a 32-byte PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 key. Write `check_stored(password, stored)` that returns `True` only when all of these hold, and `False` otherwise, without ever raising:

- `stored` has exactly four fields separated by `$`;
- the first field is exactly `pbkdf2_sha256`;
- the second is written with only the digits 0 to 9 and its value is from 1 to 10,000 (a huge count is a denial-of-service attack);
- the third is non-empty, valid hex (upper or lower case), and the fourth is valid hex that decodes to exactly 32 bytes;
- and the key computed from the UTF-8 password, the salt and the count equals the stored key, compared with `hmac.compare_digest`.

For example, a record `"pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$" + key` made from `"pw"` checks `True` for `"pw"`; `"pbkdf2_sha256$0$6162$" + key` is `False` (zero iterations), and so is `"$$$"`.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2(password_bytes, salt, iterations):
    u = hmac.new(password_bytes, salt + b"\x00\x00\x00\x01", hashlib.sha256).digest()
    t = int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    for _ in range(iterations - 1):
        u = hmac.new(password_bytes, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t ^= int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    return t.to_bytes(32, "big")


def check_stored(password, stored):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2(password_bytes, salt, iterations):
    u = hmac.new(password_bytes, salt + b"\x00\x00\x00\x01", hashlib.sha256).digest()
    t = int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    for _ in range(iterations - 1):
        u = hmac.new(password_bytes, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t ^= int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    return t.to_bytes(32, "big")


def check_stored(password, stored):
    fields = stored.split("$")
    if len(fields) != 4:
        return False
    algorithm, count, salt_hex, key_hex = fields
    if algorithm != "pbkdf2_sha256":
        return False
    if not count or any(c not in "0123456789" for c in count):
        return False
    iterations = int(count)
    if iterations < 1 or iterations > 10000:
        return False
    try:
        salt = bytes.fromhex(salt_hex)
        key = bytes.fromhex(key_hex)
    except ValueError:
        return False
    if not salt or len(key) != 32:
        return False
    return hmac.compare_digest(pbkdf2(password.encode("utf-8"), salt, iterations), key)
--- hint
Split on `$` and reject anything that is not four fields. Check the count character by character against `"0123456789"` before calling `int`, because `int` also accepts spaces, signs and underscores.
--- hint
`bytes.fromhex` raises `ValueError` on bad hex, so wrap both decodes in `try`/`except ValueError` and return `False`. Only after every check passes, compute the key and compare with `hmac.compare_digest`.
--- check test | A good record checks, and a wrong password does not
(lambda k: (check_stored("pw", "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$" + k), check_stored("px", "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$" + k)) == (True, False))(pbkdf2(b"pw", b"ab", 3).hex())
--- check test | Upper-case hex is accepted
(lambda k: check_stored("pw", "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$" + k.upper()))(pbkdf2(b"pw", b"ab", 3).hex())
--- check test | Zero, negative, signed, spaced and underscored counts are rejected
(lambda k: [check_stored("pw", "pbkdf2_sha256$" + c + "$6162$" + k) for c in ["0", "-3", "+3", " 3", "3 ", "1_0", "", "3.0"]] == [False] * 8)(pbkdf2(b"pw", b"ab", 3).hex())
--- check test | A huge count is rejected without hashing
check_stored("pw", "pbkdf2_sha256$999999999999$6162$" + "00" * 32) is False
--- check test | Exactly 10,000 is still allowed, 10,001 is not
(check_stored("pw", "pbkdf2_sha256$10000$6162$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"ab", 10000).hex()), check_stored("pw", "pbkdf2_sha256$10001$6162$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"ab", 10001).hex())) == (True, False)
--- check case | Wrong shapes never raise
[check_stored("pw", s) for s in ["", "$$$", "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162", "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$00$00", "md5$3$6162$" + "00" * 32]]
=> [False, False, False, False, False]
--- check case | Bad hex, an empty salt and a short key are rejected
[check_stored("pw", s) for s in ["pbkdf2_sha256$3$zz$" + "00" * 32, "pbkdf2_sha256$3$$" + "00" * 32, "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$" + "00" * 31, "pbkdf2_sha256$3$6162$" + "0" * 63]]
=> [False, False, False, False]

+++ practice | Debug: the block counter that starts at zero
--- task
This PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 function gives the wrong keys: `pbkdf2_sha256(b"password", b"salt", 1, 20).hex()` should be `"120fb6cffcf8b32c43e7225256c4f837a86548c9"`, but it returns something else for every input. The iteration loop is fine. Find the bug in how the blocks are numbered and fix it, so it returns the first `dklen` bytes of PBKDF2 exactly as the lesson defines it.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2_sha256(password, salt, iterations, dklen):
    blocks = (dklen + 31) // 32
    out = b""
    for i in range(blocks):
        u = hmac.new(password, salt + i.to_bytes(4, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t = int.from_bytes(u, "big")
        for _ in range(iterations - 1):
            u = hmac.new(password, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
            t ^= int.from_bytes(u, "big")
        out += t.to_bytes(32, "big")
    return out[:dklen]
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2_sha256(password, salt, iterations, dklen):
    blocks = (dklen + 31) // 32
    out = b""
    for i in range(1, blocks + 1):
        u = hmac.new(password, salt + i.to_bytes(4, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t = int.from_bytes(u, "big")
        for _ in range(iterations - 1):
            u = hmac.new(password, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
            t ^= int.from_bytes(u, "big")
        out += t.to_bytes(32, "big")
    return out[:dklen]
--- hint
Look at what `i` is on the first pass of the outer loop, and compare it with the definition of INT(i).
--- hint
PBKDF2 numbers its blocks from 1. Make the loop run `i` over 1, 2, …, `blocks`.
--- check case | One iteration, 20 bytes
pbkdf2_sha256(b"password", b"salt", 1, 20).hex()
=> "120fb6cffcf8b32c43e7225256c4f837a86548c9"
--- check case | Two iterations, 32 bytes
pbkdf2_sha256(b"password", b"salt", 2, 32).hex()
=> "ae4d0c95af6b46d32d0adff928f06dd02a303f8ef3c251dfd6e2d85a95474c43"
--- check case | Two blocks
pbkdf2_sha256(b"passwd", b"salt", 1, 64).hex()
=> "55ac046e56e3089fec1691c22544b605f94185216dde0465e68b9d57c20dacbc49ca9cccf179b645991664b39d77ef317c71b845b1e30bd509112041d3a19783"
--- check test | Output length is always dklen
[len(pbkdf2_sha256(b"k", b"s", 2, n)) for n in (1, 31, 32, 33, 64, 65)] == [1, 31, 32, 33, 64, 65]

+++ practice | Upgrade old hashes at login
--- task
A ground-station user store holds two kinds of record: old ones, `"sha256$<salt hex>$<hex of sha256(salt + password)>"`, and new ones, `"pbkdf2_sha256$<iterations>$<salt hex>$<key hex>"`. The starter gives you `pbkdf2(password_bytes, salt, iterations)`, which returns the 32-byte key. Passwords are encoded as UTF-8.

Write `login(store, user, password, policy_iterations, new_salt)`:

- If `user` is not in the dict `store`, return `False`.
- Check the password against the user's record, whichever kind it is, with `hmac.compare_digest`. If it is wrong, return `False` and leave the store unchanged.
- If it is right, and the record is an old `sha256` one or a `pbkdf2_sha256` one with fewer than `policy_iterations` iterations, replace `store[user]` with a new `pbkdf2_sha256` record made with `new_salt` and `policy_iterations`. Then return `True`.
- A correct login on a record that already meets the policy (at least `policy_iterations`) leaves the store unchanged.

This is the only moment the upgrade is possible: the server sees the plain password only at login.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2(password_bytes, salt, iterations):
    u = hmac.new(password_bytes, salt + b"\x00\x00\x00\x01", hashlib.sha256).digest()
    t = int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    for _ in range(iterations - 1):
        u = hmac.new(password_bytes, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t ^= int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    return t.to_bytes(32, "big")


def login(store, user, password, policy_iterations, new_salt):
    return False
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def pbkdf2(password_bytes, salt, iterations):
    u = hmac.new(password_bytes, salt + b"\x00\x00\x00\x01", hashlib.sha256).digest()
    t = int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    for _ in range(iterations - 1):
        u = hmac.new(password_bytes, u, hashlib.sha256).digest()
        t ^= int.from_bytes(u, "big")
    return t.to_bytes(32, "big")


def login(store, user, password, policy_iterations, new_salt):
    if user not in store:
        return False
    secret = password.encode("utf-8")
    fields = store[user].split("$")
    if fields[0] == "sha256":
        salt = bytes.fromhex(fields[1])
        ok = hmac.compare_digest(hashlib.sha256(salt + secret).hexdigest(), fields[2])
        needs_upgrade = True
    else:
        iterations = int(fields[1])
        salt = bytes.fromhex(fields[2])
        ok = hmac.compare_digest(pbkdf2(secret, salt, iterations).hex(), fields[3])
        needs_upgrade = iterations < policy_iterations
    if not ok:
        return False
    if needs_upgrade:
        key = pbkdf2(secret, new_salt, policy_iterations)
        store[user] = "pbkdf2_sha256$%d$%s$%s" % (policy_iterations, new_salt.hex(), key.hex())
    return True
--- hint
Split the record on `$` and branch on the first field. An old record has three fields (name, salt, digest); a new one has four.
--- hint
Work out two things before touching the store: whether the password is right, and whether the record needs an upgrade. Only when both are true do you write a new record.
--- check test | An old record upgrades on a correct login
(lambda s: (login(s, "ada", "orbit", 50, b"\x01\x02"), s["ada"]) == (True, "pbkdf2_sha256$50$0102$" + pbkdf2(b"orbit", b"\x01\x02", 50).hex()))({"ada": "sha256$aabb$" + __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"\xaa\xbb" + b"orbit").hexdigest()})
--- check test | A wrong password on an old record changes nothing
(lambda s: (login(s, "ada", "0rbit", 50, b"\x01\x02"), s) == (False, {"ada": "sha256$aabb$" + __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"\xaa\xbb" + b"orbit").hexdigest()}))({"ada": "sha256$aabb$" + __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"\xaa\xbb" + b"orbit").hexdigest()})
--- check test | A PBKDF2 record below the policy upgrades
(lambda s: (login(s, "lin", "pw", 20, b"\xff"), s["lin"]) == (True, "pbkdf2_sha256$20$ff$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"\xff", 20).hex()))({"lin": "pbkdf2_sha256$5$0a$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"\x0a", 5).hex()})
--- check test | A record that already meets the policy is left alone
(lambda r: (lambda s: (login(s, "lin", "pw", 20, b"\xff"), s["lin"] == r) == (True, True))({"lin": r}))("pbkdf2_sha256$20$0a$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"\x0a", 20).hex())
--- check test | A record above the policy is not downgraded
(lambda r: (lambda s: (login(s, "lin", "pw", 10, b"\xff"), s["lin"] == r) == (True, True))({"lin": r}))("pbkdf2_sha256$30$0a$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"\x0a", 30).hex())
--- check case | An unknown user fails
login({}, "mallory", "pw", 10, b"\x00")
=> False
--- check test | A wrong password on a PBKDF2 record changes nothing
(lambda r: (lambda s: (login(s, "lin", "PW", 20, b"\xff"), s["lin"] == r) == (False, True))({"lin": r}))("pbkdf2_sha256$5$0a$" + pbkdf2(b"pw", b"\x0a", 5).hex())

+++ practice | Why == leaks: recover a secret by timing
--- task
Model the timing attack that `hmac.compare_digest` prevents. A server checks a secret token with a naive comparison that walks along both strings and stops at the first wrong character, so the time it takes grows with the number of leading characters you got right. You cannot see the secret, but you can call `oracle(guess)`, which returns exactly that number: how many characters at the start of `guess` match the secret before the first mismatch (all of them, if the guess is the secret). A real attacker would measure time; here the count stands in for it. Every guess you pass must have exactly `length` characters.

Write `recover(oracle, length, alphabet)` that returns the secret string of `length` characters, each drawn from the string `alphabet`. Find it one position at a time: for each position, try every character there and keep the one that makes the count go up. Use at most `length × len(alphabet)` oracle calls, which is tiny next to the `len(alphabet) ** length` guesses of blind search.

For example, with the secret `"b3"` and the alphabet `"0123456789abcdef"`, `oracle("a0")` is 0, `oracle("b0")` is 1 and `oracle("b3")` is 2.
--- starter
def recover(oracle, length, alphabet):
    return ""
--- solution
def recover(oracle, length, alphabet):
    known = ""
    for position in range(length):
        filler = alphabet[0] * (length - position - 1)
        for c in alphabet:
            if oracle(known + c + filler) > position:
                known += c
                break
    return known
--- hint
Keep the characters found so far. For the next position, build each guess from the known part, one candidate character, and any filler character repeated to reach `length`.
--- hint
When `position` characters are already known, a wrong candidate makes the oracle return exactly `position`, and the right one makes it return more. Stop trying candidates as soon as you see that.
--- check test | Recovers a hex token and stays within the call budget
(lambda secret, alphabet: (lambda calls: recover(lambda g: (calls.append(g), next((i for i in range(len(secret)) if g[i] != secret[i]), len(secret)))[1] if len(g) == len(secret) else -99, len(secret), alphabet) == secret and len(calls) <= len(secret) * len(alphabet))([]))("3fa9c0", "0123456789abcdef")
?? Every guess must be exactly length characters long, and each position needs at most one pass through the alphabet.
--- check test | Recovers a one-character secret
(lambda secret: recover(lambda g: next((i for i in range(len(secret)) if g[i] != secret[i]), len(secret)), 1, "xyz") == secret)("z")
--- check test | Works when the right character is the first in the alphabet
(lambda secret: recover(lambda g: next((i for i in range(len(secret)) if g[i] != secret[i]), len(secret)), 4, "abc") == secret)("aaaa")
--- check test | Works when the right character is the last in the alphabet
(lambda secret: recover(lambda g: next((i for i in range(len(secret)) if g[i] != secret[i]), len(secret)), 3, "abc") == secret)("ccc")
--- check test | A longer secret over letters and digits
(lambda secret, alphabet: recover(lambda g: next((i for i in range(len(secret)) if g[i] != secret[i]), len(secret)), len(secret), alphabet) == secret)("k7q2zz9a", "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789")

=== sec-08 | Symmetric encryption: block ciphers, modes, and authenticated encryption
--- teach
Last lesson you stored passwords so that no one could read them back, not even the server. Most secrets are the other way round: telemetry, commands and files must be read back, but only by someone holding a key. That is **encryption**. This lesson builds it up from the one scheme that is provably perfect, through block ciphers and their modes, to the rule every real system follows: never encrypt without also authenticating.

### What an encryption scheme is

A **symmetric encryption scheme** is three algorithms: one that makes a random **key** k, one that turns a **plaintext** m into a **ciphertext** c = Enc(k, m), and one that turns it back, Dec(k, c) = m. "Symmetric" means the same key does both jobs, so sender and receiver must share it in advance. (Lesson 9 is about how two strangers can agree on one.)

**Kerckhoffs's principle:** the scheme itself is public; only the key is secret. A cipher whose safety depends on nobody learning how it works is broken the day someone reads the code.

What should "secure" mean? The modern definition, in words: an attacker who chooses two messages of the same length, is shown the encryption of one of them, and may ask for encryptions of anything else they like, still cannot tell which one it was better than by tossing a coin. This is called **indistinguishability under chosen-plaintext attack**, IND-CPA. The ciphertext may reveal the length and nothing more.

One consequence is worth seeing straight away: **a secure scheme cannot be deterministic.** If encrypting the same message twice gave the same ciphertext, the attacker would simply ask for the encryption of their first message and compare. So every good scheme mixes in something fresh each time, a random value or a counter.

### The one-time pad, and why it is perfect

The **one-time pad** encrypts by exclusive-or with a key as long as the message: c = m ⊕ k. Decrypting is the same operation, because (m ⊕ k) ⊕ k = m.

**Claim:** if k is uniformly random, as long as m, and used once, the ciphertext says nothing at all about m.

**Proof.** Fix any plaintext m and any ciphertext c of n bits. Exactly one key produces c from m, namely k = m ⊕ c. Every key has probability 2⁻ⁿ, so the probability of seeing c is 2⁻ⁿ **whatever m was**. Since every plaintext makes every ciphertext equally likely, seeing c cannot shift the odds between plaintexts. This is called **perfect secrecy**.

The catch is in the conditions. The key must be as long as everything you will ever send, and it must never be reused. Reuse it once and the key cancels out:

```python
m1, m2 = b"BURN", b"HOLD"
pad = bytes([0x3C, 0xA1, 0x07, 0x9E])
c1 = bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(m1, pad))
c2 = bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(m2, pad))
print(bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(c1, c2)).hex())   # 0a1a1e0a
print(bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(m1, m2)).hex())   # 0a1a1e0a, the same
```

c₁ ⊕ c₂ = m₁ ⊕ m₂. The attacker now holds the exclusive-or of two plaintexts, and knowing or guessing either one reveals the other. This is the **two-time pad**, and it broke real systems, most famously [[the Venona decryptions|venona]].

Everything that follows is a way to get the one-time pad's effect from a short key.

### Block ciphers

A **block cipher** is a keyed **permutation** of fixed-size blocks: for each key k, E(k, ·) maps every n-bit block to a different n-bit block, and D(k, ·) undoes it. **AES**, the standard since 2001, has 128-bit blocks and 128-, 192- or 256-bit keys. Its security goal is to be a **pseudorandom permutation**: with the key secret, E(k, ·) cannot be told apart from a permutation picked completely at random, by anyone with a feasible amount of computing.

How do you build a permutation that can be undone, out of scrambling steps that cannot? The classic answer is the **Feistel network**, used by DES and many others. Split the block into a left half L and a right half R. One **round** with round key kᵢ does:

(L, R) → (R, L ⊕ F(kᵢ, R))

F can be any function at all, even one that loses information. Here are three rounds on 16-bit blocks with a made-up F. It is a **toy**, far too small and simple to be secure:

```python
def F(k, r):
    return (r * 31 + k) % 256

left, right = 0x41, 0x42
for k in [7, 19, 88]:
    left, right = right, left ^ F(k, right)
print(hex(left), hex(right))   # 0xd 0xaf
```

**Why a Feistel round can always be undone.** After a round you hold L′ = R and R′ = L ⊕ F(k, R). The first equation gives R back directly: R = L′. Now you know R, so you can compute F(k, R) yourself, and exclusive-or it onto R′: R′ ⊕ F(k, R) = L ⊕ F(k, R) ⊕ F(k, R) = L. So one round is undone by (L′, R′) → (R′ ⊕ F(k, L′), L′), and the whole network by undoing the rounds in reverse order of keys. Notice that F is only ever run **forwards**; that is why it need not be invertible. [[Luby and Rackoff proved|lubyrackoff]] that four rounds with a truly random F give a pseudorandom permutation.

AES is built differently (a substitution-permutation network), but it has the same interface: a key, and a 16-byte block in and out.

### Modes: encrypting more than one block

A block cipher handles exactly one block. A **mode of operation** says how to use it on a message of any length.

The obvious mode is **ECB** (electronic codebook): cut the message into blocks and encrypt each one on its own. It is deterministic, so it fails IND-CPA immediately, and it fails in a way you can see: **equal plaintext blocks give equal ciphertext blocks.** Encrypt an image in ECB and its outline survives, the famous [[ECB penguin|penguin]]. A telemetry log in ECB shows which records repeat, even though no single record can be read.

| plaintext blocks | `ALT=0400` | `SPD=7700` | `ALT=0400` |
|---|---|---|---|
| ECB ciphertext | `9c1e…` | `47d0…` | `9c1e…` |

Before the fixes, one detail. Messages are rarely a whole number of blocks, so modes like ECB and CBC first **pad**. **PKCS#7 padding** appends k bytes each of value k, where 1 ≤ k ≤ the block size, choosing k to reach a multiple of the block size. A message that is already a whole number of blocks gets a **full extra block** of padding. That way the last byte always says how much to strip, and a real message that happens to end in `01` is never confused with padding.

**CBC** (cipher block chaining) fixes the repetition by mixing each block with the previous ciphertext before encrypting:

- pick a random, unpredictable **IV** (initialisation vector), one block long, and send it as C₀;
- Cᵢ = E(k, Pᵢ ⊕ Cᵢ₋₁) for each plaintext block Pᵢ;
- to decrypt, Pᵢ = D(k, Cᵢ) ⊕ Cᵢ₋₁.

Equal plaintext blocks now meet different Cᵢ₋₁ values, so they encrypt differently, and a fresh IV makes the same message encrypt differently every time. CBC encryption is **sequential** (each block needs the last), while decryption can run in parallel.

**CTR** (counter) mode turns the block cipher into a one-time pad generator. With a **nonce** (a "number used once") N, it computes a **keystream** of blocks E(k, N ‖ 0), E(k, N ‖ 1), E(k, N ‖ 2), … and exclusive-ors it with the message. Decrypting is the same operation. CTR needs no padding (cut the last keystream block short), runs fully in parallel, can decrypt any block on its own, and only ever uses E forwards. So in CTR any **pseudorandom function** can stand in for the block cipher; in this lesson HMAC-SHA256 cut to 16 bytes plays AES's part.

**Why CTR is secure.** If E is pseudorandom and the inputs N ‖ i never repeat under one key, every keystream block is the output of a random-looking function on a brand-new input. To the attacker the keystream is indistinguishable from uniform random bytes, so the ciphertext is, as far as they can tell, a one-time pad. The whole argument rests on **"never repeat"**: reuse a nonce with the same key and you have built the two-time pad again.

### Encryption does not stop tampering

Confidentiality and integrity are different properties, and encryption alone gives only the first. In CTR, flipping bit j of the ciphertext flips exactly bit j of the plaintext, and the receiver cannot tell. An attacker who knows or guesses part of a message can rewrite it without the key:

```python
stream = bytes([0x5A, 0x13, 0xC4, 0x77])    # stands in for the cipher's keystream
sent = bytes(p ^ s for p, s in zip(b"GO:0", stream))
changed = sent[:3] + bytes([sent[3] ^ ord("0") ^ ord("9")])
print(bytes(c ^ s for c, s in zip(changed, stream)))   # b'GO:9'
```

This is called **malleability**. CBC is malleable too, and worse: a server that reveals whether padding was valid after decryption becomes a [[padding oracle|paddingoracle]] that decrypts everything.

### Authenticated encryption: encrypt, then MAC

The fix is to authenticate every ciphertext with a MAC, and the safe order is **encrypt-then-MAC**:

1. c = Enc(k_enc, m), with a fresh nonce N;
2. t = HMAC(k_mac, N ‖ c), a **tag** over everything the receiver will use;
3. send N ‖ c ‖ t.

The receiver recomputes the tag and compares it with `hmac.compare_digest` **before decrypting anything**. If it does not match, it throws the message away.

**Why this is secure.** HMAC is unforgeable: without k_mac, no one can produce a valid tag for any N ‖ c that the sender did not send. So every ciphertext that passes the check is exactly one the sender made, and the decryption code never touches attacker-modified data. That shuts out bit flipping and padding oracles in one move. Confidentiality still comes from the encryption, which the MAC does not weaken, because the tag is computed from the ciphertext, not the plaintext. Use **two different keys**; one key for both jobs can let the two interfere.

The other orders have both been used and have both caused trouble: MAC-then-encrypt (TLS up to 1.2 with CBC, which led to padding-oracle attacks such as Lucky 13) and encrypt-and-MAC (SSH, where a MAC of the plaintext can leak whether two messages are equal). Modern systems use **AEAD** (authenticated encryption with associated data) modes such as **AES-GCM** and **ChaCha20-Poly1305**, which do encryption and authentication in one primitive and can also authenticate unencrypted headers.

**Cost:** CTR makes ⌈n / 16⌉ block calls for n bytes, so Θ(n) time, and each block is independent, so it spreads across cores. Encrypt-then-MAC adds one HMAC pass, also Θ(n). The ciphertext grows by the nonce and tag only: 8 + 32 bytes here, 12 + 16 for AES-GCM.

### Where it is used

- **TLS 1.3**, which protects HTTPS, encrypts every record with AES-GCM or ChaCha20-Poly1305 and allows nothing unauthenticated.
- **Disk encryption** uses AES in XTS mode; **WPA2 Wi-Fi** uses AES in CCM mode, another encrypt-and-authenticate construction.
- **Spacecraft links:** the CCSDS Space Data Link Security protocol uses AES-GCM so that a telecommand is both secret and provably from the ground station.
- In real Python code you would call a vetted library, such as the `cryptography` package's `AESGCM`, never a hand-made cipher. The constructions here are for understanding.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- **ECB mode.** It is the default in some libraries. Never use it for more than one block.
- **Reusing a nonce** with the same key in CTR or GCM. It is the two-time pad, and in GCM it also leaks the authentication key. A common cause is a counter that resets to zero after a reboot.
- **Encryption without authentication**, or decrypting **before** checking the tag.
- **One key for both encryption and MAC.** Derive or generate two.
- **Inventing your own cipher.** The toy Feistel above would fall to a student in an afternoon.

::: context venona The Venona project
During the Second World War the Soviet Union used one-time pads for diplomatic and intelligence messages, which should have been unbreakable. Under wartime pressure, some pad pages were printed twice and used for different messages. From the 1940s to 1980, American and British analysts in the Venona project found pairs of messages encrypted with the same pad and, by exclusive-oring them and guessing likely words, decrypted thousands of messages. The mathematics of the pad was never broken; the rule that each key is used once was.
:::

::: context lubyrackoff The Luby-Rackoff theorem
In 1988 Michael Luby and Charles Rackoff proved that a Feistel network with three rounds, each using an independent truly random function as F, cannot be told apart from a random permutation by anyone who can only ask for encryptions, and that four rounds are enough even when the attacker can also ask for decryptions. It explained why the Feistel structure works and turned block-cipher design into something that can be reasoned about. Real ciphers cannot use truly random functions, so their security still rests on years of public attack attempts.
:::

::: context penguin The ECB penguin
A well-known picture shows the Linux mascot, Tux the penguin, as an image file encrypted with a strong block cipher in ECB mode. The colours become noise, but the penguin's outline is still perfectly clear, because every block of identical background pixels encrypts to the same ciphertext block, and every block of identical black pixels does too. The same image encrypted in CBC or CTR mode looks like uniform random static. It is the quickest way to remember that a strong cipher in a weak mode is a weak system.
:::

::: context paddingoracle Padding oracle attacks
In 2002 Serge Vaudenay showed that if a server decrypts CBC ciphertext and reveals, by an error message or even by timing, whether the padding was valid, an attacker can decrypt any message by sending modified copies of it and watching the answers, about 128 tries per byte. Variants broke ASP.NET, Java frameworks and, as POODLE and Lucky 13, parts of SSL and TLS. The root cause is decrypting data that was never authenticated. Encrypt-then-MAC, checked before any decryption, removes the oracle completely.
:::
--- task
Build authenticated encryption from `hmac` and `hashlib.sha256`: CTR mode with HMAC-SHA256 as the stand-in block function, then encrypt-then-MAC. This is a teaching construction; real code uses a vetted AEAD such as AES-GCM.

- `keystream(key, nonce, n)`: return the first `n` bytes of the keystream. Block `i` (counting from 0) is the first 16 bytes of `hmac.new(key, nonce + i.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()`. `n` may be 0.
- `ctr_xor(key, nonce, data)`: exclusive-or `data` with the keystream of the same length and return the result. The same function encrypts and decrypts.
- `seal(enc_key, mac_key, nonce, plaintext)`: `nonce` is 8 bytes. Encrypt with `ctr_xor(enc_key, nonce, plaintext)`, compute the tag `hmac.new(mac_key, nonce + ciphertext, hashlib.sha256).digest()` (32 bytes), and return `nonce + ciphertext + tag`.
- `open_box(enc_key, mac_key, box)`: undo `seal`. The nonce is the first 8 bytes and the tag the last 32. If `box` is shorter than 40 bytes, or the tag does not match (compare with `hmac.compare_digest`), return `None` **without decrypting**. Otherwise return the plaintext.

For example, `seal(b"enc", b"mac", b"12345678", b"")` is 40 bytes long, and `open_box` of it gives `b""`.
--- starter
def keystream(key, nonce, n):
    raise NotImplementedError


def ctr_xor(key, nonce, data):
    raise NotImplementedError


def seal(enc_key, mac_key, nonce, plaintext):
    raise NotImplementedError


def open_box(enc_key, mac_key, box):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def keystream(key, nonce, n):
    out = b""
    counter = 0
    while len(out) < n:
        out += hmac.new(key, nonce + counter.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()[:16]
        counter += 1
    return out[:n]


def ctr_xor(key, nonce, data):
    return bytes(d ^ k for d, k in zip(data, keystream(key, nonce, len(data))))


def seal(enc_key, mac_key, nonce, plaintext):
    ciphertext = ctr_xor(enc_key, nonce, plaintext)
    tag = hmac.new(mac_key, nonce + ciphertext, hashlib.sha256).digest()
    return nonce + ciphertext + tag


def open_box(enc_key, mac_key, box):
    if len(box) < 40:
        return None
    nonce, ciphertext, tag = box[:8], box[8:-32], box[-32:]
    expected = hmac.new(mac_key, nonce + ciphertext, hashlib.sha256).digest()
    if not hmac.compare_digest(tag, expected):
        return None
    return ctr_xor(enc_key, nonce, ciphertext)
--- hint
`keystream` appends 16-byte blocks for counter 0, 1, 2, … while it is shorter than `n`, then cuts to `n`. `ctr_xor` zips the data with a keystream of the same length and exclusive-ors each pair.
--- hint
`seal` is three lines: encrypt, tag `nonce + ciphertext`, join. In `open_box`, slice out `box[:8]`, `box[8:-32]` and `box[-32:]`, recompute the tag, and return `None` if `hmac.compare_digest` says they differ.
--- check case | The first 20 bytes of a keystream
keystream(b"k", b"\x00" * 8, 20).hex()
=> "774fadacdb2f02c0dd3e0141b2964e16cf27a743"
--- check case | Asking for no bytes gives none
keystream(b"k", b"\x00" * 8, 0)
=> b""
--- check test | A longer keystream starts with the shorter one
keystream(b"key", b"nonce-99", 50)[:20] == keystream(b"key", b"nonce-99", 20) and len(keystream(b"key", b"nonce-99", 50)) == 50
--- check case | A known ciphertext
ctr_xor(b"k1", b"nonce-01", b"ATTACK AT DAWN").hex()
=> "dde098146c279dcd5b35cc8820fc"
--- check test | ctr_xor undoes itself, across block boundaries
all(ctr_xor(b"k", b"n0nce-07", ctr_xor(b"k", b"n0nce-07", bytes(range(n)))) == bytes(range(n)) for n in (0, 1, 15, 16, 17, 32, 33, 100))
--- check test | A different nonce gives a different ciphertext
ctr_xor(b"k", b"nonce-01", b"same message") != ctr_xor(b"k", b"nonce-02", b"same message")
--- check test | Reusing a nonce leaks the exclusive-or of the plaintexts
(lambda m1, m2: bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(ctr_xor(b"k", b"reused!!", m1), ctr_xor(b"k", b"reused!!", m2))) == bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(m1, m2)))(b"RELAY OPEN  now", b"RELAY SHUT soon")
--- check case | A known sealed box

--- check test | The box is nonce, ciphertext and tag

--- check case | Sealing then opening gives the plaintext back

=> b"hello orbit"
--- check case | The empty plaintext seals to 40 bytes and opens
(len(seal(b"enc", b"mac", b"12345678", b"")), open_box(b"enc", b"mac", seal(b"enc", b"mac", b"12345678", b"")))
=> (40, b"")
--- check test | Changing any single byte of the box makes it fail
(lambda b: all(open_box(b"e", b"m", b[:i] + bytes([b[i] ^ 1]) + b[i + 1:]) is None for i in range(len(b))))(seal(b"e", b"m", b"ABCDEFGH", b"set thrust 40%"))
?? Every byte, nonce and tag included, must be covered by the tag or be the tag.
--- check case | A wrong MAC key fails
open_box(b"e", b"wrong", seal(b"e", b"m", b"ABCDEFGH", b"hello"))
=> None
--- check test | Too-short boxes fail without raising
[open_box(b"e", b"m", seal(b"e", b"m", b"ABCDEFGH", b"hi")[:n]) for n in (0, 8, 39)] == [None, None, None]
--- check test | Cutting bytes off the ciphertext fails
(lambda b: open_box(b"e", b"m", b[:8] + b[9:]) is None)(seal(b"e", b"m", b"ABCDEFGH", b"orbital"))
--- check source | Compares tags with hmac.compare_digest
compare_digest

+++ practice | PKCS#7 padding
--- task
Write PKCS#7 padding for any block size from 1 to 255.

- `pad(data, block_size)`: append k bytes, each of value k, where k is from 1 to `block_size` and makes the length a multiple of `block_size`. Data that is already a whole number of blocks (including empty data) gets a full block of padding.
- `unpad(data, block_size)`: undo `pad`. Return `None` if the padding is not valid: `data` is empty or not a multiple of `block_size`, the last byte k is 0 or bigger than `block_size`, or the last k bytes are not all equal to k.

For example, `pad(b"ORBIT", 8)` is `b"ORBIT\x03\x03\x03"`, `pad(b"", 4)` is `b"\x04\x04\x04\x04"`, and `unpad(b"ORBIT\x03\x02\x03", 8)` is `None`.
--- starter
def pad(data, block_size):
    return data


def unpad(data, block_size):
    return data
--- solution
def pad(data, block_size):
    k = block_size - len(data) % block_size
    return data + bytes([k]) * k


def unpad(data, block_size):
    if not data or len(data) % block_size != 0:
        return None
    k = data[-1]
    if k < 1 or k > block_size:
        return None
    if data[-k:] != bytes([k]) * k:
        return None
    return data[:-k]
--- hint
The padding length is `block_size - len(data) % block_size`, which is never 0: when the data fills whole blocks it is a whole block.
--- hint
To unpad, read the last byte as k, check its range, check that the last k bytes are `bytes([k]) * k`, and slice them off.
--- check case | Padding a short message
pad(b"ORBIT", 8)
=> b"ORBIT\x03\x03\x03"
--- check case | Whole blocks get a full block of padding
(pad(b"", 4), pad(b"ABCD", 4), len(pad(bytes(16), 16)))
=> (b"\x04\x04\x04\x04", b"ABCD\x04\x04\x04\x04", 32)
--- check case | Block size 1 always adds one byte of value 1
pad(b"xyz", 1)
=> b"xyz\x01"
--- check test | unpad undoes pad for every length
all(unpad(pad(bytes(range(n)), 8), 8) == bytes(range(n)) for n in range(40))
--- check case | Inconsistent padding bytes
unpad(b"ORBIT\x03\x02\x03", 8)
=> None
--- check case | A zero byte or a byte too large is not padding
(unpad(b"ABCDEFG\x00", 8), unpad(b"ABCDEFG\x09", 8))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | Empty or misaligned data
(unpad(b"", 8), unpad(b"ABC\x01", 8))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | A whole block of padding unpads to empty
unpad(b"\x08" * 8, 8)
=> b""

+++ practice | Seeing the pattern ECB leaks
--- task
An attacker cannot decrypt ECB ciphertext, but can see which blocks are equal. Write `ecb_pattern(ciphertext, block_size)` that cuts `ciphertext` into blocks of `block_size` bytes (its length is always a multiple of `block_size`) and returns a list with one label per block: the first distinct block gets 0, the next new block 1, and so on, and a block equal to an earlier one gets that block's label.

For example, blocks `A B A C B` give `[0, 1, 0, 2, 1]`. Empty ciphertext gives `[]`.
--- starter
def ecb_pattern(ciphertext, block_size):
    return []
--- solution
def ecb_pattern(ciphertext, block_size):
    labels = {}
    pattern = []
    for start in range(0, len(ciphertext), block_size):
        block = ciphertext[start:start + block_size]
        if block not in labels:
            labels[block] = len(labels)
        pattern.append(labels[block])
    return pattern
--- hint
Walk the ciphertext in steps of `block_size` and slice out each block. Keep a dict from block to its label.
--- hint
A block not yet in the dict gets the label `len(labels)`, which is the number of distinct blocks seen so far.
--- check case | The example from the task
ecb_pattern(b"AAAABBBBAAAACCCCBBBB", 4)
=> [0, 1, 0, 2, 1]
--- check case | Empty ciphertext
ecb_pattern(b"", 16)
=> []
--- check case | All blocks equal
ecb_pattern(bytes(48), 16)
=> [0, 0, 0]
--- check case | All blocks different
ecb_pattern(bytes(range(12)), 3)
=> [0, 1, 2, 3]
--- check test | A deterministic block function on repeated telemetry leaks the repeats
(lambda E: ecb_pattern(b"".join(E(b) for b in [b"ALT=0400", b"SPD=7700", b"ALT=0400", b"ALT=0410", b"SPD=7700"]), 8) == [0, 1, 0, 2, 1])(lambda b: __import__("hmac").new(b"k", b, "sha256").digest()[:8])

+++ practice | A toy Feistel block cipher
--- task
Build a **toy** Feistel block cipher on 8-byte blocks. It is for learning only: it has never been analysed and must never protect anything real.

A block is split into a left half `L` (first 4 bytes) and a right half `R` (last 4 bytes). The round function is `F(k, R) = hmac.new(k, R, hashlib.sha256).digest()[:4]`. With a list of round keys, each round with key `k` turns `(L, R)` into `(R, L xor F(k, R))`, the exclusive-or taken byte by byte. The result is `L + R` after the last round.

- `feistel_encrypt(round_keys, block)`: apply one round per key, in order.
- `feistel_decrypt(round_keys, block)`: undo `feistel_encrypt` with the same list of keys, using only `F` forwards, as the lesson proved possible.

For example, `feistel_encrypt([], b"ABCDEFGH")` is `b"ABCDEFGH"` (no rounds), and `feistel_decrypt(keys, feistel_encrypt(keys, block))` is always `block`.
--- starter
def feistel_encrypt(round_keys, block):
    return block


def feistel_decrypt(round_keys, block):
    return block
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def F(k, half):
    return hmac.new(k, half, hashlib.sha256).digest()[:4]


def xor4(a, b):
    return bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(a, b))


def feistel_encrypt(round_keys, block):
    left, right = block[:4], block[4:]
    for k in round_keys:
        left, right = right, xor4(left, F(k, right))
    return left + right


def feistel_decrypt(round_keys, block):
    left, right = block[:4], block[4:]
    for k in reversed(round_keys):
        left, right = xor4(right, F(k, left)), left
    return left + right
--- hint
Write `F` and a byte-by-byte exclusive-or helper first. Encryption is a loop over the keys doing the round from the task.
--- hint
One round is undone by `(L', R') → (R' xor F(k, L'), L')`. Undo the rounds in reverse key order.
--- check case | Four rounds on a known block
feistel_encrypt([b"k1", b"k2", b"k3", b"k4"], b"ORBIT-01").hex()
=> "b1f11970c0aee7b5"
--- check case | The all-zero block
feistel_encrypt([b"k1", b"k2", b"k3", b"k4"], bytes(8)).hex()
=> "55efadadeee5a0b5"
--- check case | One round moves the right half to the left
feistel_encrypt([b"only"], b"ABCDEFGH").hex()
=> "45464748be52e239"
--- check case | No rounds changes nothing
(feistel_encrypt([], b"ABCDEFGH"), feistel_decrypt([], b"ABCDEFGH"))
=> (b"ABCDEFGH", b"ABCDEFGH")
--- check test | Decryption undoes encryption on many blocks
all(feistel_decrypt([b"a", b"b", b"c"], feistel_encrypt([b"a", b"b", b"c"], bytes([i, 2 * i % 256, 7, 0, i ^ 85, 1, 2, 3]))) == bytes([i, 2 * i % 256, 7, 0, i ^ 85, 1, 2, 3]) for i in range(256))
--- check test | It is a permutation: 256 different blocks give 256 different ciphertexts
len({feistel_encrypt([b"x", b"y", b"z", b"w"], bytes([i, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0])) for i in range(256)}) == 256
--- check test | Key order matters
feistel_encrypt([b"k1", b"k2"], b"ORBIT-01") != feistel_encrypt([b"k2", b"k1"], b"ORBIT-01")

+++ practice | CBC mode, with the edges
--- task
The starter gives you `encrypt_block(keys, block)` and `decrypt_block(keys, block)`, a toy 8-byte block cipher. Build CBC mode on it with PKCS#7 padding to 8 bytes.

- `cbc_encrypt(keys, iv, plaintext)`: `iv` is 8 bytes. Pad the plaintext, then for each 8-byte block Pᵢ compute Cᵢ = `encrypt_block(keys, Pᵢ xor Cᵢ₋₁)`, where C₀ is the IV. Return `iv + C₁ + C₂ + …`.
- `cbc_decrypt(keys, data)`: undo it: Pᵢ = `decrypt_block(keys, Cᵢ) xor Cᵢ₋₁`, then remove the padding. Return `None` if `data` is shorter than 16 bytes, is not a multiple of 8 bytes long, or the padding is invalid (last byte k not from 1 to 8, or the last k bytes not all equal to k).

An empty plaintext still produces one full block of padding, so its output is 16 bytes: the IV and one block.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def _f(k, half):
    return hmac.new(k, half, hashlib.sha256).digest()[:4]


def _x(a, b):
    return bytes(p ^ q for p, q in zip(a, b))


def encrypt_block(keys, block):
    left, right = block[:4], block[4:]
    for k in keys:
        left, right = right, _x(left, _f(k, right))
    return left + right


def decrypt_block(keys, block):
    left, right = block[:4], block[4:]
    for k in reversed(keys):
        left, right = _x(right, _f(k, left)), left
    return left + right


def cbc_encrypt(keys, iv, plaintext):
    raise NotImplementedError


def cbc_decrypt(keys, data):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def _f(k, half):
    return hmac.new(k, half, hashlib.sha256).digest()[:4]


def _x(a, b):
    return bytes(p ^ q for p, q in zip(a, b))


def encrypt_block(keys, block):
    left, right = block[:4], block[4:]
    for k in keys:
        left, right = right, _x(left, _f(k, right))
    return left + right


def decrypt_block(keys, block):
    left, right = block[:4], block[4:]
    for k in reversed(keys):
        left, right = _x(right, _f(k, left)), left
    return left + right


def cbc_encrypt(keys, iv, plaintext):
    k = 8 - len(plaintext) % 8
    padded = plaintext + bytes([k]) * k
    out = iv
    previous = iv
    for start in range(0, len(padded), 8):
        previous = encrypt_block(keys, _x(padded[start:start + 8], previous))
        out += previous
    return out


def cbc_decrypt(keys, data):
    if len(data) < 16 or len(data) % 8 != 0:
        return None
    plain = b""
    for start in range(8, len(data), 8):
        block = data[start:start + 8]
        plain += _x(decrypt_block(keys, block), data[start - 8:start])
    k = plain[-1]
    if k < 1 or k > 8 or plain[-k:] != bytes([k]) * k:
        return None
    return plain[:-k]
--- hint
For encryption, keep `previous`, starting as the IV. Each padded block is exclusive-ored with `previous`, encrypted, appended, and becomes the new `previous`.
--- hint
For decryption, the "previous" block of ciphertext block i is simply the 8 bytes before it in `data` (the IV for the first). Check the lengths first and the padding last.
--- check case | A known ciphertext
cbc_encrypt([b"k1", b"k2", b"k3", b"k4"], b"IV-12345", b"ALT=0400ALT=0400").hex()
=> "49562d31323334354efa91a199c3293e857711573f3735b7c94a179836574d0b"
?? Pad first: 16 bytes of plaintext become 24.
--- check test | Equal plaintext blocks give different ciphertext blocks
(lambda c: c[8:16] != c[16:24])(cbc_encrypt([b"k"], b"\x00" * 8, b"SAMEBLK!SAMEBLK!"))
--- check test | Round trip for every length from 0 to 30
all(cbc_decrypt([b"a", b"b"], cbc_encrypt([b"a", b"b"], b"iv-iv-iv", bytes(range(n)))) == bytes(range(n)) for n in range(31))
--- check case | The empty plaintext is IV plus one block
(len(cbc_encrypt([b"k"], b"12345678", b"")), cbc_decrypt([b"k"], cbc_encrypt([b"k"], b"12345678", b"")))
=> (16, b"")
--- check case | An exact block gets a whole block of padding
len(cbc_encrypt([b"k"], b"12345678", b"8 bytes!"))
=> 24
--- check case | Too short or misaligned data is rejected
(cbc_decrypt([b"k"], b""), cbc_decrypt([b"k"], b"12345678"), cbc_decrypt([b"k"], bytes(17)))
=> (None, None, None)
--- check test | Bad padding is rejected
cbc_decrypt([b"k"], b"12345678" + encrypt_block([b"k"], bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(b"ABCDEF\x05\x05", b"12345678")))) is None
--- check test | A different IV gives a different ciphertext for the same message
cbc_encrypt([b"k"], b"AAAAAAAA", b"hello") != cbc_encrypt([b"k"], b"BBBBBBBB", b"hello")

+++ practice | Debug: the keystream that drops the last block
--- task
This CTR-mode function should exclusive-or `data` with a keystream whose block `i` (from 0) is the first 16 bytes of `hmac.new(key, nonce + i.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()`, and return a result exactly as long as `data`. It works for messages whose length is a multiple of 16, but a 17-byte message comes back 16 bytes long, and a 10-byte message comes back empty. Fix it.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def ctr(key, nonce, data):
    stream = b""
    for i in range(len(data) // 16):
        stream += hmac.new(key, nonce + i.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()[:16]
    return bytes(d ^ s for d, s in zip(data, stream))
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def ctr(key, nonce, data):
    stream = b""
    for i in range((len(data) + 15) // 16):
        stream += hmac.new(key, nonce + i.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()[:16]
    return bytes(d ^ s for d, s in zip(data, stream))
--- hint
How many 16-byte keystream blocks does a 17-byte message need? What does `17 // 16` give?
--- hint
You need the number of blocks rounded **up**: `(len(data) + 15) // 16`.
--- check test | Output is always as long as the input
all(len(ctr(b"k", b"nonce-00", bytes(n))) == n for n in (0, 1, 10, 15, 16, 17, 31, 32, 33))
--- check test | Round trip on odd lengths
all(ctr(b"k", b"nonce-00", ctr(b"k", b"nonce-00", bytes(range(n)))) == bytes(range(n)) for n in (1, 10, 17, 47))
--- check case | A known 17-byte ciphertext
ctr(b"k1", b"nonce-01", b"ATTACK AT DAWN!!!").hex()
=> "dde098146c279dcd5b35cc8820fc89a66d"
--- check case | Empty data
ctr(b"k", b"nonce-00", b"")
=> b""

+++ practice | Breaking a reused nonce
--- task
A buggy radio encrypted every telemetry frame of a pass with CTR mode under the **same key and the same nonce**, so every frame was exclusive-ored with the same keystream. You intercepted the ciphertext frames, a list of `bytes`, and you know the full plaintext of the first frame, `known` (a status frame whose contents are fixed).

Write `recover_frames(frames, known)` that returns a list with the recovered plaintext of every frame, first frame included. Byte j of the keystream is `frames[0][j] xor known[j]`, which you can only learn for positions where the first frame exists. So each recovered frame is only as long as the shorter of that frame and `frames[0]`. An empty `frames` list gives `[]`.

For example, if the first frame is 4 bytes and the second is 6 bytes, you recover the first 4 bytes of the second frame.
--- starter
def recover_frames(frames, known):
    return frames
--- solution
def recover_frames(frames, known):
    if not frames:
        return []
    stream = bytes(c ^ p for c, p in zip(frames[0], known))
    return [bytes(c ^ s for c, s in zip(frame, stream)) for frame in frames]
--- hint
Exclusive-or the first ciphertext frame with its known plaintext to get the keystream bytes it covers.
--- hint
Exclusive-or every frame with that keystream. `zip` stops at the shorter of the two, which gives exactly the lengths the task asks for.
--- check test | Recovers every frame under a reused nonce
(lambda ks: recover_frames([bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(p, ks)) for p in [b"STATUS:NOMINAL--", b"CMD:VALVE2 OPEN ", b"TEMP=-41C"]], b"STATUS:NOMINAL--") == [b"STATUS:NOMINAL--", b"CMD:VALVE2 OPEN ", b"TEMP=-41C"])(bytes((i * 73 + 11) % 256 for i in range(16)))
--- check test | A frame longer than the first is recovered only as far as the first goes
(lambda ks: recover_frames([bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(p, ks)) for p in [b"HDR1", b"SECRET"]], b"HDR1") == [b"HDR1", b"SECR"])(bytes([9, 200, 33, 71, 5, 250]))
--- check case | No frames
recover_frames([], b"anything")
=> []
--- check test | Only the first frame
recover_frames([bytes([1, 2, 3])], b"abc") == [b"abc"]
--- check test | Works against real CTR output from the lesson's construction
(lambda ks: (lambda frames: recover_frames(frames, b"known status frame!") == [b"known status frame!", b"attack at 0400 UTC!", b"hi"])([bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(p, ks)) for p in [b"known status frame!", b"attack at 0400 UTC!", b"hi"]]))(b"".join(__import__("hmac").new(b"k", b"n" * 8 + i.to_bytes(8, "big"), "sha256").digest()[:16] for i in range(2)))

+++ practice | Rewriting a command without the key
--- task
CTR mode alone does not stop tampering. An attacker intercepts `ciphertext`, the CTR encryption of a command they can guess, `known` (same length). They want the receiver to decrypt it to `wanted` instead, which has the same length.

Write `forge(ciphertext, known, wanted)` that returns a new ciphertext that decrypts, under the same unknown key and nonce, to `wanted`. You never see the key. If the three do not all have the same length, return `None`.

Why it works: ciphertext byte = plaintext byte xor keystream byte, so exclusive-oring a ciphertext byte with `known ⊕ wanted` swaps the plaintext byte without touching the keystream. For example, turning `"GO:0"` into `"GO:9"` only changes the last byte. An authentication tag, as in encrypt-then-MAC, is what makes this attack fail.
--- starter
def forge(ciphertext, known, wanted):
    return ciphertext
--- solution
def forge(ciphertext, known, wanted):
    if not (len(ciphertext) == len(known) == len(wanted)):
        return None
    return bytes(c ^ k ^ w for c, k, w in zip(ciphertext, known, wanted))
--- hint
Check the three lengths first. Then work byte by byte over the three sequences together.
--- hint
Each new byte is the ciphertext byte exclusive-ored with the known byte and the wanted byte.
--- check test | The receiver decrypts the forged command
(lambda ks: bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(forge(bytes(a ^ b for a, b in zip(b"THRUST 010%", ks)), b"THRUST 010%", b"THRUST 100%"), ks)) == b"THRUST 100%")(bytes((i * 29 + 3) % 256 for i in range(11)))
--- check test | Wanting the same text changes nothing
(lambda c: forge(c, b"abcd", b"abcd") == c)(bytes([10, 20, 30, 40]))
--- check case | Lengths that differ are refused
(forge(b"abc", b"ab", b"xy"), forge(b"ab", b"ab", b"xyz"))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | Empty messages
forge(b"", b"", b"")
=> b""
--- check test | Only the bytes that differ are changed
(lambda c: [i for i in range(4) if forge(c, b"GO:0", b"GO:9")[i] != c[i]] == [3])(bytes([0x1D, 0x5C, 0xFE, 0x47]))

=== sec-09 | Public-key cryptography: RSA in practice, signatures and key exchange
--- teach
Last lesson you encrypted and authenticated with a shared secret key. That leaves a hole: how do a spacecraft and a ground station, or your browser and a bank it has never met, agree on that shared key? Sending it over the network lets anyone listening read it. **Public-key cryptography** closes the hole. In Discrete Mathematics you built RSA and proved it correct; this lesson shows how it is really used: why the textbook version is unsafe, how digital signatures work, and how two strangers agree on a key in the open with Diffie-Hellman.

### Two keys instead of one

Each party has a **key pair**: a **public key**, which anyone may hold, and a **private key**, which never leaves its owner. What one key does, only the other undoes or checks. That gives two jobs:

- **encryption:** anyone encrypts with your public key; only your private key reads it back;
- **signatures:** you sign with your private key; anyone checks the signature with your public key.

A padlock is the everyday picture: you hand out open padlocks (public keys) freely, anyone can snap one shut on a box, and only you hold the key that opens it.

### RSA, recalled

From Discrete Mathematics, RSA sets up like this:

1. pick two large random primes p and q, and let n = pq and phi = (p - 1)(q - 1);
2. pick e with gcd(e, phi) = 1 (almost always e = 65537);
3. let d be the inverse of e modulo phi, so that e*d is congruent to 1 (mod phi).

The public key is (n, e), the private key is d, and the raw operations x^e mod n and y^d mod n undo each other. Python's three-argument `pow` does both, and `pow(e, -1, phi)` finds the inverse. Here on the classic tiny example:

```python
p, q, e = 61, 53, 17
phi = (p - 1) * (q - 1)
d = pow(e, -1, phi)
print(d)                              # 2753
c = pow(65, e, p * q)
print(c, pow(c, d, p * q))            # 2790 65
```

**Why it works, recalled in one line.** Writing e*d = 1 + k*phi and using Fermat's little theorem modulo p and modulo q, then the Chinese remainder theorem, gives (m^e)^d congruent to m (mod n) for every m from 0 to n - 1. You proved this in full in Discrete Mathematics.

**Why it resists attack.** Anyone who can factor n recovers p and q, then phi, then d. The best known method for numbers this size, the general number field sieve, runs faster than exponential time but slower than any polynomial, so real keys are 2048 bits or more (about 112 bits of security), and 3072 bits gives about 128. Every modulus in this lesson is a [[toy|toykeys]], small enough to factor in seconds.

### Why the raw operation is not encryption

Using c = m^e mod n directly, "textbook RSA", is unsafe as an encryption scheme in three ways.

- **It is deterministic.** Last lesson showed a secure scheme cannot be: if the message is one of a few values (yes, no, a vote), the attacker just encrypts each candidate with the public key and compares.
- **It is malleable.** Because (m1^e)(m2^e) = (m1*m2)^e mod n, from the encryption of m anyone can produce the encryption of 2m without knowing m.
- **Small values do not wrap.** With e = 3 and m below the cube root of n, m^3 is less than n, so c equals m^3 as an ordinary integer and a plain integer cube root recovers m.

Real systems fix this with **randomised padding**: wrap the message in a structure with fixed markers and fresh random bytes before applying the exponent. The standard is **OAEP** (optimal asymmetric encryption padding). Even so, RSA is almost never used to encrypt a message directly. Instead it does **key encapsulation**: generate a fresh random symmetric key, encrypt the actual data with AES-GCM (last lesson), and use RSA only to wrap that one small key. This is **hybrid encryption**, and it is how RSA appears in real protocols.

### Digital signatures: the private key as a pen

Encryption uses the keys one way; signatures use them the other. To **sign** a message you first hash it (last lesson's SHA-256), then apply your **private** key to the hash. Anyone applies your **public** key to the signature and checks the result equals the hash they compute themselves.

The signature gives three things at once:

- **authenticity:** only the private-key holder could have produced it;
- **integrity:** it is tied to the exact hash, so any change to the message breaks it;
- **non-repudiation:** the signer cannot later deny it, since no one else could have signed.

Hashing first is not optional. Signing the raw message would inherit RSA's malleability (two signatures multiply into a third), would leak structure, and could only sign messages smaller than n. Hashing to a fixed size destroys the multiplicative structure and lets you sign a message of any length. This "hash-then-sign" is why last lesson's collision resistance matters: if an attacker finds two messages with the same hash, one signature covers both, which is exactly how a forged certificate was built against MD5.

```python
import hashlib
def digest_int(message, n):
    return int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(message).digest(), "big") % n
# sign with d, check with e; here on a toy modulus
n, e, d = 3233, 17, 2753
h = digest_int(b"launch", n)
signature = pow(h, d, n)
print(pow(signature, e, n) == h)      # True
```

Real signatures pad the hash (RSA-PSS, or the older PKCS#1 v1.5) so that not every number is a valid signature. Other schemes sign differently: **ECDSA** and **Ed25519** use elliptic curves and give much shorter keys and signatures for the same strength, which is why new systems prefer them.

### Diffie-Hellman: a shared key in the open

Signatures prove who sent something; they do not by themselves set up a shared secret key. **Diffie-Hellman key exchange** does, and it was the 1976 idea that started public-key cryptography.

Everyone agrees on a large prime p and a base g. Then:

- one side picks a secret a and sends A = g^a mod p;
- the other picks a secret b and sends B = g^b mod p;
- each raises what it received to its own secret: the first computes B^a mod p, the second A^b mod p.

Both get g^(a*b) mod p, the same value, because (g^b)^a = (g^a)^b. An eavesdropper sees p, g, A and B but not a or b.

```python
p, g = 23, 5
A = pow(g, 6, p)                      # one side's public value, secret 6
B = pow(g, 15, p)                     # other side's public value, secret 15
print(pow(B, 6, p), pow(A, 15, p))    # 2 2  -- the same shared value
```

**Why the eavesdropper is stuck.** Recovering a from A = g^a mod p is the **discrete logarithm problem**, and computing g^(a*b) from g^a and g^b is the **[[computational Diffie-Hellman problem|cdh]]**; both are believed hard for a well-chosen large prime. The shared number is not used as a key directly: it is run through a hash (a key-derivation function) to produce uniform key bytes.

Real systems use **ephemeral** Diffie-Hellman, a fresh secret per session, which buys **[[forward secrecy|forwardsecrecy]]**: recording today's traffic and stealing the long-term key next year still does not reveal today's session key, because the ephemeral secrets are gone. Modern deployments use the elliptic-curve version, ECDHE, over curves such as X25519.

### The missing piece: who is on the other end

Diffie-Hellman gives a shared key with **somebody**, but not proof of **who**. If an attacker sits in the middle, relaying and replacing the public values, they share one key with each side and read everything. This is the **man-in-the-middle** attack, and the next lesson is about the protocol machinery (nonces, certificates, signed transcripts) that stops it. The short version: the public values are **signed**, so each side checks a signature against a key it already trusts. Signatures authenticate; Diffie-Hellman establishes the secret; together they are the core of the TLS handshake.

### What it costs

- **Modular exponentiation** with a k-bit modulus and exponent uses square-and-multiply: about k squarings and up to k multiplications, so it is cubic in k with schoolbook multiplication. This is why public-key operations are far slower than a symmetric cipher, and why RSA wraps a symmetric key rather than the whole message.
- **Key sizes:** RSA and finite-field Diffie-Hellman need 2048 to 3072 bits for about 112 to 128 bits of security; elliptic curves reach the same with 256-bit keys, so they are smaller and faster.

### Where it is used

- **TLS**, so every HTTPS page, uses a signed (EC)DHE handshake to set up the AES-GCM keys that protect the connection.
- **SSH** authenticates servers and users with public keys; **Signal** and **WhatsApp** use elliptic-curve Diffie-Hellman for end-to-end encryption.
- **Software and firmware signing:** operating systems, app stores and secure-boot chains check a signature before running code, which for a spacecraft means a command or an update is executed only if it is signed by the mission's key.
- **Certificates** bind a name to a public key with a signature from a certificate authority, the trust anchor the next lesson depends on.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- **Textbook RSA for encryption or signatures.** Always pad (OAEP, PSS) or, better, use a vetted library.
- **Signing the message instead of its hash.** Hash first, always, with a collision-resistant hash.
- **A tiny or shared public exponent with no padding**, and reusing primes across keys: if two RSA moduli share a prime, a gcd factors both at once.
- **Static Diffie-Hellman with no authentication**, which is wide open to a man in the middle, and non-ephemeral keys, which give up forward secrecy.
- **Rolling your own big-number code for real use.** Side channels (timing, power) leak keys; production libraries are hardened against them.

::: context toykeys Why the moduli here are toys
Every RSA modulus and Diffie-Hellman prime in this lesson is only a few dozen to a few hundred bits, chosen so the arithmetic is instant and, deliberately, so it could be factored or its discrete log solved in seconds. That is the point of a teaching example: you can see every step and even break it yourself. A real modulus is 2048 bits or more, a 617-plus-digit number, because the whole security of RSA rests on that number being far too large to factor with any known method and any computer that could be built. Never use a small modulus, or key material generated by example code, to protect anything.
:::

::: context cdh Two hard problems behind Diffie-Hellman
Given g and g^a mod p, finding a is the discrete logarithm problem. Given g^a and g^b but not a or b, computing g^(a*b) is the computational Diffie-Hellman problem, and telling g^(a*b) apart from a random value is the decisional version. Security rests on these being infeasible for a large, well-chosen prime, with the prime usually a "safe prime" (one where (p-1)/2 is also prime) so that no small subgroup weakens it. As with factoring for RSA, no fast classical algorithm is known, but a large quantum computer would break both, which is why post-quantum key exchange is now being standardised.
:::

::: context forwardsecrecy Forward secrecy
Forward secrecy means that compromising a long-term private key in the future does not expose past sessions. It comes from using a fresh, ephemeral Diffie-Hellman secret for each connection and throwing it away afterwards: the session key was derived from secrets that no longer exist, so there is nothing left to steal or to compute from the long-term key. Without it, an attacker can record encrypted traffic for years and decrypt all of it the day they obtain the server's key. Modern TLS makes ephemeral (EC)DHE effectively mandatory for exactly this reason, and messaging apps like Signal go further, rotating keys on every message.
:::
--- task
Build RSA signatures and Diffie-Hellman from `pow` and `hashlib`. These use toy-sized numbers and are for learning only; real code uses a vetted library.

- `rsa_sign(message, n, d)`: hash the `bytes` `message` with SHA-256, read the digest as a big-endian integer, reduce it modulo `n`, and return that value raised to `d` modulo `n`.
- `rsa_verify(message, signature, n, e)`: return `True` if `signature` is in the range 0 to n - 1 and `pow(signature, e, n)` equals the same reduced digest of `message`, and `False` otherwise.
- `dh_public(g, secret, p)`: return `g` raised to `secret` modulo `p` (a party's public value).
- `dh_shared(their_public, secret, p)`: return `their_public` raised to `secret` modulo `p` (the shared value both sides compute).

For example, with `n, e, d = 3233, 17, 2753`, a signature made by `rsa_sign` verifies with `rsa_verify`, and a change to the message makes it fail. With `p, g = 23, 5`, `dh_shared(dh_public(g, 15, p), 6, p)` equals `dh_shared(dh_public(g, 6, p), 15, p)`.
--- starter
def rsa_sign(message, n, d):
    raise NotImplementedError


def rsa_verify(message, signature, n, e):
    raise NotImplementedError


def dh_public(g, secret, p):
    raise NotImplementedError


def dh_shared(their_public, secret, p):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib


def _digest_int(message, n):
    return int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(message).digest(), "big") % n


def rsa_sign(message, n, d):
    return pow(_digest_int(message, n), d, n)


def rsa_verify(message, signature, n, e):
    if signature < 0 or signature >= n:
        return False
    return pow(signature, e, n) == _digest_int(message, n)


def dh_public(g, secret, p):
    return pow(g, secret, p)


def dh_shared(their_public, secret, p):
    return pow(their_public, secret, p)
--- hint
Write a helper that returns `int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(message).digest(), "big") % n`. Signing raises it to `d` mod `n`; verifying raises the signature to `e` mod `n` and compares to the same helper.
--- hint
Reject a signature outside 0 to n - 1 before the `pow`. The two Diffie-Hellman functions are each a single `pow(base, exponent, p)`.
--- check test | A signature verifies, and a changed message does not
(lambda n, e, d: (lambda s: rsa_verify(b"launch at 0400", s, n, e) and not rsa_verify(b"launch at 0500", s, n, e))(rsa_sign(b"launch at 0400", n, d)))(3233, 17, 2753)
--- check test | A large toy key round-trips
(lambda n, e, d: (lambda s: rsa_verify(b"open valve 3", s, n, e))(rsa_sign(b"open valve 3", n, d)))(1553796643855265753408399997158304397004945239137457885130325708591289381612562189403602142983569, 65537, 1053282416038482633640868185509785787285681328010269694527916461912235096949056652963229784958081)
--- check case | The signature on the large toy key is deterministic (no padding here)
rsa_sign(b"open valve 3", 1553796643855265753408399997158304397004945239137457885130325708591289381612562189403602142983569, 1053282416038482633640868185509785787285681328010269694527916461912235096949056652963229784958081)
=> 1246884374552091222932103157768432759048247638442104494930419457029235813646571228140480814159081
--- check test | Someone else's private key does not produce a valid signature
(lambda n, e, d_wrong: not rsa_verify(b"transfer funds", rsa_sign(b"transfer funds", n, d_wrong), n, e))(3233, 17, 2757)
--- check test | A signature at or beyond n is rejected without error
(lambda n, e: rsa_verify(b"x", n, n, e) is False and rsa_verify(b"x", -1, n, e) is False)(3233, 17)
--- check test | Both sides of Diffie-Hellman reach the same secret
(lambda p, g: dh_shared(dh_public(g, 15, p), 6, p) == dh_shared(dh_public(g, 6, p), 15, p))(23, 5)
--- check case | Known Diffie-Hellman values on p = 23, g = 5
(dh_public(5, 6, 23), dh_public(5, 15, 23), dh_shared(dh_public(5, 15, 23), 6, 23))
=> (8, 19, 2)
--- check test | A large safe-prime exchange agrees
(lambda p, g: dh_shared(dh_public(g, 123456789, p), 987654321, p) == dh_shared(dh_public(g, 987654321, p), 123456789, p))(14037981752521318643, 2)
--- check test | An eavesdropper's own secret gives a different value
(lambda p, g: dh_shared(dh_public(g, 15, p), 6, p) != dh_shared(dh_public(g, 15, p), 7, p))(23, 5)
--- check source | Signs the hash, not the raw message
sha256

+++ practice | Textbook RSA is deterministic
--- task
Show why raw RSA leaks a small message set. With a public key `(n, e)`, a value is encrypted as `pow(m, e, n)`. An attacker who knows the message is one of a few `candidates` can find which.

Write `guess_message(ciphertext, candidates, n, e)` that returns the element of the list `candidates` (each a small integer) whose encryption equals `ciphertext`, or `None` if none match. This works only because textbook RSA has no randomness.

For example, with `n, e = 3233, 17`, the encryption of `42` is `2557`, so `guess_message(2557, [0, 1, 42, 100], 3233, 17)` is `42`.
--- starter
def guess_message(ciphertext, candidates, n, e):
    return None
--- solution
def guess_message(ciphertext, candidates, n, e):
    for m in candidates:
        if pow(m, e, n) == ciphertext:
            return m
    return None
--- hint
Encrypt each candidate the same way the sender did, with `pow(m, e, n)`, and compare to the ciphertext.
--- hint
Return the first candidate that matches, or `None` after the loop.
--- check case | The example from the task
guess_message(2557, [0, 1, 42, 100], 3233, 17)
=> 42
--- check case | No candidate matches
guess_message(2557, [0, 1, 2], 3233, 17)
=> None
--- check case | A yes or no vote is trivially recovered
(lambda n, e: guess_message(pow(1, e, n), [0, 1], n, e))(3233, 17)
=> 1
--- check test | Finds the right one for every small message
(lambda n, e: all(guess_message(pow(m, e, n), list(range(20)), n, e) == m for m in range(20)))(3233, 17)
--- check case | An empty candidate list
guess_message(1, [], 3233, 17)
=> None

+++ practice | Signatures multiply: forging from two
--- task
Textbook RSA signatures (no hashing, no padding) are malleable. A signature on message m is `pow(m, d, n)`. Because signing is just a power, the product of the signatures of m1 and m2, modulo n, is a valid signature on the product `m1 * m2 mod n`.

Write `forge_product(sig1, sig2, n)` that returns a valid raw signature on `m1 * m2 mod n`, given only the two signatures `sig1` and `sig2` and the modulus `n`, without knowing `d`. This is why real signatures hash the message first: the product attack has no matching message.

For example, with `n, e, d = 3233, 17, 2753`, the raw signatures of `6` and `7` combine into the raw signature of `42`.
--- starter
def forge_product(sig1, sig2, n):
    return 0
--- solution
def forge_product(sig1, sig2, n):
    return sig1 * sig2 % n
--- hint
A raw signature is `m ** d mod n`. What is `(m1 ** d) * (m2 ** d)` equal to, modulo n?
--- hint
It is `(m1 * m2) ** d mod n`, so the forged signature is `sig1 * sig2 % n`.
--- check test | The forged signature verifies on the product message
(lambda n, e, d: (lambda raw: forge_product(raw(6), raw(7), n) == raw(6 * 7 % n))(lambda m: pow(m, d, n)))(3233, 17, 2753)
--- check test | It verifies with the public exponent
(lambda n, e, d: pow(forge_product(pow(6, d, n), pow(7, d, n), n), e, n) == 6 * 7 % n)(3233, 17, 2753)
--- check case | Two known raw signatures combine
forge_product(2902, 2667, 3233)
=> 3065
--- check test | The product wraps modulo n
(lambda n, e, d: (lambda raw: pow(forge_product(raw(100), raw(90), n), e, n) == 100 * 90 % n)(lambda m: pow(m, d, n)))(3233, 17, 2753)

+++ practice | Deriving the session key
--- task
The raw Diffie-Hellman number is not used as a key directly; it is hashed to uniform bytes. Write `session_key(p, shared, context)`:

- turn the integer `shared` into bytes, big-endian, using exactly `byte_length` bytes, where `byte_length` is `(p.bit_length() + 7) // 8` (the fixed size of a value modulo `p`, so both sides encode it identically);
- return the SHA-256 hex digest of `context` (a `bytes` label such as `b"orbit-link v1"`) followed by those bytes.

Using a fixed length matters: if one side dropped leading zero bytes and the other did not, they would hash different inputs and get different keys.

For example, `session_key(23, 2, b"x")` hashes `b"x" + (2).to_bytes(1, "big")`.
--- starter
def session_key(p, shared, context):
    return ""
--- solution
import hashlib


def session_key(p, shared, context):
