    byte_length = (p.bit_length() + 7) // 8
    return hashlib.sha256(context + shared.to_bytes(byte_length, "big")).hexdigest()
--- hint
The number of bytes is `(p.bit_length() + 7) // 8`. Encode `shared` with `shared.to_bytes(byte_length, "big")`.
--- hint
Concatenate `context` and those bytes, then take `hashlib.sha256(...).hexdigest()`.
--- check test | Matches hashing the bytes directly
session_key(23, 2, b"x") == __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"x" + (2).to_bytes(1, "big")).hexdigest()
--- check case | A known large-prime session key
session_key(14037981752521318643, 12471292723554852104, b"orbit-link v1")
=> "21d0cac9d1f9d20f8f2a0b255931df4530c857f0595d3d0a782e8a53adf750f8"
--- check test | Both Diffie-Hellman sides derive the same key
(lambda p, g: session_key(p, pow(pow(g, 6, p), 15, p), b"ctx") == session_key(p, pow(pow(g, 15, p), 6, p), b"ctx"))(23, 5)
--- check test | The context label changes the key
session_key(23, 2, b"link-a") != session_key(23, 2, b"link-b")
--- check test | The output is 64 hex characters
all(len(session_key(3233, s, b"c")) == 64 for s in (0, 1, 3232))
--- check test | Fixed length: a value with a leading zero byte still fills byte_length bytes
session_key(65537, 255, b"c") == __import__("hashlib").sha256(b"c" + (255).to_bytes(3, "big")).hexdigest()

+++ practice | Factor a toy key and recover the private exponent
--- task
The whole security of RSA is that n cannot be factored. For a **toy** key it can. Write `break_key(n, e)` that factors the modulus `n` (a product of two primes) by trial division, then returns the private exponent `d`.

Steps: find the smallest factor `p` of `n` at or above 2 by trial division; let `q = n // p`; compute `phi = (p - 1) * (q - 1)`; return `pow(e, -1, phi)`. You may assume `n` is a product of two primes and that `e` has an inverse.

For example, `break_key(3233, 17)` is `2753`, the private exponent for the textbook key.
--- starter
def break_key(n, e):
    return 0
--- solution
def break_key(n, e):
    p = 2
    while p * p <= n:
        if n % p == 0:
            break
        p += 1
    q = n // p
    phi = (p - 1) * (q - 1)
    return pow(e, -1, phi)
--- hint
Trial-divide from 2 upward until a factor divides `n`; the other factor is `n // p`.
--- hint
Then `phi = (p - 1) * (q - 1)` and the private exponent is `pow(e, -1, phi)`.
--- check case | The textbook key
break_key(3233, 17)
=> 2753
--- check test | The recovered d actually decrypts
(lambda n, e: (lambda d: pow(pow(42, e, n), d, n) == 42)(break_key(n, e)))(3233, 17)
--- check case | Another small key
break_key(3599, 31)
=> 3031
--- check test | Recovered d signs and verifies
(lambda n, e: (lambda d: pow(pow(12345, d, n), e, n) == 12345)(break_key(n, e)))(60013 * 60017, 65537)
--- check test | Works when the two primes are close
(lambda n, e: (lambda d: pow(pow(7, e, n), d, n) == 7)(break_key(n, e)))(101 * 103, 7)

+++ practice | Debug: the verifier that trusts any signature
--- task
This RSA signature verifier accepts forged signatures. It is meant to return `True` only when `signature` raised to `e` modulo `n` equals the SHA-256 digest of `message` read as an integer and reduced modulo `n`. Instead, `verify(b"anything", 0, n, e)` returns `True`, and so does a signature of `n` itself. The bug is that it compares the digest before reducing it modulo `n`, and it never checks the signature's range. Fix it so it reduces the digest modulo `n` and rejects any signature outside 0 to n - 1.
--- starter
import hashlib


def verify(message, signature, n, e):
    digest = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(message).digest(), "big")
    return pow(signature, e, n) == digest
--- solution
import hashlib


def verify(message, signature, n, e):
    if signature < 0 or signature >= n:
        return False
    digest = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(message).digest(), "big") % n
    return pow(signature, e, n) == digest
--- hint
`pow(signature, e, n)` is always between 0 and n - 1, but the digest is not reduced, so on a small `n` they can never match honestly, yet edge values slip through. Reduce the digest modulo `n`.
--- hint
Also reject a signature that is negative or at least `n` before doing the `pow`.
--- check test | A genuine signature still verifies
(lambda n, e, d: verify(b"real message", pow(int.from_bytes(__import__("hashlib").sha256(b"real message").digest(), "big") % n, d, n), n, e))(3233, 17, 2753)
--- check test | Zero and n are no longer accepted
(lambda n, e: verify(b"anything", 0, n, e) is False and verify(b"anything", n, n, e) is False)(3233, 17)
--- check test | A random wrong signature fails
(lambda n, e: verify(b"launch", 1234, n, e) is False)(3233, 17)
--- check test | Verifies against the large toy key
(lambda n, e, d: verify(b"open valve 3", pow(int.from_bytes(__import__("hashlib").sha256(b"open valve 3").digest(), "big") % n, d, n), n, e))(1553796643855265753408399997158304397004945239137457885130325708591289381612562189403602142983569, 65537, 1053282416038482633640868185509785787285681328010269694527916461912235096949056652963229784958081)

=== sec-10 | Protocols: replay, nonces, man-in-the-middle, and how TLS defends
--- teach
The last three lessons built the primitives: slow password hashes, authenticated encryption, signatures and key exchange. A **protocol** is the choreography that puts them together: who sends what, in what order, and what each side checks before trusting the next step. Getting the primitives right is not enough; almost every real break is a protocol flaw. This lesson covers the attacks that target the conversation itself, and how TLS, the protocol behind HTTPS, defends against them.

### The network is the attacker

Assume a **[[Dolev-Yao attacker|dolevyao]]**: someone who controls the network completely. They can read every message, drop it, delay it, replay an old one, reorder a stream, and inject messages of their own. They cannot break the cryptography, so a correct MAC or signature they did not have the key for is still unforgeable. Everything below is about what such an attacker can do **without** breaking any primitive, just by playing with the messages.

### Replay attacks

The plainest attack keeps the cryptography intact. An attacker records a valid message and sends it again later.

Suppose the ground station sends a signed, authenticated command `OPEN VALVE 3`. The signature is valid; the command is genuine. But if the spacecraft accepts any correctly signed command, the attacker can record it and **replay** it an hour later, or a thousand times, opening the valve again and again. Nothing was forged; a real message was reused out of context. Authentication answers "is this a genuine message?"; it does not answer "**is this message fresh, and have I seen it before?**"

The fixes all add something that makes each message usable exactly once:

- a **nonce** (number used once): the receiver remembers nonces it has seen and rejects repeats;
- a **sequence number** or **counter**: messages must arrive in strictly increasing order, so a replayed old one has a stale number;
- a **timestamp** with a narrow acceptance window: a message older than a few seconds is refused (this needs synchronised clocks);
- a fresh, **[[server-chosen challenge|challenge]]** the client must incorporate, so a reply only fits the one request that provoked it.

The freshness value must be **covered by the MAC or signature**. If the counter is authenticated but the nonce is sent in the clear beside the signature, the attacker just changes it. Bind the anti-replay value into the same tag as the message.

### Nonces done wrong

A nonce is only as good as its uniqueness.

- **A predictable nonce** (a small counter the attacker can guess) lets them precompute responses or set traps for future values.
- **A reused nonce** is the two-time-pad disaster from lesson 8 all over again in AES-GCM, and worse, in GCM it also leaks the authentication key.
- **A too-short random nonce** collides by the birthday bound: 64 random bits are expected to repeat after about 2^32 messages, which a busy server reaches.

The rule: a nonce must be **unique** for the lifetime of a key. Either count carefully (and never reset the counter, e.g. after a reboot) or draw enough random bits (96 or more) that a collision is astronomically unlikely.

### Challenge-response: proving freshness and identity at once

To prove that the party on the other end holds a secret **right now**, and not that they once recorded a valid message, use **challenge-response**. The verifier sends a fresh random **challenge**; the prover returns a MAC of it under the shared key. Because the challenge is new every time and unpredictable, an old recording never matches.

```python
import hmac, hashlib
def respond(key, challenge):
    return hmac.new(key, b"resp" + challenge, hashlib.sha256).digest()
key = b"shared-secret"
challenge = b"\x9f\x0c...16 fresh random bytes..."
answer = respond(key, challenge)
print(hmac.compare_digest(respond(key, challenge), answer))   # True
```

This proves the prover knows the key and is live, without ever sending the key. It is how Wi-Fi WPA2's four-way handshake and many hardware tokens work. A subtlety: label what is being signed (the `b"resp"` prefix, or better a full transcript) so a challenge in one direction cannot be replayed as a response in the other, the **reflection attack**.

### Man-in-the-middle

The deepest attack targets key exchange itself. Recall from last lesson that Diffie-Hellman gives a shared key with **somebody**, but does not say who. Picture the attacker M sitting between A and B:

- A sends its public value g^a; M intercepts it and sends B its own g^m instead;
- B sends g^b; M intercepts it and sends A g^m;
- now A shares a key with M, and B shares a different key with M.

M decrypts everything from A, reads or alters it, re-encrypts it for B, and vice versa. Both sides believe they have a private channel. Every primitive worked perfectly; the flaw is that **nothing tied the key exchange to an identity.**

The only cure is **authentication of the exchange**: each side must verify that the public value really came from the party it thinks it is talking to. That verification has to root in something known ahead of time, a pre-shared key, or, at Internet scale, a trusted **certificate**.

### Certificates and the chain of trust

A **certificate** is a public key plus a name (like `bank.example.com`), signed by a **certificate authority** (CA). Your browser and operating system ship with a set of **root CA** public keys they trust. When a server presents its certificate, the browser checks the CA's signature on it with the CA's public key it already holds. Roots sign intermediates that sign server certificates, forming a **chain** the browser verifies link by link up to a trusted root.

This turns "trust this stranger's key" into "trust that a CA verified this key belongs to this name", moving trust to a small, audited set of roots. It is exactly the signatures of lesson 9 used to bootstrap trust in an unknown key. **[[Certificate transparency|ct]]** logs make every issued certificate public so a bogus one is detectable.

### How TLS ties it together

The TLS 1.3 handshake (you met TLS in Computer Networks) combines every idea in this course:

1. The client sends a random **nonce** and its ephemeral Diffie-Hellman public value.
2. The server replies with its own nonce and ephemeral public value, its **certificate**, and a **signature over the whole handshake transcript** so far.
3. The client verifies the certificate chain to a trusted root, then verifies the signature against the key in the certificate. This defeats the man in the middle: M cannot forge the server's signature over M's substituted public value, because M does not have the server's private key.
4. Both sides derive the same session keys from the Diffie-Hellman shared secret and the two nonces, through a key-derivation function, and switch to AES-GCM or ChaCha20-Poly1305.
5. Each record carries an implicit **sequence number** folded into its nonce, so a replayed or reordered record fails its authentication tag.

So: **ephemeral Diffie-Hellman** gives a fresh secret with forward secrecy; the **signature over the transcript** authenticates it and stops the man in the middle; the **certificate chain** roots that signature in a trusted key; the two **nonces** make every session's keys unique; **authenticated encryption** with **sequence numbers** protects and orders the data and blocks replay. Every earlier lesson appears here.

### What it costs

Anti-replay by counter costs O(1) per message (compare and increment). A nonce cache that remembers every nonce costs memory that grows without bound, so real systems bound it with a sliding window (accept sequence numbers within a range of the highest seen, like TCP) or a time window. The handshake pays a few public-key operations once per connection; after that everything is fast symmetric cryptography.

### Where it is used

- **TLS/HTTPS**, the example above, protects the web, email transport and most app traffic.
- **SSH** authenticates the server by a key you trust on first use and the user by a signature.
- **Kerberos** uses timestamps and short-lived tickets to prevent replay across a network of services.
- **Spacecraft telecommand:** the CCSDS standards add an authenticated, monotonically increasing frame counter so a recorded command cannot be replayed to a satellite.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- **Authenticating a message but not its freshness.** A valid MAC does not stop replay; bind a counter, nonce or timestamp into the tag.
- **Sending the anti-replay value outside the signature**, where the attacker can change it.
- **Unauthenticated key exchange.** Plain Diffie-Hellman with no signature or certificate is wide open to a man in the middle.
- **Not verifying the certificate chain**, or ignoring a name mismatch or an expired certificate. A famous class of mobile-app bugs skipped this and trusted any certificate.
- **A nonce cache that grows forever**, or a counter that resets on reboot.

::: context dolevyao The Dolev-Yao model
Named after a 1983 paper by Danny Dolev and Andrew Yao, this is the standard threat model for reasoning about protocols. The attacker is assumed to be the network itself: they carry every message, so they can read, drop, delay, reorder, replay and inject at will, and they can combine and take apart messages using any keys they legitimately hold. What they cannot do is break the cryptography: without the key, a ciphertext is opaque and a MAC or signature is unforgeable. Analysing a protocol means showing that even this all-powerful network cannot make an honest party accept something false. It is deliberately pessimistic, because the real Internet nearly is.
:::

::: context challenge Why a challenge must be fresh and unpredictable
A challenge-response exchange proves the other side holds a secret right now. For that, the challenge must be both new every time and hard to guess. If it repeats, an attacker who once saw the matching response simply replays it. If it is predictable, the attacker can ask the honest prover for the response to a future challenge in advance, then use it when that challenge comes up, the "pre-play" attack. Drawing the challenge from a cryptographic random source with enough bits (128 is typical) makes both problems vanish. The same reasoning is why TLS uses fresh random nonces from both client and server in every handshake.
:::

::: context ct Certificate transparency
Because any of hundreds of certificate authorities can sign a certificate for any name, a single compromised or dishonest CA could issue a valid certificate for your bank. Certificate transparency, deployed from 2013, requires CAs to publish every certificate they issue to public, append-only logs built on Merkle trees (lesson 6). Browsers refuse certificates that are not in a log. A domain owner can then watch the logs and spot any certificate issued for their name that they did not request, catching a misissuance quickly. It is a good example of using an integrity structure and public accountability to shore up a trust system that would otherwise depend on every CA being perfect.
:::
--- task
Model an authenticated, replay-proof message channel with a sequence number, built on `hmac` and `hashlib.sha256`.

- `frame_tag(key, seq, message)`: return `hmac.new(key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + message, hashlib.sha256).digest()`, the tag binding a message to its sequence number.
- `make_frame(key, seq, message)`: return the tuple `(seq, message, frame_tag(key, seq, message))`.
- Write a class `Receiver` created with `Receiver(key)`. It has a method `accept(seq, message, tag)` that returns `True` and advances the channel only when: `seq` equals the next expected number (starting at 0), and `tag` matches `frame_tag(key, seq, message)`, compared with `hmac.compare_digest`. Otherwise it returns `False` and does not advance. So a replayed or out-of-order frame, or one with a bad tag, is rejected, and the next expected number only ever moves forward on an accepted frame.

For example, a receiver accepts frame 0, then frame 1, but rejects frame 0 replayed, and rejects frame 1 if it arrives before frame 0.
--- starter
def frame_tag(key, seq, message):
    raise NotImplementedError


def make_frame(key, seq, message):
    raise NotImplementedError


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, key):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def accept(self, seq, message, tag):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def frame_tag(key, seq, message):
    return hmac.new(key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + message, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def make_frame(key, seq, message):
    return (seq, message, frame_tag(key, seq, message))


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.expected = 0

    def accept(self, seq, message, tag):
        if seq != self.expected:
            return False
        if not hmac.compare_digest(frame_tag(self.key, seq, message), tag):
            return False
        self.expected += 1
        return True
--- hint
`frame_tag` HMACs the 8-byte big-endian sequence number followed by the message. `make_frame` returns the triple with that tag.
--- hint
The receiver keeps `self.expected`, starting at 0. `accept` returns `False` unless `seq == self.expected` and the tag matches; only then does it increment `self.expected`.
--- check case | Tags bind the sequence number, so moving a message to another slot fails
frame_tag(b"k", 0, b"ab") == frame_tag(b"k", 1, b"ab")
=> False
--- check case | A known frame tag
frame_tag(b"k", 0, b"a").hex()
=> "353a3b535a7932eebaa4c9ba2538d03f75d66149abf50e1a516bae4c9d08cc9d"
--- check test | make_frame produces frames a fresh receiver accepts in order
(lambda key: (lambda r: [r.accept(*make_frame(key, i, m)) for i, m in enumerate([b"a", b"b", b"c"])] == [True, True, True])(Receiver(key)))(b"k")
--- check test | A replayed frame is rejected
(lambda key: (lambda r: (r.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"go")), r.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"go"))) == (True, False))(Receiver(key)))(b"k")
--- check test | An out-of-order frame is rejected and does not advance
(lambda key: (lambda r: (r.accept(*make_frame(key, 1, b"b")), r.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"a")), r.accept(*make_frame(key, 1, b"b"))) == (False, True, True))(Receiver(key)))(b"k")
--- check test | A tampered message with a stale tag is rejected
(lambda key: (lambda r: r.accept(0, b"HACKED", frame_tag(key, 0, b"go")) is False and r.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"go")) is True)(Receiver(key)))(b"k")
--- check test | A wrong key never authenticates
(lambda: (lambda r: r.accept(*make_frame(b"attacker", 0, b"go")) is False)(Receiver(b"real")))()
--- check test | Two receivers with the same key are independent
(lambda key: (lambda a, b: a.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"x")) and b.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"y")) and not b.accept(*make_frame(key, 0, b"y")))(Receiver(key), Receiver(key)))(b"k")
--- check source | Uses constant-time comparison
compare_digest

+++ practice | Challenge-response login
--- task
Model a challenge-response check. The server sends a fresh random `challenge` (bytes); the client answers with `hmac.new(key, b"login:" + challenge, hashlib.sha256).digest()`.

- `respond(key, challenge)`: return that HMAC.
- `check(key, challenge, answer)`: return `True` if `answer` equals `respond(key, challenge)`, compared with `hmac.compare_digest`, else `False`.
- `replay_works(key, challenge_old, challenge_new, answer_old)`: an attacker recorded `answer_old`, the response to `challenge_old`, and the server now issues `challenge_new`. Return whether the old answer is accepted for the new challenge, that is `check(key, challenge_new, answer_old)`. For different challenges this is `False`, which is the point.

For example, `check(key, c, respond(key, c))` is `True`, and `replay_works(key, c1, c2, respond(key, c1))` is `False` when `c1 != c2`.
--- starter
def respond(key, challenge):
    raise NotImplementedError


def check(key, challenge, answer):
    raise NotImplementedError


def replay_works(key, challenge_old, challenge_new, answer_old):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def respond(key, challenge):
    return hmac.new(key, b"login:" + challenge, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def check(key, challenge, answer):
    return hmac.compare_digest(respond(key, challenge), answer)


def replay_works(key, challenge_old, challenge_new, answer_old):
    return check(key, challenge_new, answer_old)
--- hint
`respond` HMACs `b"login:" + challenge`. `check` recomputes it and compares with `hmac.compare_digest`.
--- hint
`replay_works` is just `check(key, challenge_new, answer_old)`; it returns `True` only when the two challenges happen to be equal.
--- check test | A correct response passes
(lambda key, c: check(key, c, respond(key, c)))(b"k", b"\x01\x02\x03\x04")
--- check test | A wrong key fails
(lambda c: not check(b"real", c, respond(b"fake", c)))(b"\x01\x02\x03\x04")
--- check test | A replayed answer fails against a new challenge
replay_works(b"k", b"chal-one", b"chal-two", respond(b"k", b"chal-one")) is False
--- check test | The same challenge would accept the old answer (why it must be fresh)
replay_works(b"k", b"same", b"same", respond(b"k", b"same")) is True
--- check case | The response is 32 bytes
len(respond(b"k", b"abc"))
=> 32
--- check test | Answering a different challenge does not match
(lambda key: not check(key, b"c1", respond(key, b"c2")))(b"k")

+++ practice | A nonce cache with a sliding window
--- task
Remembering every nonce forever costs unbounded memory. A **sliding window** bounds it: accept a sequence number only if it is greater than `highest - window` and has not been seen, where `highest` is the largest accepted so far. Numbers that fall too far behind the window are refused.

Write a class `Window` created with `Window(size)` (size at least 1). Its method `accept(seq)` (seq a non-negative integer) returns `True` and records `seq` if it is new and not older than `size - 1` below the current highest accepted; otherwise `False`. Before anything is accepted, treat the highest as -1, so `accept(0)` succeeds. Accepting a number far ahead slides the window forward and forgets numbers that fall off the back.

For example, with size 4, accepting 0, 1, 2 then replaying 1 gives `True, True, True, False`; then accepting 10 slides the window, and 6 (now off the back) is refused while 7 (still just inside) is accepted.
--- starter
class Window:
    def __init__(self, size):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def accept(self, seq):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
class Window:
    def __init__(self, size):
        self.size = size
        self.highest = -1
        self.seen = set()

    def accept(self, seq):
        if seq <= self.highest - self.size:
            return False
        if seq in self.seen:
            return False
        self.seen.add(seq)
        if seq > self.highest:
            self.highest = seq
        self.seen = {s for s in self.seen if s > self.highest - self.size}
        return True
--- hint
Track the highest accepted number and a set of seen numbers within the window. Reject `seq` if it is at or below `highest - size`, or already seen.
--- hint
After accepting, update the highest, then drop from the set any number that is now older than `highest - size + 1`.
--- check test | In-order acceptance and a replay
(lambda w: [w.accept(s) for s in (0, 1, 2, 1)] == [True, True, True, False])(Window(4))
--- check test | Sliding forward forgets old numbers
(lambda w: [w.accept(s) for s in (0, 1, 2, 10, 6, 7)] == [True, True, True, True, False, True])(Window(4))
--- check test | A number just inside the window is accepted, just outside refused
(lambda w: ([w.accept(s) for s in (0, 5)], w.accept(2), w.accept(1)) == ([True, True], True, False))(Window(4))
--- check case | Size one accepts only strictly increasing numbers
(lambda w: [w.accept(s) for s in (0, 1, 1, 2, 2, 5)])(Window(1))
=> [True, True, False, True, False, True]
--- check test | The seen set never grows past the window size
(lambda w: (all(w.accept(s) for s in range(100)), len(w.seen) <= 5)[1])(Window(5))
--- check test | Out-of-order within the window all pass once
(lambda w: [w.accept(s) for s in (5, 3, 4, 2, 3)] == [True, True, True, True, False])(Window(4))

+++ practice | Detecting the man in the middle by a key fingerprint
--- task
After a Diffie-Hellman exchange, two honest parties share the same key, but a man in the middle leaves each side with a *different* key. Comparing a **fingerprint** of the key over an authenticated side channel (reading it aloud, a pre-shared value) reveals the attack.

Write `fingerprint(shared_int)`: return the first 8 hex characters of the SHA-256 digest of `shared_int.to_bytes(32, "big")` (assume the value fits in 32 bytes). Then write `channel_safe(a_shared, b_shared)`: return `True` if the two sides' shared integers have the same fingerprint (no man in the middle), else `False`.

For example, two equal shared values give equal fingerprints and `channel_safe` is `True`; the split keys a man in the middle produces give different fingerprints and `False`.
--- starter
def fingerprint(shared_int):
    raise NotImplementedError


def channel_safe(a_shared, b_shared):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib


def fingerprint(shared_int):
    return hashlib.sha256(shared_int.to_bytes(32, "big")).hexdigest()[:8]


def channel_safe(a_shared, b_shared):
    return fingerprint(a_shared) == fingerprint(b_shared)
--- hint
`fingerprint` encodes the integer as 32 big-endian bytes, hashes with SHA-256, and takes the first 8 hex characters.
--- hint
`channel_safe` compares the two fingerprints for equality.
--- check test | Equal shared values are safe
channel_safe(123456789, 123456789) is True
--- check test | Different shared values (a man in the middle) are caught
channel_safe(123456789, 987654321) is False
--- check case | A known fingerprint
fingerprint(2)
=> "9267d3db"
--- check test | The fingerprint is 8 hex characters
all(len(fingerprint(v)) == 8 and all(c in "0123456789abcdef" for c in fingerprint(v)) for v in (0, 1, 2 ** 100, 2 ** 255 - 1))
--- check test | A real split from a man in the middle is detected
(lambda p, g: channel_safe(pow(pow(g, 6, p), 99, p), pow(pow(g, 99, p), 15, p)) is False)(2147483647, 7)

+++ practice | Timestamp freshness with a window
--- task
Some protocols use timestamps for freshness instead of counters. A message carries the time it was sent; the receiver accepts it only if it is close to the receiver's clock and has not been seen before (to stop a fast replay within the window).

Write a class `FreshCheck` created with `FreshCheck(window)`. Its method `accept(msg_id, sent_time, now)` returns `True` only when `abs(now - sent_time) <= window` and this `msg_id` has not already been accepted; otherwise `False`. Record every accepted `msg_id`.

For example, with window 5, a message sent at time 100 checked at 103 is accepted; the same `msg_id` again is refused (replay); a message sent at 100 checked at 200 is refused (too old).
--- starter
class FreshCheck:
    def __init__(self, window):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def accept(self, msg_id, sent_time, now):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
class FreshCheck:
    def __init__(self, window):
        self.window = window
        self.seen = set()

    def accept(self, msg_id, sent_time, now):
        if abs(now - sent_time) > self.window:
            return False
        if msg_id in self.seen:
            return False
        self.seen.add(msg_id)
        return True
--- hint
Check the time difference with `abs(now - sent_time) <= window` first, then whether the id was seen.
--- hint
Only record the id and return `True` when both the time is within the window and the id is new.
--- check test | A fresh message is accepted
(lambda f: f.accept("m1", 100, 103))(FreshCheck(5))
--- check test | A replay of the same id is refused
(lambda f: (f.accept("m1", 100, 103), f.accept("m1", 100, 104)) == (True, False))(FreshCheck(5))
--- check test | A message from too long ago is refused
(lambda f: f.accept("old", 100, 200) is False)(FreshCheck(5))
--- check test | A message from the future beyond the window is refused
(lambda f: f.accept("future", 200, 100) is False)(FreshCheck(5))
--- check case | Exactly on the window boundary is accepted
(lambda f: (f.accept("a", 100, 105), f.accept("b", 100, 95)))(FreshCheck(5))
=> (True, True)
--- check test | Different ids at the same time are all accepted
(lambda f: [f.accept(i, 100, 100) for i in ("x", "y", "z")] == [True, True, True])(FreshCheck(5))

+++ practice | Debug: the tag that forgets the sequence number
--- task
This channel is meant to stop an attacker from moving an authenticated message to a different position in the stream, by binding each message to its sequence number in the tag. But the tag does not depend on the sequence number, so a frame `(seq, message, tag)` still verifies at any position. As a result `verify(key, 5, b"go", frame_tag(key, 0, b"go"))` wrongly returns `True`. Fix `frame_tag` so the tag covers the sequence number (8 big-endian bytes) as well as the message, and leave `verify` as it is.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def frame_tag(key, seq, message):
    return hmac.new(key, message, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def verify(key, seq, message, tag):
    return hmac.compare_digest(frame_tag(key, seq, message), tag)
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def frame_tag(key, seq, message):
    return hmac.new(key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + message, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def verify(key, seq, message, tag):
    return hmac.compare_digest(frame_tag(key, seq, message), tag)
--- hint
The HMAC input must include the sequence number, not only the message.
--- hint
Prepend `seq.to_bytes(8, "big")` to the message inside the HMAC.
--- check test | A tag no longer verifies at a different sequence number
(lambda key: verify(key, 5, b"go", frame_tag(key, 0, b"go")) is False)(b"k")
--- check test | The right position still verifies
(lambda key: verify(key, 5, b"go", frame_tag(key, 5, b"go")))(b"k")
--- check test | Different sequence numbers give different tags
(lambda key: frame_tag(key, 0, b"m") != frame_tag(key, 1, b"m"))(b"k")
--- check test | The message still matters
(lambda key: verify(key, 3, b"tampered", frame_tag(key, 3, b"go")) is False)(b"k")

+++ practice | Order the handshake steps
--- task
Given the security goal of each step, put the TLS 1.3 handshake steps in the order that actually defends the connection. You are given `steps`, a dict from a step's name to a short description, and must return the list of step names in the correct order.

Write `handshake_order()` that returns exactly this list, in this order:
`["client_hello", "server_hello", "verify_certificate", "verify_signature", "derive_keys", "encrypted_data"]`.

The reasoning to encode: the client and server first exchange nonces and Diffie-Hellman values (`client_hello`, then `server_hello` which also carries the certificate and a signature over the transcript); the client must verify the certificate chain **before** trusting the key in it (`verify_certificate`), then verify the transcript signature against that key (`verify_signature`) to defeat a man in the middle; only then derive the session keys (`derive_keys`); and only then send application data under authenticated encryption (`encrypted_data`). This is a fixed sequence, so the function takes no arguments.
--- starter
def handshake_order():
    return []
--- solution
def handshake_order():
    return [
        "client_hello",
        "server_hello",
        "verify_certificate",
        "verify_signature",
        "derive_keys",
        "encrypted_data",
    ]
--- hint
The client cannot trust the key until it has checked the certificate chain, and cannot check the transcript signature until it has that key.
--- hint
Keys are derived only after the exchange is authenticated, and data flows only after keys exist.
--- check case | The full order
handshake_order()
=> ["client_hello", "server_hello", "verify_certificate", "verify_signature", "derive_keys", "encrypted_data"]
--- check test | The certificate is verified before the signature that relies on it
(lambda o: o.index("verify_certificate") < o.index("verify_signature"))(handshake_order())
--- check test | Keys are derived only after authentication, and data only after keys
(lambda o: o.index("verify_signature") < o.index("derive_keys") < o.index("encrypted_data"))(handshake_order())
--- check test | Both hellos come first, client before server
(lambda o: o[0] == "client_hello" and o[1] == "server_hello")(handshake_order())

=== sec-11 | Authentication and authorisation: sessions, tokens and least privilege
--- teach
Last lesson secured the conversation between two machines. This lesson is about the two questions a system asks once a request arrives: **who are you** (authentication) and **what are you allowed to do** (authorisation). They are different, they fail differently, and confusing them is the source of a huge share of real breaches. You will build signed tokens and a permission check from the tools of lessons 6 to 10.

### Authentication versus authorisation

- **Authentication** (authn) establishes *identity*: proving you are who you claim. Lesson 7's password check is authentication; so is a signature, a hardware key, or a one-time code.
- **Authorisation** (authz) decides *permission*: given who you are, may you do this action to this resource? Reading your own file is allowed; deleting someone else's may not be.

A door with a photo ID check is authentication; the list of which rooms your badge opens is authorisation. Authenticating a user says nothing about what they may do, and every request must be authorised, not just the login.

### The core mistake: trusting the client

The internet's first rule of authorisation: **never trust data the client can change to decide what it is allowed to do.** A hidden form field `role=user`, a cookie `is_admin=false`, a price in the request body, an object id in the URL: all of these are under the attacker's control. The check must be made on the server against state the server controls.

The commonest form is the **[[insecure direct object reference|idor]]** (IDOR): a request for `/invoice/1481` is served without checking that invoice 1481 belongs to *this* user. The attacker just tries 1482. The identity was authenticated; the authorisation was skipped.

### Sessions: remembering a login

HTTP is stateless, so after you log in the server needs a way to recognise you on the next request without asking for your password again. Two designs.

**Server-side sessions.** On login the server generates a large random **session id**, stores the user's data under it server-side, and sends the id to the browser as a cookie. Each request carries the cookie; the server looks the id up.

- The id must be **unguessable**: from a cryptographic random source (`secrets`), long enough (128 bits) that it cannot be brute-forced or predicted. A sequential or timestamp-based id is a **session-prediction** hole.
- The cookie is marked `HttpOnly` (JavaScript cannot read it, limiting XSS theft from lesson 5), `Secure` (sent only over HTTPS), and `SameSite` (limits the CSRF of lesson 5).
- Logout, and expiry, delete the server-side record, so the id stops working immediately.

**Tokens (stateless).** Instead of storing state, the server hands the client a **signed token** carrying the identity, and verifies the signature on each request. Nothing is stored server-side. This is what a **JWT** (JSON Web Token) is: a JSON payload plus a MAC or signature. The trade-off: tokens scale (any server with the key can verify one, no shared database) but are hard to revoke before they expire, because there is no server record to delete.

### Building a signed token

A token is a payload the client may read but cannot forge. The pattern, straight from lesson 6's HMAC:

1. serialise the payload (who, when it expires, what roles) to bytes;
2. append a MAC of those bytes under a server key;
3. to read it back, recompute the MAC and compare with `hmac.compare_digest` before trusting anything inside.

```python
import hmac, hashlib, base64, json
def b64u(b):
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(b).rstrip(b"=").decode()
key = b"server-signing-key"
body = b64u(json.dumps({"user": "ada"}, sort_keys=True).encode())
sig = b64u(hmac.new(key, body.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest())
print(body + "." + sig)
```

The payload is only **encoded**, not encrypted: anyone can read it, so never put a secret in a plain token. The signature guarantees **integrity and authenticity**: the client cannot change the user or role and still have a valid MAC. This is why a token needs no server-side storage; the MAC is the proof.

An **[[expiry|expiry]]** (`exp`) is essential. Without it a stolen token is valid forever. The server checks the expiry after verifying the signature. Short-lived **access tokens** plus a longer, revocable **refresh token** are the usual balance between scale and control.

**Watch out for the "alg=none" bug.** Early JWT libraries let the token itself name its algorithm, and accepted a token that said "algorithm: none" with no signature at all. The lesson: the *server* decides the algorithm; never let the attacker's input choose how it is verified.

### Authorisation models

Once you know who the user is, decide what they may do.

- **Access control lists (ACLs):** each resource lists who may do what to it. Flexible, but scattered.
- **Role-based access control (RBAC):** users get **roles** (viewer, operator, admin), and permissions attach to roles. A user's permissions are the union over their roles. This is the workhorse of most systems, because roles are few and stable while users are many.
- **Attribute-based (ABAC):** rules over attributes of the user, resource and context ("a flight director may send a command during a pass window"). Most expressive, hardest to reason about.

RBAC in a few lines: map each role to its permissions, and a user is allowed an action if any of their roles grants it.

### Least privilege and defence in depth

The organising principle is **least privilege**: every user, process and component gets the **minimum** rights it needs to do its job, and no more. If the telemetry-display service is compromised, it should not be able to send commands, because it never needed to. Least privilege turns a total compromise into a limited one. It shows up everywhere: a database account that can only read the tables it uses, a container that drops every capability it does not need, an API token scoped to one repository.

Related ideas:

- **Fail closed:** when an authorisation check errors or is uncertain, deny. A check that defaults to "allow" on an exception is a backdoor.
- **Complete mediation:** check **every** access, every time, not just the first. Caching "this user is allowed" and skipping later checks is how privilege-escalation bugs happen.
- **Separation of privilege:** require two independent conditions for a dangerous action (two-person rule for a launch command, MFA for a money transfer).
- **Defence in depth:** never rely on one control. A user-facing check *and* a server check *and* a database permission, so one mistake is not fatal.

**[[Multi-factor authentication|mfa]]** (MFA) applies separation of privilege to login: something you know (password), something you have (a phone or hardware key), something you are (a fingerprint). A stolen password alone is then not enough. **TOTP** codes and hardware security keys (WebAuthn) are the common second factors.

### What it costs

Verifying a signed token is one HMAC, O(n) in the payload size, with no database lookup, which is why tokens scale. Server-side sessions cost one storage lookup per request but can be revoked instantly. An RBAC check is a set union and membership test over a user's roles, effectively O(1) for the small numbers involved.

### Where it is used

- **Every web app** uses sessions or tokens; OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect (the "log in with Google" flows) issue scoped tokens between services.
- **Cloud platforms** (AWS IAM, Kubernetes RBAC) are giant authorisation systems built on roles and least privilege.
- **Operating systems** enforce least privilege with user accounts, capabilities and sandboxes (the OS course's user/kernel split).
- **Mission control:** roles separate who may view telemetry, who may plan, and who may transmit a command, with a two-person rule on the most dangerous ones.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- **Confusing authn and authz:** authenticating a user and then not checking, per request, that they may do this specific thing (IDOR).
- **Trusting client-supplied role, price or id fields.** Decide on the server from server state.
- **Guessable session ids**, or ids that never expire, or that survive logout.
- **Putting secrets in a token payload**, which is only encoded; and letting the token choose its own verification algorithm (`alg=none`).
- **Over-broad privileges:** an "admin" service account used everywhere, a token scoped to everything. Grant the least that works.
- **Comparing tokens or MACs with `==`.** Use `hmac.compare_digest`.

::: context idor Insecure direct object references
An insecure direct object reference is when an application uses a client-supplied identifier (a number in a URL, a filename, a database key) to fetch a resource without checking that the authenticated user is actually allowed that particular resource. You log in as yourself, then change `/account/1001/statement` to `/account/1002/statement` and read someone else's statement. The user was authenticated, but the authorisation check "does this object belong to this user?" was missing. It sits near the top of the OWASP list year after year because it is so easy to introduce: the happy path works perfectly in testing, and the hole only shows when someone edits the id. The fix is to scope every lookup to the current user, or to check ownership before returning anything.
:::

::: context expiry Why tokens must expire
A signed token is a bearer credential: whoever holds it is treated as the user, no questions asked. If it never expires, a token leaked once, through a log file, a browser history, a proxy, a stolen backup, is a permanent skeleton key. An expiry time bounds the damage: a token stolen today is useless tomorrow. But expiry fights with the stateless design, because a valid, unexpired token cannot easily be revoked when there is no server record to delete. The usual answer is two tokens: a short-lived access token (minutes) that does the work, and a longer refresh token held more carefully and checked against a revocation list, so a compromise is both limited in time and revocable.
:::

::: context mfa Something you know, have, and are
Multi-factor authentication asks for evidence from two or more independent categories: something you know (a password or PIN), something you have (a phone running a code generator, or a hardware security key), and something you are (a fingerprint or face). The strength is independence: an attacker who phishes your password still lacks your physical key, and one who steals your laptop still lacks your password. Time-based one-time passwords (TOTP) derive a six-digit code from a shared secret and the current time, so a code works only for about thirty seconds. Hardware keys using the WebAuthn standard go further and sign a challenge from the actual site, which makes them resistant even to a convincing phishing page, because the signature is bound to the real domain.
:::
--- task
Build stateless signed tokens and an RBAC check from `hmac`, `hashlib`, `base64` and `json`.

- `make_token(key, payload)`: `payload` is a dict of JSON-serialisable values. Serialise it with `json.dumps(payload, separators=(",", ":"), sort_keys=True)`, encode the UTF-8 bytes with URL-safe base64 **without** padding `=` (a helper `b64url` is described below), then return `body + "." + signature`, where `signature` is the URL-safe-base64, unpadded HMAC-SHA256 of the body's bytes under `key`.
- `read_token(key, token)`: split on `.`; if there is not exactly one `.`, return `None`. Recompute the signature over the body and compare with `hmac.compare_digest`; if it differs, return `None`. Otherwise decode the body and return the payload dict. If anything fails to decode, return `None`.
- `can(roles, permissions_by_role, action)`: `roles` is a list of the user's role names, `permissions_by_role` maps a role name to a list of permission strings. Return `True` if `action` is in the union of the permissions of the user's roles, else `False`. An unknown role contributes nothing.

For `b64url`, use `base64.urlsafe_b64encode(data).rstrip(b"=").decode()` to encode and, to decode a string `s`, `base64.urlsafe_b64decode(s + "=" * (-len(s) % 4))`.

For example, `read_token(k, make_token(k, {"user": "ada"}))` is `{"user": "ada"}`, and a token verified with the wrong key gives `None`. `can(["operator"], {"operator": ["read", "command"]}, "command")` is `True`.
--- starter
def make_token(key, payload):
    raise NotImplementedError


def read_token(key, token):
    raise NotImplementedError


def can(roles, permissions_by_role, action):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import base64
import hashlib
import hmac
import json


def _b64url(data):
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(data).rstrip(b"=").decode()


def _b64url_decode(s):
    return base64.urlsafe_b64decode(s + "=" * (-len(s) % 4))


def make_token(key, payload):
    body = _b64url(json.dumps(payload, separators=(",", ":"), sort_keys=True).encode())
    signature = _b64url(hmac.new(key, body.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest())
    return body + "." + signature


def read_token(key, token):
    if token.count(".") != 1:
        return None
    body, signature = token.split(".")
    expected = _b64url(hmac.new(key, body.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest())
    if not hmac.compare_digest(signature, expected):
        return None
    try:
        return json.loads(_b64url_decode(body))
    except Exception:
        return None


def can(roles, permissions_by_role, action):
    granted = set()
    for role in roles:
        granted |= set(permissions_by_role.get(role, []))
    return action in granted
--- hint
Write `_b64url` and its decoder from the formulas given. `make_token` is body plus a dot plus the signature of the body's bytes.
--- hint
`read_token` must verify the signature with `hmac.compare_digest` *before* decoding the body, and return `None` on any failure. Wrap the JSON decode in `try`/`except`.
--- hint
`can` unions the permission lists of the user's roles into a set (an unknown role gives an empty list via `.get(role, [])`) and tests membership.

(lambda: read_token(b"attacker", make_token(b"real", {"user": "ada"})) is None)()

--- check test | A malformed token gives None, not an error
(lambda key: [read_token(key, t) for t in ["", "no-dot", "a.b.c", "x.y"]] == [None, None, None, None])(b"k")
--- check test | The payload is only encoded, not encrypted (readable without the key)

=> False

+++ practice | Authorise a resource request (stop IDOR)
--- task
Model the ownership check that stops an insecure direct object reference. Each resource has an owner. A request must be for a resource the requester owns, unless the requester is an admin.

Write `authorise(owners, admins, user, resource_id)`:

- `owners` maps a resource id to the user who owns it;
- `admins` is a set (or list) of user names who may access anything;
- return `True` if `user` is in `admins`, or if `resource_id` is in `owners` and `owners[resource_id] == user`;
- return `False` otherwise, including when `resource_id` is unknown.

For example, if `owners = {1481: "ada"}` and `admins = {"root"}`, then `authorise(owners, admins, "ada", 1481)` is `True`, `authorise(owners, admins, "lin", 1481)` is `False` (IDOR blocked), and `authorise(owners, admins, "root", 1481)` is `True`.
--- starter

    return True
--- solution
def authorise(owners, admins, user, resource_id):
    if user in admins:
        return True
    return resource_id in owners and owners[resource_id] == user
--- hint
Admins pass straight away. Otherwise the resource must exist and be owned by this exact user.
--- hint
Return `False` for an unknown `resource_id`; check `resource_id in owners` before comparing the owner.
--- check case | The owner is allowed

=> True
--- check case | A different user is blocked (the IDOR attempt)

=> False
--- check case | An admin may access anything

=> True
--- check case | An unknown resource is refused even for its would-be owner

=> False
--- check test | An admin may access an unknown resource (they are allowed anything)

--- check test | Ownership must match exactly

+++ practice | Verify an access token with expiry
--- task
The starter gives you `read_token(key, token)` from the lesson, which returns the payload dict or `None`. Build the full access check on top of it.

Write `check_access(key, token, now, action, permissions_by_role)`:

- read the token; if it is `None`, return the string `"invalid"`;
- if the payload has no `"exp"` key, or `now` is greater than or equal to `payload["exp"]`, return `"expired"`;
- if the action is not permitted by the payload's roles (a list under `"roles"`, missing means no roles), using the union rule, return `"forbidden"`;
- otherwise return `"ok"`.

Check them in that order: an invalid signature is `"invalid"` before anything else, then expiry, then permission.

For example, a freshly signed token with `roles: ["operator"]` and `exp: 100`, checked at `now = 50` for action `"command"` with operator granted `command`, returns `"ok"`; the same at `now = 100` returns `"expired"`.
--- starter
import base64
import hashlib
import hmac
import json


def read_token(key, token):
    if token.count(".") != 1:
        return None
    body, signature = token.split(".")
    expected = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(hmac.new(key, body.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest()).rstrip(b"=").decode()
    if not hmac.compare_digest(signature, expected):
        return None
    try:
        return json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(body + "=" * (-len(body) % 4)))
    except Exception:
        return None


def check_access(key, token, now, action, permissions_by_role):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import base64
import hashlib
import hmac
import json


def read_token(key, token):
    if token.count(".") != 1:
        return None
    body, signature = token.split(".")
    expected = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(hmac.new(key, body.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest()).rstrip(b"=").decode()
    if not hmac.compare_digest(signature, expected):
        return None
    try:
        return json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(body + "=" * (-len(body) % 4)))
    except Exception:
        return None


def check_access(key, token, now, action, permissions_by_role):
    payload = read_token(key, token)
    if payload is None:
        return "invalid"
    if "exp" not in payload or now >= payload["exp"]:
        return "expired"
    granted = set()
    for role in payload.get("roles", []):
        granted |= set(permissions_by_role.get(role, []))
    if action not in granted:
        return "forbidden"
    return "ok"
--- hint
Read the token first and return `"invalid"` on `None`. Then handle a missing or passed `exp`.
--- hint
Union the permissions of the payload's `roles` (default an empty list) and return `"forbidden"` unless the action is in it, otherwise `"ok"`.

--- check test | A bad signature is invalid
check_access(b"k", "x.y", 0, "read", {}) == "invalid"
--- check test | An expired token is expired
(lambda key, mk: check_access(key, mk({"roles": ["operator"], "exp": 100}), 100, "command", {"operator": ["command"]}) == "expired")(b"k", lambda p: (lambda body: body + "." + __import__("base64").urlsafe_b64encode(__import__("hmac").new(b"k", body.encode(), "sha256").digest()).rstrip(b"=").decode())(__import__("base64").urlsafe_b64encode(__import__("json").dumps(p, separators=(",", ":"), sort_keys=True).encode()).rstrip(b"=").decode()))
--- check test | A token with no exp counts as expired
(lambda key, mk: check_access(key, mk({"roles": ["operator"]}), 0, "command", {"operator": ["command"]}) == "expired")(b"k", lambda p: (lambda body: body + "." + __import__("base64").urlsafe_b64encode(__import__("hmac").new(b"k", body.encode(), "sha256").digest()).rstrip(b"=").decode())(__import__("base64").urlsafe_b64encode(__import__("json").dumps(p, separators=(",", ":"), sort_keys=True).encode()).rstrip(b"=").decode()))
--- check test | A valid token without the permission is forbidden
(lambda key, mk: check_access(key, mk({"roles": ["viewer"], "exp": 100}), 50, "command", {"viewer": ["read"], "operator": ["command"]}) == "forbidden")(b"k", lambda p: (lambda body: body + "." + __import__("base64").urlsafe_b64encode(__import__("hmac").new(b"k", body.encode(), "sha256").digest()).rstrip(b"=").decode())(__import__("base64").urlsafe_b64encode(__import__("json").dumps(p, separators=(",", ":"), sort_keys=True).encode()).rstrip(b"=").decode()))
--- check test | Invalid beats expired beats forbidden (order matters)
check_access(b"k", "not.valid.token", 999, "anything", {}) == "invalid"

+++ practice | Unguessable session ids
--- task
Session ids must come from a cryptographic source and be long. Write two functions.

- `new_session_id()`: return a URL-safe random session id with at least 32 bytes of entropy, using `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`. (It is random, so checks test its shape, not its value.)
- `is_strong(session_id)`: return `True` if `session_id` is at least 43 characters long (32 bytes in URL-safe base64) and uses only URL-safe base64 characters (letters, digits, `-` and `_`), else `False`. A short or non-random-looking id like `"session-1"` or `"1002"` must be rejected.

For example, `is_strong(new_session_id())` is always `True`, and `is_strong("1002")` is `False`.
--- starter
def new_session_id():
    raise NotImplementedError


def is_strong(session_id):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import secrets
import string


def new_session_id():
    return secrets.token_urlsafe(32)


_ALLOWED = set(string.ascii_letters + string.digits + "-_")


def is_strong(session_id):
    if len(session_id) < 43:
        return False
    return all(c in _ALLOWED for c in session_id)
--- hint
`secrets.token_urlsafe(32)` gives about 43 URL-safe characters from a cryptographic source.
--- hint
For `is_strong`, check the length is at least 43, then that every character is a letter, digit, `-` or `_`.
--- check test | Generated ids are always strong
all(is_strong(new_session_id()) for _ in range(20))
--- check test | Generated ids are unique (cryptographic randomness)
len({new_session_id() for _ in range(100)}) == 100
--- check case | Short or sequential ids are rejected
(is_strong("1002"), is_strong("session-1"), is_strong("a" * 42))
=> (False, False, False)
--- check case | A 43-character URL-safe string is accepted
is_strong("A" * 43)
=> True
--- check case | Disallowed characters are rejected
(is_strong("A" * 42 + "!"), is_strong("A" * 42 + "+"), is_strong("A" * 42 + "/"))
=> (False, False, False)
