+++ practice | Least privilege: the smallest role set
--- task
Grant a user the fewest roles that cover the actions they must perform. Each role grants a set of permissions; a user needs a given set of actions. Return the smallest number of roles whose combined permissions cover every needed action, or `None` if no combination can.

Write `min_roles(permissions_by_role, needed)`:

- `permissions_by_role` maps a role name to a list of permissions;
- `needed` is a set (or list) of required actions;
- return the least number of roles whose union of permissions is a superset of `needed`; return 0 if nothing is needed, and `None` if even all roles together cannot cover `needed`.

There are only a handful of roles, so try combinations from smallest upward. For example, if `manage` covers everything a task needs in one role while it would take two others, return 1.
--- starter
def min_roles(permissions_by_role, needed):
    return None
--- solution
from itertools import combinations


def min_roles(permissions_by_role, needed):
    needed = set(needed)
    if not needed:
        return 0
    roles = list(permissions_by_role)
    everything = set()
    for role in roles:
        everything |= set(permissions_by_role[role])
    if not needed <= everything:
        return None
    for k in range(1, len(roles) + 1):
        for combo in combinations(roles, k):
            covered = set()
            for role in combo:
                covered |= set(permissions_by_role[role])
            if needed <= covered:
                return k
    return None
--- hint
First check whether the union of all roles even covers `needed`; if not, return `None`. Handle empty `needed` as 0.
--- hint
Try every combination of 1 role, then 2, and so on with `itertools.combinations`, returning the first size k whose union covers `needed`.
--- check test | One broad role beats two narrow ones
min_roles({"reader": ["read"], "writer": ["write"], "manager": ["read", "write"]}, {"read", "write"}) == 1
--- check test | Two roles are needed when no single one covers all
min_roles({"reader": ["read"], "writer": ["write"]}, {"read", "write"}) == 2
--- check case | Nothing needed
min_roles({"a": ["x"]}, set())
=> 0
--- check case | Impossible to cover
min_roles({"a": ["x"]}, {"y"})
=> None
--- check test | A single sufficient role
min_roles({"admin": ["read", "write", "delete"]}, {"read", "delete"}) == 1
--- check test | Picks the fewest even among many roles
min_roles({"a": ["1"], "b": ["2"], "c": ["3"], "big": ["1", "2", "3"]}, {"1", "2", "3"}) == 1

+++ practice | Debug: the login that leaks whether a user exists
--- task
This login function returns a different message when the user does not exist ("no such user") than when the password is wrong ("wrong password"). That lets an attacker discover which usernames are registered, a **username enumeration** flaw, and it also compares the stored hash with `==`. Fix both problems: return the single message `"login failed"` for any failure, whether the user is unknown or the password is wrong, and compare the hashes with `hmac.compare_digest`. Return `"welcome"` on success. Passwords are checked as `sha256(salt + password.encode())` where each record is `(salt_hex, expected_hex)`; the starter's helper `_hash(salt_hex, password)` computes the hex digest.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def _hash(salt_hex, password):
    return hashlib.sha256(bytes.fromhex(salt_hex) + password.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()


def login(users, name, password):
    if name not in users:
        return "no such user"
    salt_hex, expected_hex = users[name]
    if _hash(salt_hex, password) == expected_hex:
        return "welcome"
    return "wrong password"
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def _hash(salt_hex, password):
    return hashlib.sha256(bytes.fromhex(salt_hex) + password.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()


def login(users, name, password):
    if name not in users:
        return "login failed"
    salt_hex, expected_hex = users[name]
    if hmac.compare_digest(_hash(salt_hex, password), expected_hex):
        return "welcome"
    return "login failed"
--- hint
Both the unknown-user path and the wrong-password path must return the same string, `"login failed"`.
--- hint
Replace `==` with `hmac.compare_digest` on the two hex digests.
--- check test | A correct login succeeds
(lambda h: login({"ada": ("aabb", h("aabb", "orbit"))}, "ada", "orbit") == "welcome")(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check test | An unknown user and a wrong password give the same message
(lambda h: login({"ada": ("aabb", h("aabb", "orbit"))}, "mallory", "x") == login({"ada": ("aabb", h("aabb", "orbit"))}, "ada", "wrong") == "login failed")(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check test | The message never reveals that a user exists
(lambda h: "user" not in login({"ada": ("aabb", h("aabb", "orbit"))}, "nobody", "x"))(lambda s, p: __import__("hashlib").sha256(bytes.fromhex(s) + p.encode()).hexdigest())
--- check case | Empty user store always fails the same way
login({}, "anyone", "any")
=> "login failed"
--- check source | Uses constant-time comparison
compare_digest

=== sec-12 | Secure coding in flight software: input validation, fault containment, defence in depth
--- teach
The last five lessons were about cryptography. This one steps back to the discipline that decides whether all of it actually holds: writing code that stays safe when the input is hostile, the hardware misbehaves, and one part fails. Flight software, the code that runs a spacecraft, is the sharpest example, because a crash cannot be fixed by a reboot from the ground in the middle of a manoeuvre and there is no user to click "retry". The habits here apply to any system where failure is expensive: medical devices, cars, payment systems.

### Why flight software is different

A program on your laptop can crash and be restarted. Flight software runs where:

- **you cannot reach it.** A bug found after launch may be unfixable, or patchable only over a slow, intermittent link with one chance to get it right.
- **failure is physical.** A missed deadline or a wrong command can lose the vehicle. [[Ariane 5|ariane]] was destroyed 37 seconds after launch by an unhandled numeric overflow in reused code.
- **resources are fixed and small.** A slow processor, a few megabytes of radiation-hardened memory, no swap. You cannot allocate your way out of trouble.
- **the environment is harsh.** Cosmic rays flip bits in memory (single-event upsets); sensors return noise and dropouts.

So the standards are strict. NASA's [[Power of Ten|powerten]] rules and the automotive MISRA C rules ban whole language features: no recursion (unbounded stack), no dynamic memory allocation after start-up (no fragmentation, no out-of-memory mid-flight), every loop with a provable upper bound, and a check on the return value of every call. The idea is not cleverness; it is removing the ways a program can surprise you.

### Input validation: the first wall

**Every input from outside the trust boundary is guilty until proven innocent.** A telecommand, a sensor reading, a packet, a configuration value: validate it fully before it reaches any logic. Lesson 4's principle, an **allowlist**, is the rule: define exactly what is acceptable and reject everything else, rather than trying to name what is dangerous.

For a command that must be validated:

- **check the shape:** exactly the expected fields, no more (an extra field is a red flag), no fewer;
- **check the type:** an integer where an integer is required, and beware Python's quirk that `True` is an `int`;
- **check the range:** a valve number within the valves that exist, a burn duration within physical limits;
- **reject, do not clamp,** unless clamping is a deliberate, documented policy: silently changing a bad value can hide an attack or a fault.

```python
def validate_reading(value, low, high):
    if not isinstance(value, int) or isinstance(value, bool):
        return None
    if value < low or value > high:
        return None
    return value
print(validate_reading(120, 0, 600))    # 120
print(validate_reading(9000, 0, 600))   # None
print(validate_reading(True, 0, 600))   # None -- a bool is not a valid reading
```

Validation at the boundary means the interior can assume its data is well-formed, which is what makes the interior simple enough to reason about.

### Fault containment: a failure stays local

Things will still go wrong: a sensor fails, a computation overflows, a partner module misbehaves. **Fault containment** means one failure does not spread. The techniques:

- **Fail safe / fail closed.** On an unrecoverable error, go to a known safe state, not an undefined one. Spacecraft enter **[[safe mode|safemode]]**: shut down non-essential systems, point the solar panels at the sun, wait for instructions. A door lock that fails *closed* is fault containment; one that fails *open* is not.
- **Bounded everything.** Fixed-size buffers, bounded queues that drop or reject when full rather than growing without limit, loops with a hard iteration cap. An unbounded queue under a fault becomes an out-of-memory crash.
- **Bounded retries and timeouts.** Retry a flaky operation, but a fixed number of times, then give up to a defined fallback. A retry loop with no cap turns a transient glitch into a hang.
- **Watchdogs.** A hardware timer that resets the system unless the software "pets" it regularly, so a hung task is caught even when the software cannot catch itself.
- **Isolation.** Separate the critical from the non-critical so a fault in telemetry display cannot touch attitude control (least privilege from lesson 11, at the module level).

### Defence in depth: no single point of failure

**Defence in depth** layers independent controls so that one failure is not a breach. You saw it in lesson 6 (a hash *and* a keyed hash) and lesson 11 (a client check *and* a server check *and* a database permission). In flight software it means:

- **redundancy:** multiple sensors and voting (if three sensors disagree, take the majority), backup processors that take over on failure;
- **sanity checks between layers:** a value that passed input validation is still cross-checked against physics ("altitude cannot drop faster than free fall") before acting;
- **graceful degradation:** lose a sensor and fall back to a coarser estimate, rather than failing entirely.

The principle behind all of it: **assume each layer will sometimes fail, and make sure the next one catches it.** A single perfect check is a fantasy; several imperfect independent checks are engineering.

### The overflow that mattered

Lesson 2 showed memory-safety overflows. There is also **numeric** overflow, and it has ended missions. A value that fits in a wide type is converted to a narrow one and wraps or saturates. In C, signed overflow is undefined behaviour (Systems Programming), so the compiler may assume it never happens and delete your check. The defences: use a wide enough type, validate ranges *before* the arithmetic, and in C use checked or unsigned arithmetic deliberately. In ORBIT's world, remember from earlier lessons that the browser's C++ builds `long`, `size_t` and pointers as 4 bytes, so a value above about two billion needs `long long`.

### What it costs

Validation and sanity checks add a small constant cost per input, negligible next to the work they protect. Bounded structures trade a fixed memory ceiling for the guarantee of never exhausting memory, which is exactly the trade flight software wants: predictable worst-case behaviour over best-case speed. Redundancy multiplies hardware cost, spent where a failure is unacceptable.

### Where it is used

- **Spacecraft** (safe mode, watchdogs, triple-redundant flight computers on crewed vehicles, fixed memory).
- **Avionics** (DO-178C), **cars** (ISO 26262, MISRA), **medical devices** (IEC 62304): the same discipline under different names.
- **Any hardened service:** input validation at the edge, bounded queues and timeouts, circuit breakers, and health checks that fail closed are the same ideas in a data centre.
- **ORBIT's capstone** flight-software system builds exactly this: a telemetry pipeline with fixed memory, a scheduler, and fault handling.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- **Trusting input because it "comes from our own system".** A trust boundary is where control changes hands; validate across it regardless of source.
- **Clamping silently** where you should reject, hiding a fault or an attack.
- **Unbounded queues, retries or recursion** that turn a small fault into a crash.
- **Failing open:** defaulting to "allow" or "act" on an error, instead of to a safe state.
- **Relying on one check.** Layer independent controls; assume each can fail.
- **Ignoring return values and error codes** (Systems Programming's lesson): the error you do not check is the one that bites.

::: context ariane The Ariane 5 flight 501 failure
On its maiden flight in 1996 the Ariane 5 rocket veered off course and self-destructed 37 seconds after launch, destroying about 370 million dollars of hardware. The cause was software reused from the slower Ariane 4: a 64-bit floating-point value for horizontal velocity was converted to a 16-bit signed integer, and because Ariane 5 flew faster, the value did not fit and the conversion overflowed. The exception was unhandled, the guidance computer shut down, its identical backup shut down for the same reason a moment earlier, and the rocket lost control. Every ingredient of this lesson appears: an unchecked range, reused code run outside its validated envelope, an unhandled fault, and redundancy that did not help because both units ran the same flawed code.
:::

::: context safemode Safe mode
When a spacecraft detects a fault it cannot handle, it enters safe mode: a minimal, well-understood configuration meant to keep the vehicle alive and reachable until engineers on the ground diagnose the problem. Typically it shuts down non-essential instruments, orients the solar panels toward the Sun to guarantee power, points the antenna toward Earth, and waits for commands. It is the physical embodiment of "fail to a known safe state" rather than continuing in an uncertain one. Deep-space missions such as the Voyagers and the Mars rovers have entered safe mode many times over the decades; it is a normal, designed-for event, not a disaster, and it is what buys the time to fix things without losing the vehicle.
:::

::: context powerten The Power of Ten rules
In 2006 Gerard Holzmann of NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory published ten rules for writing safety-critical code, chosen so that a program can be checked automatically and reasoned about with confidence. Among them: use only simple control flow (no goto, no recursion, so the call stack is bounded); give every loop a fixed upper bound that a checker can verify; do no dynamic memory allocation after initialisation, so memory use is fixed and there is no fragmentation or out-of-memory failure in flight; keep functions short; check the return value of every non-void call, and validate every parameter; and compile with all warnings on and treat them as errors. The rules trade expressiveness for predictability, which is exactly the bargain flight software needs: code whose worst-case behaviour is known in advance.
:::
--- task
Model a flight-software command validator and a bounded command queue.

- `validate_command(command, spec)`: `command` is a dict, `spec` maps each required field name to a `(low, high)` inclusive integer range. Return a new dict with the validated fields if **all** of these hold, else `None`: `command` has exactly the fields in `spec` (no extra, none missing); every value is an `int` but **not** a `bool`; every value is within its range. Reject, never clamp.
- Write a class `CommandQueue` created with `CommandQueue(capacity)`. Its method `push(command)` appends the command and returns `True` if there is room (fewer than `capacity` items), otherwise drops it, counts it, and returns `False`. Its method `pop()` removes and returns the oldest command, or `None` if empty. A read-only attribute `dropped` counts how many were dropped. The queue never grows past `capacity`.

For example, with `spec = {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}`, `validate_command({"valve": 3, "duration": 120}, spec)` returns `{"valve": 3, "duration": 120}`, while a valve of 9, a missing field, an extra field, or a boolean value each give `None`.
--- starter
def validate_command(command, spec):
    raise NotImplementedError


class CommandQueue:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def push(self, command):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def pop(self):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def validate_command(command, spec):
    if set(command) != set(spec):
        return None
    validated = {}
    for field, (low, high) in spec.items():
        value = command[field]
        if not isinstance(value, int) or isinstance(value, bool):
            return None
        if value < low or value > high:
            return None
        validated[field] = value
    return validated


class CommandQueue:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.items = []
        self.dropped = 0

    def push(self, command):
        if len(self.items) >= self.capacity:
            self.dropped += 1
            return False
        self.items.append(command)
        return True

    def pop(self):
        if not self.items:
            return None
        return self.items.pop(0)
--- hint
Compare `set(command)` with `set(spec)` to catch extra or missing fields at once. For each field check the type (rejecting `bool`) and the range, building a new dict; return `None` on the first failure.
--- hint
The queue keeps a list and a `dropped` counter. `push` refuses and counts when the list already holds `capacity` items; `pop` returns `None` on empty, else removes the first item.
--- check case | A valid command passes
validate_command({"valve": 3, "duration": 120}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)})
=> {"valve": 3, "duration": 120}
--- check case | Out of range is rejected, not clamped
(validate_command({"valve": 9, "duration": 120}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}), validate_command({"valve": -1, "duration": 0}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | A missing or extra field is rejected
(validate_command({"valve": 3}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}), validate_command({"valve": 3, "duration": 120, "hack": 1}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | A boolean is not a valid integer reading
validate_command({"valve": True, "duration": 120}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)})
=> None
--- check case | Boundary values are inclusive
validate_command({"valve": 0, "duration": 600}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)})
=> {"valve": 0, "duration": 600}
--- check case | A non-integer value is rejected
(validate_command({"x": 1.5}, {"x": (0, 10)}), validate_command({"x": "3"}, {"x": (0, 10)}))
=> (None, None)
--- check test | The queue never grows past capacity, and counts drops
(lambda q: ([q.push(i) for i in range(5)], len(q.items), q.dropped) == ([True, True, True, False, False], 3, 2))(CommandQueue(3))
--- check test | pop returns oldest first, then None
(lambda q: ([q.push(c) for c in ("a", "b")], q.pop(), q.pop(), q.pop()) == ([True, True], "a", "b", None))(CommandQueue(5))
--- check case | Popping from an empty queue is safe
CommandQueue(2).pop()
=> None
--- check test | Room frees up after a pop
(lambda q: (q.push("a"), q.push("b"), q.push("c"), q.pop(), q.push("d"), q.dropped) == (True, True, False, "a", True, 1))(CommandQueue(2))

+++ practice | Sanity check between layers
--- task
Defence in depth: a reading that already passed range validation is still cross-checked against physics before use. Consecutive altitude readings cannot change faster than a limit.

Write `plausible(readings, max_step)` that returns `True` if the list `readings` (integers) never changes by more than `max_step` in absolute value between consecutive readings, else `False`. A list of 0 or 1 readings is trivially plausible (`True`).

For example, `plausible([100, 105, 103], 10)` is `True`, and `plausible([100, 130], 10)` is `False` (a jump of 30).
--- starter
def plausible(readings, max_step):
    return True
--- solution
def plausible(readings, max_step):
    for previous, current in zip(readings, readings[1:]):
        if abs(current - previous) > max_step:
            return False
    return True
--- hint
Compare each reading with the one before it using `zip(readings, readings[1:])`.
--- hint
If any absolute difference exceeds `max_step`, return `False`; otherwise `True`. Zero or one readings never enter the loop.
--- check case | A smooth series
plausible([100, 105, 103, 108], 10)
=> True
--- check case | A sudden jump
plausible([100, 130], 10)
=> False
--- check case | Empty and single readings
(plausible([], 5), plausible([42], 5))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | A jump exactly at the limit is allowed
plausible([0, 10, 0], 10)
=> True
--- check test | A drop as well as a rise is caught
plausible([500, 400], 50) is False
--- check test | Negative readings work
plausible([-5, -3, -6], 4) is True

+++ practice | Bounded retries
--- task
Retry a flaky operation a bounded number of times, then give up. Write `run_with_retries(action, max_attempts)`:

- call `action(attempt)` for `attempt` = 1, 2, …, up to `max_attempts`;
- `action` returns `True` on success and `False` on failure;
- return the attempt number on the first success, or `None` if all attempts fail;
- never call `action` more than `max_attempts` times.

For example, if the action first succeeds on its third try, return `3`; if it always fails within 5 attempts, return `None`.
--- starter
def run_with_retries(action, max_attempts):
    return None
--- solution
def run_with_retries(action, max_attempts):
    for attempt in range(1, max_attempts + 1):
        if action(attempt):
            return attempt
    return None
--- hint
Loop `attempt` from 1 to `max_attempts` inclusive and call `action(attempt)`.
--- hint
Return the attempt on the first `True`; after the loop, return `None`.
--- check test | Succeeds on the third attempt
(lambda: run_with_retries(lambda a: a == 3, 5) == 3)()
--- check test | Never exceeds the cap
(lambda calls: (run_with_retries(lambda a: (calls.append(a), False)[1], 3), calls) == (None, [1, 2, 3]))([])
--- check test | Succeeds immediately
run_with_retries(lambda a: True, 4) == 1
--- check test | Stops calling after success
(lambda calls: (run_with_retries(lambda a: (calls.append(a), a == 2)[1], 5), calls) == (2, [1, 2]))([])
--- check case | Zero attempts never calls and fails
run_with_retries(lambda a: True, 0)
=> None
--- check test | All fail gives None
run_with_retries(lambda a: False, 4) is None

+++ practice | Majority voting over redundant sensors
--- task
Triple redundancy: three sensors report, and the system trusts the majority. Write `vote(readings, tolerance)` where `readings` is a list of three integers:

- if at least two readings are within `tolerance` of each other (absolute difference at most `tolerance`), return the average of the closest such agreeing pair as an integer using integer division `//`;
- if no two agree within `tolerance`, return `None` (the sensors are too scattered to trust).

If more than one pair agrees, use the pair with the smallest difference; on a tie in difference, use the pair that appears first when checking pairs in the order (0,1), (0,2), (1,2).

For example, `vote([100, 101, 130], 5)` returns `100` (readings 0 and 1 agree, average 100), and `vote([100, 130, 160], 5)` returns `None`.
--- starter
def vote(readings, tolerance):
    return None
--- solution
def vote(readings, tolerance):
    pairs = [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2)]
    best = None
    for i, j in pairs:
        diff = abs(readings[i] - readings[j])
        if diff <= tolerance:
            if best is None or diff < best[0]:
                best = (diff, (readings[i] + readings[j]) // 2)
    return None if best is None else best[1]
--- hint
Check the three pairs in order, computing each absolute difference and whether it is within `tolerance`.
--- hint
Keep the agreeing pair with the smallest difference (the first one on a tie), and return the integer average of that pair, or `None` if none agreed.
--- check case | Two of three agree
vote([100, 101, 130], 5)
=> 100
--- check case | All scattered
vote([100, 130, 160], 5)
=> None
--- check case | All three agree, closest pair wins
vote([100, 102, 101], 5)
=> 100
--- check test | The closest pair is chosen, not just the first
vote([100, 108, 109], 5) == 108
--- check case | Exact agreement
vote([50, 50, 999], 0)
=> 50
--- check test | Integer average rounds down
vote([10, 15, 999], 5) == 12

+++ practice | Debug: the parser that trusts its input
--- task
This function parses a telecommand string `"FIELD=VALUE;FIELD=VALUE"` into a dict of integers, but it trusts the input and crashes on anything malformed: a missing `=`, a non-integer value, or an empty field. Flight software must never crash on bad input. Fix `parse_command` so that it returns `None` for any malformed input instead of raising: the string must be one or more `name=value` pairs separated by `;`, each name non-empty, each value a valid (possibly negative) integer, and no duplicate names. On valid input return the dict.
--- starter
def parse_command(text):
    result = {}
    for pair in text.split(";"):
        name, value = pair.split("=")
        result[name] = int(value)
    return result
--- solution
def parse_command(text):
    result = {}
    for pair in text.split(";"):
        if pair.count("=") != 1:
            return None
        name, value = pair.split("=")
        if not name or name in result:
            return None
        try:
            result[name] = int(value)
        except ValueError:
            return None
    return result
--- hint
`pair.split("=")` raises when there is not exactly one `=`, and `int(value)` raises on a non-integer. Guard both.
--- hint
Check `pair.count("=") == 1`, reject an empty or duplicate name, and wrap `int(value)` in `try`/`except ValueError`, returning `None` on any problem.
--- check case | A valid command
parse_command("valve=3;duration=120")
=> {"valve": 3, "duration": 120}
--- check case | A negative value is valid
parse_command("offset=-5")
=> {"offset": -5}
--- check case | Missing equals sign
(parse_command("valve3;duration=120"), parse_command("valve==3"))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | A non-integer value
parse_command("valve=open")
=> None
--- check case | An empty name or duplicate name
(parse_command("=3"), parse_command("v=1;v=2"))
=> (None, None)
--- check test | Never raises on arbitrary junk
all(parse_command(s) is None for s in ["", "abc", "a=1;", ";a=1", "a=1;;b=2", "a=1.5"])

+++ practice | A fixed-memory ring buffer for telemetry
--- task
Flight software logs telemetry into a fixed block of memory that never grows: a **ring buffer** that overwrites the oldest entry when full (you saw ring buffers in DSA and C++). Build one over a fixed-size list.

Write a class `RingLog` created with `RingLog(capacity)` (capacity at least 1):

- `record(value)`: store `value`, overwriting the oldest if already full;
- `contents()`: return a list of the stored values from oldest to newest (at most `capacity` of them);
- a read-only attribute `total` counting how many values have ever been recorded (including overwritten ones).

The memory used never exceeds `capacity` entries. For example, a `RingLog(3)` after recording 1, 2, 3, 4 has `contents()` equal to `[2, 3, 4]` and `total` equal to 4.
--- starter
class RingLog:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def record(self, value):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def contents(self):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
class RingLog:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.buffer = [None] * capacity
        self.start = 0
        self.count = 0
        self.total = 0

    def record(self, value):
        end = (self.start + self.count) % self.capacity
        if self.count < self.capacity:
            self.buffer[end] = value
            self.count += 1
        else:
            self.buffer[self.start] = value
            self.start = (self.start + 1) % self.capacity
        self.total += 1

    def contents(self):
        return [self.buffer[(self.start + i) % self.capacity] for i in range(self.count)]
--- hint
Keep a fixed list of `capacity` slots, an index of the oldest item, a count of items held, and a `total`. Writing when full overwrites the oldest and advances the start.
--- hint
`contents` reads `count` items starting from `start`, wrapping with modulo. `total` increases on every `record`.
--- check test | Overwrites the oldest when full
(lambda r: ([r.record(v) for v in (1, 2, 3, 4)], r.contents(), r.total) == ([None] * 4, [2, 3, 4], 4))(RingLog(3))
--- check test | Below capacity keeps everything
(lambda r: ([r.record(v) for v in (7, 8)], r.contents(), r.total) == ([None, None], [7, 8], 2))(RingLog(5))
--- check case | Empty log
(RingLog(4).contents(), RingLog(4).total)
=> ([], 0)
--- check test | Memory never exceeds capacity
(lambda r: ([r.record(v) for v in range(100)], len(r.buffer) == 4 and len(r.contents()) == 4 and r.total == 100)[1])(RingLog(4))
--- check test | Capacity one always holds the latest
(lambda r: ([r.record(v) for v in (5, 6, 7)], r.contents(), r.total) == ([None, None, None], [7], 3))(RingLog(1))
--- check test | Wrap-around order is correct after many records
(lambda r: ([r.record(v) for v in range(10)], r.contents()) == ([None] * 10, [7, 8, 9]))(RingLog(3))

=== sec-gate | Security: mastery gate
--- teach
This gate covers the whole course, from thinking like an attacker and memory-safety bugs through injection, web security and cryptography to authentication, protocols and secure flight-software coding. Every problem combines ideas from more than one lesson, and there are no hints. To prepare, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hardest, especially the cryptography lessons where a wrong byte-encoding or a non-constant-time comparison is easy to miss. Use only `hashlib`, `hmac`, `secrets`, `base64`, `json`, `sqlite3` and the rest of the standard library; any hand-built cipher is a toy.
--- gate

+++ problem | Render a comment safely and fingerprint it
--- task
Combine web output-escaping with an integrity fingerprint. Write `render_and_hash(comment)` that:

- escapes the five HTML-special characters in `comment`, in the order `&` `<` `>` `"` `'`, to `&amp;` `&lt;` `&gt;` `&quot;` `&#x27;`;
- wraps the escaped text as `"<li>" + escaped + "</li>"`;
- returns the SHA-256 hex digest of that HTML string encoded as UTF-8.

For example, `render_and_hash("Nice & tidy")` fingerprints `"<li>Nice &amp; tidy</li>"`.
--- starter
def render_and_hash(comment):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib


def render_and_hash(comment):
    escaped = comment
    for a, b in [("&", "&amp;"), ("<", "&lt;"), (">", "&gt;"), ('"', "&quot;"), ("'", "&#x27;")]:
        escaped = escaped.replace(a, b)
    html = "<li>" + escaped + "</li>"
    return hashlib.sha256(html.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
--- check case | A benign comment
render_and_hash("Nice & tidy")
=> "1b07efa72191f72b57071e547c67061d1d9a11200b8a7faf9faa9be04badf40e"
--- check case | A script payload is escaped before hashing
render_and_hash("<b>hi</b>")
=> "088e396a288992f98f8fb6be335d2ef7a45168a0f6a1a636f48191d7be9670a1"
--- check test | The ampersand is escaped first, not double-escaped
render_and_hash("<") == __import__("hashlib").sha256("<li>&lt;</li>".encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
--- check test | A raw angle bracket never reaches the hashed HTML
"&lt;" in "<li>" + "<script>".replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;") + "</li>"
--- check test | The digest is 64 hex characters
all(len(render_and_hash(s)) == 64 for s in ["", "plain", "a\"b'c&d<e>f"])

+++ problem | Verify a password record in constant time
--- task
A record is `"pbkdf2_sha256$<iterations>$<salt hex>$<key hex>"`, where the key is `hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac("sha256", password_utf8, salt, iterations)` with the default 32-byte length. Write `verify(password, record)` that returns `True` only if `password` (a `str`) reproduces the stored key, comparing with `hmac.compare_digest`, and `False` on any wrong password or malformed record (not four `$`-fields, a non-integer or non-positive iteration count, or bad hex). It must never raise.
--- starter
def verify(password, record):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def verify(password, record):
    fields = record.split("$")
    if len(fields) != 4 or fields[0] != "pbkdf2_sha256":
        return False
    count, salt_hex, key_hex = fields[1], fields[2], fields[3]
    if not count.isdigit() or int(count) < 1:
        return False
    try:
        salt = bytes.fromhex(salt_hex)
        key = bytes.fromhex(key_hex)
    except ValueError:
        return False
    computed = hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac("sha256", password.encode("utf-8"), salt, int(count))
    return hmac.compare_digest(computed, key)
--- check test | The right password verifies
(lambda r: verify("launch", r))("pbkdf2_sha256$1000$4e61436c$834459a0640e282c869b0ab389799b17e2bb808bba4914ffc279715744ea742d")
--- check test | A wrong password does not
(lambda r: verify("Launch", r) is False)("pbkdf2_sha256$1000$4e61436c$834459a0640e282c869b0ab389799b17e2bb808bba4914ffc279715744ea742d")
--- check test | Malformed records never raise and return False
[verify("x", r) for r in ["", "$$$", "pbkdf2_sha256$0$4e61436c$00", "pbkdf2_sha256$ten$4e61436c$00", "pbkdf2_sha256$1000$zz$00", "md5$1000$4e61436c$00"]] == [False] * 6
--- check test | UTF-8 passwords work
(lambda salt, pw: verify(pw, "pbkdf2_sha256$500$" + salt.hex() + "$" + __import__("hashlib").pbkdf2_hmac("sha256", pw.encode("utf-8"), salt, 500).hex()))(b"saltsalt", "café-9")
--- check source | Uses constant-time comparison
compare_digest

+++ problem | A safe SQL WHERE clause
--- task
Two ways to put a user value into SQL. Write:

- `escape_literal(value)`: return the value as a safely quoted SQL string literal: wrap it in single quotes and double every single quote inside it. For example `O'Brien` becomes `'O''Brien'`.
- `safe_lookup(con, name)`: given an open `sqlite3` connection `con` with a table `users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)`, run a **parameterised** query returning the ids whose name equals `name`, in ascending id order, as a list. Use a `?` placeholder, never string formatting.

For example, `escape_literal("x' OR '1'='1")` neutralises the classic injection payload into one harmless literal, and `safe_lookup` returns `[]` for that same payload.
--- starter
def escape_literal(value):
    raise NotImplementedError


def safe_lookup(con, name):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def escape_literal(value):
    return "'" + value.replace("'", "''") + "'"


def safe_lookup(con, name):
    cur = con.execute("SELECT id FROM users WHERE name = ? ORDER BY id", (name,))
    return [row[0] for row in cur.fetchall()]
--- check case | An apostrophe is doubled
(escape_literal("O'Brien"), escape_literal("plain"))
=> ("'O''Brien'", "'plain'")
--- check case | The injection payload becomes one literal
escape_literal("x' OR '1'='1")
=> "'x'' OR ''1''=''1'"
--- check case | The empty string
escape_literal("")
=> "''"
--- check test | The parameterised lookup finds the right rows and resists injection
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)"), con.executemany("INSERT INTO users VALUES(?,?)", [(1, "ada"), (2, "lin"), (3, "ada")]), (safe_lookup(con, "ada"), safe_lookup(con, "' OR '1'='1")))[-1] == ([1, 3], []))(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))
--- check test | A name with an apostrophe is handled by the parameter
(lambda con: (con.execute("CREATE TABLE users(id INTEGER, name TEXT)"), con.execute("INSERT INTO users VALUES(1, ?)", ("O'Brien",)), safe_lookup(con, "O'Brien"))[-1] == [1])(__import__("sqlite3").connect(":memory:"))

+++ problem | Authenticated encryption
--- task
Build authenticated encryption with CTR mode and encrypt-then-MAC over `hmac`/`hashlib.sha256`. Keystream block `i` (from 0) is the first 16 bytes of `hmac.new(enc_key, nonce + i.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()`.

- `seal(enc_key, mac_key, nonce, plaintext)`: `nonce` is 8 bytes. Exclusive-or the plaintext with the keystream, tag `nonce + ciphertext` with `hmac.new(mac_key, ..., hashlib.sha256).digest()` (32 bytes), and return `nonce + ciphertext + tag`.
- `open_box(enc_key, mac_key, box)`: return the plaintext, or `None` if `box` is shorter than 40 bytes or the tag does not match (compare with `hmac.compare_digest`), **without** decrypting on failure.
--- starter
def seal(enc_key, mac_key, nonce, plaintext):
    raise NotImplementedError


def open_box(enc_key, mac_key, box):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def _keystream(key, nonce, n):
    out = b""
    counter = 0
    while len(out) < n:
        out += hmac.new(key, nonce + counter.to_bytes(8, "big"), hashlib.sha256).digest()[:16]
        counter += 1
    return out[:n]


def seal(enc_key, mac_key, nonce, plaintext):
    ciphertext = bytes(p ^ k for p, k in zip(plaintext, _keystream(enc_key, nonce, len(plaintext))))
    tag = hmac.new(mac_key, nonce + ciphertext, hashlib.sha256).digest()
    return nonce + ciphertext + tag


def open_box(enc_key, mac_key, box):
    if len(box) < 40:
        return None
    nonce, ciphertext, tag = box[:8], box[8:-32], box[-32:]
    if not hmac.compare_digest(hmac.new(mac_key, nonce + ciphertext, hashlib.sha256).digest(), tag):
        return None
    return bytes(c ^ k for c, k in zip(ciphertext, _keystream(enc_key, nonce, len(ciphertext))))
--- check case | Seal then open round-trips
open_box(b"e", b"m", seal(b"e", b"m", b"12345678", b"set thrust 40%"))
=> b"set thrust 40%"
--- check case | The empty plaintext seals to 40 bytes and opens
(len(seal(b"e", b"m", b"12345678", b"")), open_box(b"e", b"m", seal(b"e", b"m", b"12345678", b"")))
=> (40, b"")
--- check test | Any single-byte change is rejected
(lambda b: all(open_box(b"e", b"m", b[:i] + bytes([b[i] ^ 1]) + b[i + 1:]) is None for i in range(len(b))))(seal(b"e", b"m", b"ABCDEFGH", b"open valve 3"))
--- check case | A wrong MAC key fails
open_box(b"e", b"wrong", seal(b"e", b"m", b"ABCDEFGH", b"hi"))
=> None
--- check test | A fresh nonce changes the ciphertext
seal(b"e", b"m", b"nonce-01", b"same") != seal(b"e", b"m", b"nonce-02", b"same")
--- check test | Too-short boxes return None without raising
[open_box(b"e", b"m", x) for x in [b"", b"short", bytes(39)]] == [None, None, None]

+++ problem | Sign and verify a command
--- task
Sign commands with RSA over their SHA-256 hash (toy modulus). Given the public key `(n, e)` and private `d`:

- `sign(message, n, d)`: hash the `bytes` `message` with SHA-256, read the digest as a big-endian integer, reduce modulo `n`, and raise to `d` modulo `n`.
- `verify(message, signature, n, e)`: return `True` only if `0 <= signature < n` and `pow(signature, e, n)` equals the same reduced digest, else `False`.

For example, with `n, e, d = 3233, 17, 2753`, a signature made by `sign` verifies, a changed message does not, and a signature of `n` is rejected.
--- starter
def sign(message, n, d):
    raise NotImplementedError


def verify(message, signature, n, e):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib


def _digest(message, n):
    return int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(message).digest(), "big") % n


def sign(message, n, d):
    return pow(_digest(message, n), d, n)


def verify(message, signature, n, e):
    if signature < 0 or signature >= n:
        return False
    return pow(signature, e, n) == _digest(message, n)
--- check test | A signature verifies and a changed message fails
(lambda: (lambda s: verify(b"ABORT", s, 3233, 17) and not verify(b"abort", s, 3233, 17))(sign(b"ABORT", 3233, 2753)))()
--- check case | A known signature
sign(b"ABORT", 3233, 2753)
=> 2393
--- check test | A signature at or beyond n is rejected
(verify(b"ABORT", 3233, 3233, 17), verify(b"ABORT", -1, 3233, 17)) == (False, False)
--- check test | Another party's key does not forge
(lambda: not verify(b"pay", sign(b"pay", 3233, 2757), 3233, 17))()
--- check test | Round-trips on a larger toy key
(lambda n, e, d: (lambda s: verify(b"open valve 3", s, n, e))(sign(b"open valve 3", n, d)))(1553796643855265753408399997158304397004945239137457885130325708591289381612562189403602142983569, 65537, 1053282416038482633640868185509785787285681328010269694527916461912235096949056652963229784958081)

+++ problem | A bounds-checked copy that protects the return address
--- task
Model a stack frame as a `bytearray`: `cap` bytes of buffer followed by a 4-byte saved return address, stored little-endian. An unbounded copy would overflow the buffer into the return address; a bounds-checked copy must not.

- `make_frame(cap, return_addr)`: return a `bytearray` of length `cap + 4`: `cap` zero bytes then `return_addr` as 4 little-endian bytes.
- `saved_return(frame)`: read the 4-byte little-endian return address from the end of `frame`.
- `safe_copy(frame, cap, src)`: copy `src` (a `bytes`) into the first `cap` bytes of `frame`, writing at most `cap - 1` bytes and then a terminating zero byte, so it never touches the saved return address. Return the number of source bytes copied (`min(len(src), cap - 1)`).

For example, copying a 5-byte source into a frame with `cap = 8` copies all 5 and leaves the return address intact; copying a 20-byte source copies only 7 and still leaves it intact.
--- starter
def make_frame(cap, return_addr):
    raise NotImplementedError


def saved_return(frame):
    raise NotImplementedError


def safe_copy(frame, cap, src):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def make_frame(cap, return_addr):
    return bytearray(cap) + bytearray(return_addr.to_bytes(4, "little"))


def saved_return(frame):
    return int.from_bytes(frame[-4:], "little")


def safe_copy(frame, cap, src):
    n = min(len(src), cap - 1)
    for i in range(n):
        frame[i] = src[i]
    frame[n] = 0
    return n
--- check case | The frame stores the return address little-endian
make_frame(4, 0x01020304).hex()
=> "0000000004030201"
--- check test | saved_return reads it back
saved_return(make_frame(8, 0xdeadbeef)) == 0xdeadbeef
--- check test | A short source is fully copied and the return address survives
(lambda f: (safe_copy(f, 8, b"HELLO"), bytes(f[:6]), saved_return(f)) == (5, b"HELLO\x00", 0xdeadbeef))(make_frame(8, 0xdeadbeef))
--- check test | A long source is truncated, never reaching the return address
(lambda f: (safe_copy(f, 8, b"A" * 20), saved_return(f)) == (7, 0xcafef00d))(make_frame(8, 0xcafef00d))
--- check test | Capacity 1 has room only for the terminator
(lambda f: (safe_copy(f, 1, b"xyz"), saved_return(f)) == (0, 0x11223344))(make_frame(1, 0x11223344))

+++ problem | A replay-proof command channel
--- task
Authenticate commands and reject replays and reordering with a sequence number. The tag is `hmac.new(key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + message, hashlib.sha256).digest()`.

Write a class `Channel` created with `Channel(key)`. Its method `accept(seq, message, tag)` returns `True` and advances only when `seq` equals the next expected number (from 0) **and** `tag` matches, compared with `hmac.compare_digest`; otherwise it returns `False` and does not advance. So a replayed or out-of-order or tampered frame is rejected.
--- starter
class Channel:
    def __init__(self, key):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def accept(self, seq, message, tag):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


class Channel:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.expected = 0

    def accept(self, seq, message, tag):
        if seq != self.expected:
            return False
        good = hmac.new(self.key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + message, hashlib.sha256).digest()
        if not hmac.compare_digest(good, tag):
            return False
        self.expected += 1
        return True
--- check test | Frames accepted in order, replay rejected
(lambda key: (lambda c, t: [c.accept(0, b"a", t(0, b"a")), c.accept(1, b"b", t(1, b"b")), c.accept(1, b"b", t(1, b"b")), c.accept(0, b"a", t(0, b"a"))] == [True, True, False, False])(Channel(key), lambda s, m: __import__("hmac").new(key, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + m, "sha256").digest()))(b"k")
--- check test | An out-of-order frame does not advance the channel
(lambda key: (lambda c, t: [c.accept(1, b"b", t(1, b"b")), c.accept(0, b"a", t(0, b"a")), c.accept(1, b"b", t(1, b"b"))] == [False, True, True])(Channel(key), lambda s, m: __import__("hmac").new(key, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + m, "sha256").digest()))(b"k")
--- check test | A tampered message with a stale tag is rejected
(lambda key: (lambda c, t: c.accept(0, b"HACKED", t(0, b"go")) is False)(Channel(key), lambda s, m: __import__("hmac").new(key, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + m, "sha256").digest()))(b"k")
--- check test | A wrong key never authenticates
(lambda: (lambda c: c.accept(0, b"go", __import__("hmac").new(b"attacker", (0).to_bytes(8, "big") + b"go", "sha256").digest()) is False)(Channel(b"real")))()

+++ problem | Authorise a token request
--- task
A signed token is `body + "." + signature`, where `body` is URL-safe-base64 (no `=` padding) of the compact JSON payload, and `signature` is URL-safe-base64 (no padding) of `hmac.new(key, body_bytes, hashlib.sha256).digest()`. The payload has `exp` (an integer) and `roles` (a list of role names).

Write `authorise(key, token, now, action, permissions_by_role)` returning:

- `"invalid"` if the token does not have exactly one `.`, or its signature does not match (constant-time), or the payload cannot be decoded;
- else `"expired"` if `now >= payload["exp"]` or `exp` is missing;
- else `"forbidden"` if `action` is not in the union of the permissions of the payload's roles;
- else `"ok"`.

Check them strictly in that order.
--- starter
def authorise(key, token, now, action, permissions_by_role):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import base64
import hashlib
import hmac
import json


def authorise(key, token, now, action, permissions_by_role):
    if token.count(".") != 1:
        return "invalid"
    body, signature = token.split(".")
    expected = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(hmac.new(key, body.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest()).rstrip(b"=").decode()
    if not hmac.compare_digest(signature, expected):
        return "invalid"
    try:
        payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(body + "=" * (-len(body) % 4)))
    except Exception:
        return "invalid"
    if "exp" not in payload or now >= payload["exp"]:
        return "expired"
    granted = set()
    for role in payload.get("roles", []):
        granted |= set(permissions_by_role.get(role, []))
    return "ok" if action in granted else "forbidden"
--- check test | A valid, unexpired, permitted token is ok
authorise(b"k", "eyJleHAiOjEwMCwicm9sZXMiOlsib3BlcmF0b3IiXSwidXNlciI6ImFkYSJ9.bsRnmg2dSfJfo0-_d6Lp2TRHxIETWA6F2ybuW85M5TA", 50, "command", {"operator": ["read", "command"]}) == "ok"
--- check test | The same token expired
authorise(b"k", "eyJleHAiOjEwMCwicm9sZXMiOlsib3BlcmF0b3IiXSwidXNlciI6ImFkYSJ9.bsRnmg2dSfJfo0-_d6Lp2TRHxIETWA6F2ybuW85M5TA", 100, "command", {"operator": ["command"]}) == "expired"
--- check test | The same token without the permission
authorise(b"k", "eyJleHAiOjEwMCwicm9sZXMiOlsib3BlcmF0b3IiXSwidXNlciI6ImFkYSJ9.bsRnmg2dSfJfo0-_d6Lp2TRHxIETWA6F2ybuW85M5TA", 50, "manage_users", {"operator": ["command"]}) == "forbidden"
--- check test | A bad signature or malformed token is invalid
[authorise(b"k", t, 0, "x", {}) for t in ["", "a.b.c", "no-dot", "eyJ4IjoxfQ.WRONG"]] == ["invalid", "invalid", "invalid", "invalid"]
--- check test | The wrong key is invalid, not merely forbidden
authorise(b"attacker", "eyJleHAiOjEwMCwicm9sZXMiOlsib3BlcmF0b3IiXSwidXNlciI6ImFkYSJ9.bsRnmg2dSfJfo0-_d6Lp2TRHxIETWA6F2ybuW85M5TA", 50, "command", {"operator": ["command"]}) == "invalid"

+++ problem | A Merkle root over a manifest
--- task
Fingerprint a whole dataset so any change to any block changes the root. Write `merkle_root(blocks)` where `blocks` is a list of `bytes`:

- if `blocks` is empty, return the SHA-256 hex digest of the empty input;
- otherwise start with the SHA-256 digest (raw bytes) of each block as the leaves;
- repeatedly combine the level in pairs: each parent is the SHA-256 digest of the two children's bytes joined, left then right; if a level has an odd number of nodes, duplicate the last node to make a pair;
- stop when one node remains and return it as a hex string.

For example, a single block's root is that block's own digest, and three blocks duplicate the third to pair it.
--- starter
def merkle_root(blocks):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
import hashlib


def merkle_root(blocks):
    if not blocks:
        return hashlib.sha256(b"").hexdigest()
    level = [hashlib.sha256(b).digest() for b in blocks]
    while len(level) > 1:
        if len(level) % 2:
            level.append(level[-1])
        level = [hashlib.sha256(level[i] + level[i + 1]).digest() for i in range(0, len(level), 2)]
    return level[0].hex()
--- check case | Empty input
merkle_root([])
=> "e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855"
--- check case | A single block is its own digest
merkle_root([b"only"])
=> "f905b19542ed08c9a9c26543cca32e5711d207dcffb81b4cdb44ce0b989431c9"
--- check case | Three blocks duplicate the last
merkle_root([b"a", b"b", b"c"])
=> "d31a37ef6ac14a2db1470c4316beb5592e6afd4465022339adafda76a18ffabe"
--- check test | Changing any block changes the root
(lambda base: all(merkle_root([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"]) != merkle_root([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"][:i] + [b"X"] + [b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"][i + 1:]) for i in range(4)))(None)
--- check test | Two equal single-leaf pairs combine predictably
merkle_root([b"x", b"x"]) == __import__("hashlib").sha256(__import__("hashlib").sha256(b"x").digest() * 2).hexdigest()

+++ problem | Validate a command and vote across sensors
--- task
Flight-software input validation plus defence-in-depth voting.

- `validate(command, spec)`: `spec` maps each required field to an inclusive `(low, high)` integer range. Return the command dict unchanged if it has exactly the spec's fields, every value is an `int` (not a `bool`) within range; else `None`. Reject, never clamp.
- `vote(readings, tolerance)`: `readings` is a list of three integers. If at least two are within `tolerance` of each other, return the integer average (`//`) of the closest agreeing pair (checking pairs in the order (0,1), (0,2), (1,2), smallest difference wins, first on a tie); else `None`.

For example, `validate({"valve": 3, "duration": 120}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)})` returns the dict, a valve of 9 gives `None`, and `vote([100, 101, 130], 5)` returns `100`.
--- starter
def validate(command, spec):
    raise NotImplementedError


def vote(readings, tolerance):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def validate(command, spec):
    if set(command) != set(spec):
        return None
    for field, (low, high) in spec.items():
        value = command[field]
        if not isinstance(value, int) or isinstance(value, bool):
            return None
        if value < low or value > high:
            return None
    return command


def vote(readings, tolerance):
    best = None
    for i, j in [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2)]:
        diff = abs(readings[i] - readings[j])
        if diff <= tolerance and (best is None or diff < best[0]):
            best = (diff, (readings[i] + readings[j]) // 2)
    return None if best is None else best[1]
--- check case | A valid command passes, a bad range or bool is rejected
(validate({"valve": 3, "duration": 120}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}), validate({"valve": 9, "duration": 0}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}), validate({"valve": True, "duration": 0}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}))
=> ({"valve": 3, "duration": 120}, None, None)
--- check case | Missing or extra fields are rejected
(validate({"valve": 3}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}), validate({"valve": 3, "duration": 0, "x": 1}, {"valve": (0, 7), "duration": (0, 600)}))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | Two of three sensors agree
(vote([100, 101, 130], 5), vote([100, 130, 160], 5))
=> (100, None)
--- check test | The closest agreeing pair is chosen
vote([100, 108, 109], 5) == 108
--- check test | Boundary values are inclusive and exact agreement works
(validate({"x": 0}, {"x": (0, 7)}), vote([50, 50, 999], 0)) == ({"x": 0}, 50)

+++ question | Why salt every password
--- ask
A leaked table stores each password as an unsalted SHA-256 digest. Why does adding a unique random salt per user help, even though the salts are stored in the clear right next to the hashes?
--- choice
It makes SHA-256 slower, so each guess takes longer.
--- choice correct
It makes precomputed tables useless and forces the attacker to crack each user separately, since equal passwords now hash differently.
--- choice
It encrypts the password so the digest cannot be reversed.
--- choice
It hides which users exist in the table.
--- why
A salt does not slow the hash (that is what the iteration count is for) and does not encrypt anything. Its job is to make every user's hash unique even for equal passwords, which defeats rainbow tables and stops one cracked hash from covering many users. Slowness is a separate defence (key stretching).

+++ question | Nonce reuse in CTR
--- ask
Two messages are encrypted with CTR mode under the **same key and the same nonce**. What can an attacker who captures both ciphertexts learn with no other information?
--- choice
The secret key.
--- choice correct
The exclusive-or of the two plaintexts.
--- choice
Nothing; CTR is secure regardless of the nonce.
--- choice
The length of the key.
--- why
Both ciphertexts used the same keystream K, so C1 xor C2 = (P1 xor K) xor (P2 xor K) = P1 xor P2. The keystream cancels, revealing the xor of the plaintexts, from which known or guessed structure recovers both. The key itself is not exposed, but confidentiality is lost. This is the two-time-pad failure.

+++ question | What the escaping prints
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
def esc(s):
    for a, b in [("&", "&amp;"), ("<", "&lt;")]:
        s = s.replace(a, b)
    return s
print(esc("a<b&c"))
```
--- answer
a&lt;b&amp;c
--- why
The ampersand is replaced first, turning nothing yet since there is one `&` between b and c: `a<b&c` becomes `a<b&amp;c` after the first pass, then `<` becomes `&lt;`, giving `a&lt;b&amp;c`. Escaping `&` first is what stops the `&` introduced by `&lt;` from being double-escaped.

+++ question | Encrypt-then-MAC order
--- ask
Why does authenticated encryption verify the MAC **before** decrypting, rather than decrypting first and checking afterward?
--- choice
Decryption is slower than the MAC, so it saves time on valid messages.
--- choice correct
So attacker-modified ciphertext is thrown away before any decryption logic runs, which removes padding-oracle and related attacks.
--- choice
Because the MAC can only be computed on plaintext.
--- choice
It does not matter; the order has no security effect.
--- why
If you decrypt first, the decryption code (and things like padding checks) run on data the attacker chose, and any observable difference becomes an oracle, as in the padding-oracle attacks on MAC-then-encrypt TLS. Verifying the MAC first means only genuine ciphertexts are ever decrypted. The order is a real security property, not an optimisation.

+++ question | The cost of a collision
--- ask
SHA-256 produces a 256-bit digest. About how many random inputs must an attacker hash before two of them probably collide?
--- choice
About 2^256.
--- choice correct
About 2^128.
--- choice
About 2^255.
--- choice
About 256.
--- why
By the birthday bound, a collision becomes likely after about 2^(n/2) hashes for an n-bit digest, because it is the number of pairs that matters. So a 256-bit hash gives only about 128 bits of collision resistance, which is why collision-sensitive designs need twice the output size of preimage-sensitive ones.

+++ question | Diffie-Hellman without authentication
--- ask
Two parties run an unauthenticated Diffie-Hellman exchange over a network an attacker fully controls. What attack succeeds even though the discrete-logarithm problem is hard?
--- choice
The attacker factors the prime to recover both secrets.
--- choice correct
A man-in-the-middle runs a separate exchange with each side, sharing one key with each and relaying between them.
--- choice
The attacker computes the shared key from the two public values directly.
--- choice
None; Diffie-Hellman is secure on its own.
--- why
Diffie-Hellman establishes a shared key with *someone* but does not authenticate who. An attacker in the middle completes one exchange with each party and sits between them, reading and altering everything, without ever solving a discrete log. The fix is to authenticate the public values (a signature, a certificate), which is what TLS does.

+++ question | Spot the authorisation bug
--- ask
A web handler authenticates the logged-in user, then serves `GET /invoice/<id>` by returning `invoices[id]` for whatever `id` is in the URL. What is the flaw?
--- choice
The id should be sent in a POST body, not the URL.
--- choice correct
It never checks that the invoice belongs to the logged-in user, so anyone can read others' invoices by changing the id.
--- choice
Invoices should be encrypted at rest.
--- choice
The session cookie is missing the Secure flag.
--- why
This is an insecure direct object reference: authentication established who the user is, but the handler skipped authorisation, the check that this user may access this particular object. The other options may be good practice but none is the actual hole. Every access must be scoped to the current user or checked for ownership.

+++ question | Predict the token check
--- ask
`hmac.compare_digest(a, b)` is used instead of `a == b` when checking a MAC or token. What specific attack does this prevent?
--- choice correct
A timing attack that recovers the secret byte by byte from how long the comparison takes.
--- choice
A replay attack.
--- choice
A SQL injection.
--- choice
A buffer overflow.
--- why
A normal `==` on strings stops at the first differing byte, so its running time reveals how many leading bytes matched. An attacker who measures this can find a valid MAC or token one byte at a time. `compare_digest` runs in time independent of where the inputs differ, closing that channel. It does nothing about replay, injection or overflows, which need their own defences.

+++ question | Why flight software bounds everything
--- ask
Flight-software standards forbid dynamic memory allocation after start-up and require every loop to have a fixed upper bound. What is the main safety reason?
--- choice
It makes the code shorter.
--- choice correct
It gives predictable, bounded worst-case memory and time, so the system cannot run out of memory or hang unexpectedly in flight.
--- choice
It allows the use of recursion instead.
--- choice
It makes the program run faster on average.
--- why
The goal is predictability, not speed or brevity. No allocation after start-up means no fragmentation and no out-of-memory failure mid-mission; bounded loops mean no unexpected hang. The system trades best-case performance for a known, provable worst case, which is exactly what a vehicle you cannot reboot from the ground needs.

+++ question | The Ariane 5 lesson
--- ask
Ariane 5 was lost when a 64-bit floating-point velocity was converted to a 16-bit integer and overflowed, and the unhandled exception shut down both the primary and backup computers. Which single change would most directly have prevented the loss?
--- choice correct
Validating the value's range (or handling the conversion fault) before the narrowing conversion, so an out-of-range value did not crash the program.
--- choice
Using a faster processor.
--- choice
Adding a third redundant computer running the same software.
--- choice
Encrypting the telemetry link.
--- why
The root cause was an unchecked numeric range feeding an unhandled fault. Range validation before the conversion, or catching and handling the overflow, keeps the computer alive. More redundancy running the *same* code fails identically (both units did), and speed and encryption are irrelevant to this bug. It is a textbook case of input validation plus fault containment.

+++ question | Predict the injection
--- ask
A server builds a query as `"SELECT * FROM users WHERE name = '" + name + "'"` and a user supplies the name `' OR '1'='1`. What is the resulting WHERE clause, and why is it dangerous?
--- answer
WHERE name = '' OR '1'='1'
name = '' OR '1'='1'
--- why
The pasted value closes the string and adds `OR '1'='1'`, which is always true, so the WHERE clause matches every row and the query returns the whole table. The fix is a parameterised query with a `?` placeholder, which sends the value out of band so it can never change the query's structure.

+++ question | ECB versus CTR
--- ask
Why does encrypting a bitmap image with a strong block cipher in ECB mode still leak the picture's outline, while CTR mode does not?
--- choice correct
ECB encrypts equal plaintext blocks to equal ciphertext blocks, so repeated regions stay visible; CTR xors each block with a unique keystream position.
--- choice
ECB uses a weaker key than CTR.
--- choice
CTR compresses the image first.
--- choice
ECB leaves the image unencrypted.
--- why
ECB is deterministic and stateless per block, so identical blocks (large areas of one colour) produce identical ciphertext, preserving structure. CTR makes each block's keystream depend on its position (and a nonce), so identical plaintext blocks encrypt differently and the output looks random. It is the classic demonstration that a strong cipher in a weak mode is a weak system.
